From this single law, the entire foundational scaffolding emerges by elementary calculus and linear algebra. The integrated form of the principle, with origin convention *x*₄(0) = 0, is the constraint

> *C_M* = *t* + i *x*₄/*c* ⇒ *C_M* = 0 on the McGucken hypersurface *the McGucken Sphere Σ_M*

and the chain-rule derivative tangent to this hypersurface is the *McGucken operator*

> D_M = ∂ₜ + i*c* ∂₄

> **Theorem II.4.1 (Space–Operator Co-Generation).** The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* simultaneously generates the McGucken Space ℳ_G, with constraint surface {*x* ∈ *E*₄ : *C_M*(*x*) = 0}, and the McGucken Operator D_M (Theorem 0.1 of [86]; Theorem 0.S of [87]; Theorem 11 above). Specifically: **(i)** The space the McGucken Space ℳ_G is the kernel of the constraint scalar *C_M* = *t* + i*x*₄/*c*, which is the integrated form of *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* with origin convention *x*₄(0) = 0. **(ii)** Within the two-dimensional vector space of first-order linear differential operators *L* = *a*∂ₜ + *b*∂₄ with complex coefficients, the subspace tangent to *C_M* = 0 (i.e. those *L* for which *LC_M* = 0) is one-dimensional and is generated by the McGucken operator D_M = ∂ₜ + i*c*∂₄. **(iii)** The normalization *a* = 1 is the unique choice that makes the McGucken Operator D_M reduce to ∂ₜ on functions independent of *x*₄. Together (i)–(iii) imply that the law *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* co-generates the pair (McGucken Space ℳ_G, McGucken Operator D_M) uniquely.

*Proof.* **(i)** Integrating *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* with *x*₄(0) = 0 gives *x*₄ = i*ct*, equivalently *t* + i*x*₄/*c* = 0 (since i*x*₄/*c* = i·i*ct*/*c* = −*t*), which is the defining equation of the constraint surface of the McGucken Space ℳ_G. The integration constant is fixed by the source-origin convention *x*₄(0) = 0 (Convention κ of [130]), a framework-level choice of origin and no additional axiom; the carrier is *E*₄ = **R**³ × ℂ, and existence and uniqueness of the solution *x*₄(*t*) = i*ct* + *C* is the Picard–Lindelöf theorem for a constant right-hand side [130, Theorem 3.4, Step 1]. **(ii)** Tangency requires *LC_M* = 0. Compute: *LC_M* = *a*∂ₜ*t* + *b*∂₄(i*x*₄/*c*) = *a* + i*b*/*c*. Setting this to zero yields *b* = i*ac*, so *L* = *a*(∂ₜ + i*c*∂₄) = *aD_M*. The kernel of *L* ↦ *LC_M* is therefore one-dimensional and is spanned by the McGucken Operator D_M. Equivalently, for every *f*(*t*, *x*₄) differentiable in *t* and holomorphic in *x*₄, the value of *the McGucken Operator D_M f* on the McGucken Space ℳ_G is the total derivative *d*/*dt* of *f*(*t*, i*ct*): the McGucken Operator D_M is differentiation along the expansion *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* (Definition 4.1 and Theorem 6.1 of [87]); the tangency reads the McGucken Operator D_MΦ_M = 0 (Theorem 5.1 of [87]), and the functions annihilated by the McGucken Operator D_M are the differentiable functions of Φ_M = *x*₄ − i*ct* (Theorem 5.2 of [87]). **(iii)** Among the operators *aD_M* with *a* ∈ **C**, exactly one (namely *a* = 1) reduces to ∂ₜ on the slice ∂₄ = 0 (Galilean limit), fixing the normalization. □

### II.5. Partial Answers: A Century of Region-Specific Axiomatizations

The McGucken Principle asserts that the fourth dimension *x*₄ is expanding at the velocity of light: *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*, with *x*₄ = i*ct*, as a spherically symmetric McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action *h* and whose volume defines a nonlocality.

With the McGucken Axiom *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* now in hand from §II.4 as the source-law Hilbert demanded, we may return to the historical record and measure each prior programme against it. Between 1915 and the present day, six major axiomatization programmes made substantial progress on portions of Hilbert’s Sixth Problem. None completed it, and each understood, with varying degrees of explicitness, that what they were producing was a *regional* axiomatization rather than the source-axiomatization Hilbert had demanded—the very source-axiomatization the McGucken Axiom *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* now supplies.

### The Programme in the Voices of Its Own Architects

Before reviewing the partial answers technically, it is useful to hear the participants speak for themselves about what they were and were not attempting. The historical record is unambiguous: at no stage did any of the principal architects of twentieth-century axiomatic physics claim to have completed Hilbert’s Sixth Problem in its full form—each acknowledged a residual, exogenous structure in his own programme.

> The object of this book is to present the new quantum mechanics in a unified representation which, so far as it is possible and useful, is mathematically rigorous. … The reader who is not familiar with the points of view of Hilbert’s axiomatic method will find that they are emphasized throughout the book.  
> — J. von Neumann, *Mathematische Grundlagen der Quantenmechanik* (1932), Preface.

The crucial words are *so far as it is possible and useful*. Von Neumann did not derive Hilbert space from anything more primitive; he *postulated* it as the arena in which quantum mechanics is formulated. As Duncan and Janssen note [126], von Neumann was performing an *axiomatic completion* of an existing theory in Hilbert’s sense, not an axiomatic *derivation* of physics from a deeper source. The Hilbert space was input, not output.

The standard Wightman–Gårding framing (cf. Streater & Wightman, *PCT, Spin and Statistics, and All That* (1964), Introduction [116]) is, in paraphrase: Quantum field theory … has so far defied any attempt to give it a rigorous mathematical formulation valid for interacting fields in four spacetime dimensions. … The axioms presented here are an attempt to specify what such a theory should look like; they are not, of course, a derivation of the theory from anything more fundamental.

Wightman’s six axioms (W1–W6, listed below in this section) take as input a separable complex Hilbert space, a unitary representation of the Poincaré group on it, and Minkowski space itself as the indexing manifold. None of these is derived; the spacetime arena and the Hilbert space arena are exogenous. Wightman states the axioms; he does not produce them from a more primitive substrate.

> The supreme task of the physicist is to arrive at those universal elementary laws from which the cosmos can be built up by pure deduction. There is no logical path to these laws; only intuition, resting on sympathetic understanding of experience, can reach them.  
> — A. Einstein, “Principles of Research,” address before the Physical Society, Berlin, 1918.
>
> As far as the laws of mathematics refer to reality, they are not certain; and as far as they are certain, they do not refer to reality.  
> — A. Einstein, *Geometrie und Erfahrung* (“Geometry and Experience”), Prussian Academy of Sciences, Berlin, 27 January 1921.

Einstein’s two remarks frame the obstacle Hilbert’s sixth set out to overcome. The first asserts that the universal laws are not logically derivable but must be intuited; the second, more sharply, that mathematical certainty and physical content stand in inverse proportion. Both presuppose that the source-laws of physics are *postulated* rather than *derived*. The McGucken Principle challenges precisely this presupposition: it exhibits *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* as the universal elementary law and derives the cosmos—Lorentzian signature, Hilbert space, Born rule, second law—from it by pure deduction.

Haag and Kastler’s framing (cf. “An Algebraic Approach to Quantum Field Theory,” *J. Math. Phys.* 5, 848 (1964) [40]; Haag, *Local Quantum Physics* (Springer 1992), §I.1) is, in paraphrase: The basic problem of relativistic quantum theory is the construction of a Lorentz-invariant theory whose elementary observables are localized at points or in arbitrarily small regions of spacetime. … We assume that we are given the topological space of points of spacetime, the Lorentz group acting on it, and the basic notion of a C*-algebra of observables; these are the framework within which the physical theory is formulated.

That these are assumed as given is decisive. Haag and Kastler took spacetime, its symmetry group, and the C*-algebra structure as inputs. Their programme purifies and clarifies the observable algebra; it does not derive the arena. Haag, in his celebrated theorem, demonstrated the failure of the interaction picture in interacting QFT, but the programme remained an axiomatization within a fixed mechanical and geometric arena.

> The whole point of constructive quantum field theory is to construct examples of nontrivial, fully relativistic, fully quantum theories satisfying the Wightman or Haag–Kastler axioms. We do not attempt to derive the axioms; we attempt to satisfy them.  
> — A. Jaffe, summarizing the constructive programme, in Glimm & Jaffe, *Quantum Physics: A Functional Integral Point of View* (Springer 1981), preface and chapter 1.

Glimm and Jaffe state with characteristic clarity what every constructive theorist has tacitly assumed: the axioms are a *target*, not a derivation. Constructive QFT showed how to build models satisfying the axioms in two and three spacetime dimensions; the four-dimensional case became a Clay Millennium Problem. At no point did the programme propose to derive the axioms from a deeper source-law.

The pattern is total. Each of the architects of axiomatic physics stipulated an arena and a structure—a Hilbert space, a spacetime, a symmetry group, a C*-algebra, an axiom-system to satisfy—and each addressed the regional question of how a particular physical theory should be axiomatized within that arena. None claimed to derive the arena itself, nor a unifying source-law. Each, in his own voice, leaves the central demand of the Sixth Problem (the single-source axiomatization of physics in the manner of Euclid for geometry) explicitly open. The McGucken Principle is the first candidate that closes that demand.

**Kolmogorov (1933).** Andrey Nikolaevich Kolmogorov’s *Grundbegriffe der Wahrscheinlichkeitsrechnung* supplied a rigorous measure-theoretic foundation for probability. The triple (Ω, *F*, P) of a sample space, a σ-algebra of events, and a probability measure satisfying countable additivity and unit normalization gave probability theory the same axiomatic standing geometry had received in Euclid. The four Kolmogorov axioms—(K1) P(*A*) ≥ 0 for every *A* ∈ *F*; (K2) P(Ω) = 1; (K3) *F* is closed under countable union, intersection, and complement; (K4) P(∪*Aₙ*) = Σ P(*Aₙ*) for pairwise disjoint *Aₙ*—close the first half of Hilbert’s Sixth Problem in pure mathematics. They say nothing about *physical* probability: why a fair coin obeys K1–K4 with P(heads) = ½, why an electron’s amplitude squared yields the observed click rate, are unanswered. Kolmogorov gave probability an arena; he did not explain why this arena describes the world.

**von Neumann (1932).** John von Neumann’s *Mathematische Grundlagen der Quantenmechanik* formalized the new quantum theory of Heisenberg, Schrödinger, and Dirac in the language of self-adjoint operators on a complex separable Hilbert space. The axioms—states as rays in *H*, observables as self-adjoint operators, evolution as a one-parameter unitary group, measurement via the spectral theorem and Born rule—became the standard presentation of quantum mechanics. But von Neumann took the Hilbert space itself as primitive. He did not derive *why* physical states should live in a complex Hilbert space, or why observables should be self-adjoint operators. The arena was given; the dynamics were axiomatized within it.

**Wightman (1956), Gårding–Wightman (1964).** Arthur Wightman, working at Princeton with Lars Gårding from 1952 onward, formulated the first rigorous axiomatic framework for *relativistic* quantum field theory. The 1956 *Physical Review* paper introduced vacuum expectation values as the basic object; the full system was published with Gårding in 1964 in *Arkiv för Fysik*. The Wightman axioms—sometimes called the Gårding–Wightman axioms—demand: **(W1)** a separable complex Hilbert space *H* carrying a continuous unitary representation *U*(*a*, Λ) of the connected Poincaré group; **(W2)** a unique Poincaré-invariant vacuum state Ω ∈ *H*, *U*Ω = Ω; **(W3)** a set of operator-valued tempered distributions φₐ(*f*) on a common dense domain 𝒟 ⊂ *H*, with cyclicity of Ω under the polynomial algebra of smeared fields; **(W4)** Poincaré-covariance of the fields, *U*φₐ(*f*)*U*^−1 = *S*_ab(Λ^−1)φ_b(*f*_{a,Λ}); **(W5)** microcausality: [φₐ(*x*), φ_b(*y*)]_± = 0 for spacelike separation; (W6) the spectrum condition: the joint spectrum of the four-momentum operators *P*^μ lies in the closed forward light cone *V*₊. The Wightman reconstruction theorem shows that the vacuum expectation values ⟨Ω|φ_a1(*x*₁)···φ_an(*x*ₙ)|Ω⟩ satisfying these axioms determine the field theory completely. Yet Minkowski space, the Poincaré group, and the Hilbert space remain inputs. Wightman axiomatized *what a relativistic quantum field theory must look like*; he did not derive the Lorentzian arena from anything more primitive. The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading: each of the six axioms is stated as a theorem of *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* in [129] (Theorems 16.15.1–16.15.6). In the numbering of [129] these are W1, a separable complex Hilbert space; W2, a strongly continuous unitary representation of the Poincaré group; W3, commutation or anticommutation of fields at spacelike separation; W4, fields as operator-valued distributions on a dense domain; W5, a unique Poincaré-invariant state, modulo the standard irreducibility-of-representation hypothesis; W6, the joint energy-momentum spectrum in the closed forward light cone, from the +i*c* orientation. By the count we adopt in §II.7, the Wightman programme has at least five independent axiomatic primitives, plus the Minkowski-space arena that hosts them.

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* Reading of the Wightman Axioms: the Free Field of Mass *m* Constructed from the McGucken Wavefunctions, with Proofs of the Six Properties.** **McGucken Principle anchor.** The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* states that the fourth dimension is expanding at the velocity of light in a spherically symmetric manner from every spacetime event, as a McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action *h*. The wavefunctions of a body of rest mass *m* are the superpositions of the waves exp(i(**k**·**x** − *ωt*)) with *ω* = *c*(|**k**|² + *μ*²)^(1/2), *μ* = *mc*/ℏ, of Theorem II.8.3.3(ii); they carry the positive frequency of the rest amplitude exp(−i*ω*_C*t*) of Theorem II.8.3.1, which is the orientation +i*c*. The Principle predicts that the field built on these wavefunctions has the six properties (W1)–(W6) listed above. Theorem II.5.4 proves this for the free scalar field of mass *m* > 0. The six theorems 16.15.1–16.15.6 of [129] state the properties for the fields of the Principle in general; the proofs below are complete for the free scalar field and use no input beyond the theorems of the present paper and the integration theory of L² spaces.

**Definition II.5.1 (Mass shell, one-particle space, Fock space, field).** (a) Write *x* = (*x*⁰, **x**) with *x*⁰ = *ct*, and for a wave vector **k** write *k* = (*k*⁰, **k**) with *k*⁰ = (|**k**|² + *μ*²)^(1/2) = *ω*/*c*; these *k* form the mass shell. Write *k*·*x* = **k**·**x** − *k*⁰*x*⁰ = **k**·**x** − *ωt*, and *dμ*(*k*) = *d*³*k*/(2*k*⁰). (b) The one-particle space ℋ₁ is the space of functions *φ*(**k**) with ∫ |*φ*|² *dμ* finite, with ⟨*φ*, *χ*⟩ = ∫ *φ*∗*χ* *dμ*. To *φ* corresponds the wavefunction ∫ *φ*(*k*) exp(i*k*·*x*) *dμ*(*k*), a solution of the Klein–Gordon equation by Theorem II.8.3.3(ii). (c) For a translation *a* and a Lorentz transformation Λ of §II.8.1 that preserves the direction of time and the orientation, (*V*₁(*a*, Λ)*φ*)(*k*) = exp(−i*k*·*a*) *φ*(Λ⁻¹*k*). This is the action on ℋ₁ of the replacement of a wavefunction *ψ*(*x*) by *ψ*(Λ⁻¹(*x* − *a*)). (d) ℋ_n is the space of functions Ψ_n(*k*₁, …, *k*_n), symmetric under permutations of the *n* arguments, with ∫ |Ψ_n|² *dμ*(*k*₁)⋯*dμ*(*k*_n) finite; ℋ₀ is the complex numbers. The Fock space ℱ is the space of sequences Ψ = (Ψ₀, Ψ₁, Ψ₂, …) with Ψ_n in ℋ_n and Σ_n ‖Ψ_n‖² finite. The vacuum is Ω = (1, 0, 0, …). *V*(*a*, Λ) acts on ℋ_n by *V*₁(*a*, Λ) in each argument. 𝒟 is the set of Ψ with finitely many non-zero components, each a Schwartz function. (e) For a Schwartz function *g*(**k**), on 𝒟,

(*A*(*g*)Ψ)_n(*k*₁, …, *k*_n) = (*n* + 1)^(1/2) ∫ *g*∗(*k*) Ψ_(n+1)(*k*, *k*₁, …, *k*_n) *dμ*(*k*),

(*A*†(*g*)Ψ)_n(*k*₁, …, *k*_n) = *n*^(−1/2) Σ_j *g*(*k*_j) Ψ_(n−1)(*K*_j),

where *K*_j stands for the arguments *k*₁, …, *k*_n with *k*_j left out.

(f) For a Schwartz function *f* on spacetime put (*Ef*)(**k**) = ∫ *f*(*x*) exp(−i*k*·*x*) *d*⁴*x* with *k* on the mass shell. The field is Φ(*f*) = *A*(*E*(*f*∗)) + *A*†(*Ef*) on 𝒟.

**Lemma II.5.2 (The mass shell and its measure are Lorentz invariant).** Every Lorentz transformation Λ of Definition II.5.1(c) maps the mass shell onto itself, and ∫ *F*(Λ*k*) *dμ*(*k*) = ∫ *F*(*k*) *dμ*(*k*) for every integrable *F*.

*Proof.* Λ preserves *η*_μν*k*^μ*k*^ν = −*μ*². It preserves *k*⁰ > 0: the first row of Λ satisfies (Λ⁰₀)² − Σ_j(Λ⁰_j)² = 1 with Λ⁰₀ ≥ 1, so by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality (Λ*k*)⁰ ≥ Λ⁰₀*k*⁰ − (Σ_j(Λ⁰_j)²)^(1/2)|**k**| > 0, because |**k**| < *k*⁰. For a rotation of **k** the measure is invariant, since *k*⁰ depends on |**k**| alone. For the boost along the first axis of Theorem II.8.1.7, with *β* = *v*/*c* and the factor *γ*, *k*′¹ = *γ*(*k*¹ − *βk*⁰) and *k*′⁰ = *γ*(*k*⁰ − *βk*¹), the other components being unchanged; since ∂*k*⁰/∂*k*¹ = *k*¹/*k*⁰, ∂*k*′¹/∂*k*¹ = *γ*(1 − *βk*¹/*k*⁰) = *k*′⁰/*k*⁰, so *d*³*k*′/*k*′⁰ = *d*³*k*/*k*⁰. Every Λ is a product of a rotation, a boost along the first axis and a rotation: Λ carries the unit vector of the time axis to a vector *u* with *u*⁰ ≥ 1; a rotation ρ₁ turns the spatial part of *u* into the first axis, a boost *B* along the first axis then carries the unit vector of the time axis to ρ₁*u*, and *B*⁻¹ρ₁Λ fixes the unit vector of the time axis, so it is a rotation ρ₂. □

**Lemma II.5.3 (The Klein–Gordon equation propagates at most at the rate *c*).** Let *v*(*t*, **x**) be a twice continuously differentiable complex solution of (□ − *μ*²)*v* = 0, and let *v*(*s*, **x**) = 0 and ∂ₜ*v*(*s*, **x**) = 0 for all **x** with |**x** − **x**₀| ≤ *R*. Then *v*(*t*, **x**) = 0 whenever |**x** − **x**₀| ≤ *R* − *c*|*t* − *s*|.

*Proof.* Let *e* = *c*⁻²|∂ₜ*v*|² + |∇*v*|² + *μ*²|*v*|², and for *s* ≤ *t* ≤ *s* + *R*/*c* let *W*(*t*) be the integral of *e* over the ball *B*(*t*) of radius *R* − *c*(*t* − *s*) about **x**₀. Using the equation, ∂ₜ*e* = 2 Re[∂ₜ*v*∗(*c*⁻²∂ₜ²*v* − ∇²*v* + *μ*²*v*)] + 2 Re ∇·(∂ₜ*v*∗∇*v*) = 2 Re ∇·(∂ₜ*v*∗∇*v*). The derivative of *W* is the integral of ∂ₜ*e* over *B*(*t*) minus *c* times the integral of *e* over the surface of *B*(*t*), because the radius decreases at the rate *c*. By the divergence theorem the first part is the surface integral of 2 Re(∂ₜ*v*∗ **n**·∇*v*) ≤ 2|∂ₜ*v*||∇*v*|. So *dW*/*dt* is at most the surface integral of 2|∂ₜ*v*||∇*v*| − *c*(*c*⁻²|∂ₜ*v*|² + |∇*v*|²) = −*c*(*c*⁻¹|∂ₜ*v*| − |∇*v*|)² ≤ 0. Since *W*(*s*) = 0 and *W* ≥ 0, *W*(*t*) = 0, so ∂ₜ*v* = 0 at every point (*t*, **x**) with |**x** − **x**₀| ≤ *R* − *c*(*t* − *s*); with *v*(*s*, **x**) = 0 this gives *v* = 0 there. For *t* < *s* apply the result to *v*(2*s* − *t*, **x**), which satisfies the same equation. □

**Theorem II.5.4 (The free scalar field of mass *m* > 0 has the six Wightman properties; Theorems 16.15.1–16.15.6 of [129] for this field).** With the objects of Definition II.5.1: (W1) Hilbert space and representation: ℱ is a separable complex Hilbert space and *V*(*a*, Λ) is a strongly continuous unitary representation on it of the group of the transformations *x* → Λ*x* + *a*. (W2) Vacuum: *V*(*a*, Λ)Ω = Ω, and every vector fixed by all translations is a multiple of Ω. (W3) Fields: each Φ(*f*) maps the dense set 𝒟 into itself; ⟨Ψ, Φ(*f*)Ψ′⟩ = ⟨Φ(*f*∗)Ψ, Ψ′⟩ and |⟨Ψ, Φ(*f*)Ψ′⟩| ≤ *C* ∫ |(1 − ∇²)*f*| *d*⁴*x* for Ψ, Ψ′ in 𝒟, with *C* depending on Ψ, Ψ′; Φ((□ − *μ*²)*f*) = 0; and the vectors Φ(*f*₁)⋯Φ(*f*_n)Ω span a dense subspace of ℱ. (W4) Covariance: *V*(*a*, Λ)Φ(*f*)*V*(*a*, Λ)⁻¹ = Φ(*f*′) with *f*′(*x*) = *f*(Λ⁻¹(*x* − *a*)). (W5) Commutation at spacelike separation: if no point of the support of *f* lies within or on the light cone of any point of the support of *g*, then Φ(*f*)Φ(*g*) = Φ(*g*)Φ(*f*) on 𝒟. (W6) Spectrum: *V*(*a*, 1) multiplies Ψ_n by exp(−i(*k*₁ + ⋯ + *k*_n)·*a*): the energy-momentum operators *P*^μ multiply Ψ_n by ℏ(*k*₁^μ + ⋯ + *k*_n^μ), and their joint spectrum lies in the closed forward cone *p*⁰ ≥ |**p**|.

*Proof.* (W1) Each ℋ_n is a closed subspace of an L² space over a Euclidean space with a measure given by a positive continuous density, which is separable; a countable sum of separable Hilbert spaces is separable. *V*₁(*a*, Λ) preserves the norm of ℋ₁ by Lemma II.5.2 and |exp(−i*k*·*a*)| = 1, and it is invertible, so *V* is unitary on each ℋ_n. The law of composition holds: (*V*₁(*a*, Λ)*V*₁(*b*, Λ′)*φ*)(*k*) = exp(−i*k*·*a*) exp(−i(Λ⁻¹*k*)·*b*) *φ*(Λ′⁻¹Λ⁻¹*k*) = exp(−i*k*·(*a* + Λ*b*)) *φ*((ΛΛ′)⁻¹*k*), because (Λ⁻¹*k*)·*b* = *k*·Λ*b*. For Ψ with finitely many components, each continuous and vanishing outside a bounded set, *V*(*a*, Λ)Ψ converges to Ψ at every point as (*a*, Λ) tends to the identity, with a common bound and a common bounded support, so it converges in norm by dominated convergence. Such Ψ are dense and *V* is unitary, so the convergence holds for every Ψ; with the law of composition this is strong continuity.

(W2) Ω has the single component 1, which no transformation changes. Let Ψ be fixed by every translation in time, *a* = (*a*⁰, 0). Then (exp(i(*k*₁⁰ + ⋯ + *k*_n⁰)*a*⁰) − 1)Ψ_n = 0 almost everywhere, for each *a*⁰ in a countable dense set of real numbers. For *n* ≥ 1 the sum *k*₁⁰ + ⋯ + *k*_n⁰ is at least *nμ* > 0, and a real number *σ* with exp(i*σa*⁰) = 1 for a dense set of *a*⁰ is zero. So Ψ_n = 0 almost everywhere for *n* ≥ 1.

(W3) If *g* and Ψ_n are Schwartz functions, so are the functions of Definition II.5.1(e), and by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and the triangle inequality ‖(*A*(*g*)Ψ)_n‖ ≤ (*n* + 1)^(1/2)‖*g*‖‖Ψ_(n+1)‖ and ‖(*A*†(*g*)Ψ)_n‖ ≤ *n*^(1/2)‖*g*‖‖Ψ_(n−1)‖. By the symmetry of Ψ_n, ⟨Ψ, *A*(*g*)Ψ′⟩ = ⟨*A*†(*g*)Ψ, Ψ′⟩: each of the *n* + 1 terms of *A*†(*g*) contributes the same integral. The functions *Ef* are Schwartz functions of **k**, being the Fourier transform of *f* evaluated at (*k*⁰(**k**), **k**). Hence Φ(*f*) maps 𝒟 into 𝒟, and since Φ(*f*∗) = *A*(*Ef*) + *A*†(*E*(*f*∗)), ⟨Ψ, Φ(*f*)Ψ′⟩ = ⟨Φ(*f*∗)Ψ, Ψ′⟩. Integrating by parts, the function (1 + |**k**|²) *Ef* is *E* applied to (1 − ∇²)*f*, which is at most ∫ |(1 − ∇²)*f*| *d*⁴*x* in modulus; so ‖*Ef*‖² is at most the square of this integral times ∫ (1 + |**k**|²)⁻² *dμ*, which is finite, and the same holds for *E*(*f*∗). With the two norm bounds this gives the stated estimate. Integrating by parts twice, *E*((□ − *μ*²)*f*) = ((*k*⁰)² − |**k**|² − *μ*²)*Ef* = 0. For the density: let *g* be a smooth function of **k** vanishing outside a bounded set, let *χ* be a smooth function of one variable with *χ*(0) = 1 vanishing outside the interval from −*μ*/2 to *μ*/2, and let *f* be the function whose Fourier transform is *g*(**k**)*χ*(*k*⁰ − (|**k**|² + *μ*²)^(1/2)) as a function of four independent variables (*k*⁰, **k**). Then *Ef* = *g*, and *E*(*f*∗) = 0 because the Fourier transform of *f* vanishes for *k*⁰ ≤ 0. So Φ(*f*) = *A*†(*g*), and Φ(*f*₁)⋯Φ(*f*_n)Ω = *A*†(*g*₁)⋯*A*†(*g*_n)Ω, whose only component is the square root of *n*! times the symmetrized product of *g*₁(*k*₁), …, *g*_n(*k*_n). The products of such functions span a dense subspace of the L² space of *n* arguments, and symmetrization is the orthogonal projection of that space onto ℋ_n, so the symmetrized products span a dense subspace of ℋ_n for each *n*.

(W4) By Lemma II.5.2 and the definitions, *V*(*a*, Λ)*A*(*g*)*V*(*a*, Λ)⁻¹ = *A*(*V*₁(*a*, Λ)*g*), and the same for *A*†. With *y* = Λ⁻¹(*x* − *a*), (*Ef*′)(*k*) = ∫ *f*(*y*) exp(−i*k*·(Λ*y* + *a*)) *d*⁴*y* = exp(−i*k*·*a*)(*Ef*)(Λ⁻¹*k*) = (*V*₁(*a*, Λ)*Ef*)(*k*), the determinant of Λ being 1; the same holds for *f*∗.

(W5) First, *A*(*g*)*A*†(*h*) − *A*†(*h*)*A*(*g*) = ⟨*g*, *h*⟩ on 𝒟, while two operators *A* commute and two operators *A*† commute. Indeed, with *K*_j as in Definition II.5.1(e), (*A*†(*h*)Ψ)_(n+1)(*k*, *k*₁, …, *k*_n) = (*n* + 1)^(−1/2)[*h*(*k*)Ψ_n(*k*₁, …, *k*_n) + Σ_j *h*(*k*_j)Ψ_n(*k*, *K*_j)]; applying *A*(*g*) gives ⟨*g*, *h*⟩Ψ_n plus Σ_j *h*(*k*_j) ∫ *g*∗(*k*)Ψ_n(*k*, *K*_j) *dμ*(*k*), and the last sum is (*A*†(*h*)*A*(*g*)Ψ)_n. Therefore Φ(*f*)Φ(*g*) − Φ(*g*)Φ(*f*) is multiplication by the number *K*(*f*, *g*) = ⟨*E*(*f*∗), *Eg*⟩ − ⟨*E*(*g*∗), *Ef*⟩ = ∫ *f*(*x*) *u*(*x*) *d*⁴*x*, where *u*(*x*) = ∫ [exp(i*k*·*x*)(*Eg*)(*k*) − exp(−i*k*·*x*)(*E*(*g*∗))∗(*k*)] *dμ*(*k*). The integral for *u* converges absolutely with all its derivatives. Writing *g*_s(**y**) = *g*(*s*, **y**) for the values of *g* at the time *s*, *u*(*t*, **x**) = ∫ *v*_s(*t*, **x**) *ds*, where *v*_s is the same integral with *g* replaced by its part at the time *s*: *v*_s(*t*, **x**) = *c* ∫ [exp(i**k**·**x** − i*ω*(*t* − *s*)) *G*_s(**k**) − exp(−i**k**·**x** + i*ω*(*t* − *s*)) *G*_s(−**k**)] *dμ*(*k*), with *G*_s(**k**) = ∫ *g*_s(**y**) exp(−i**k**·**y**) *d*³*y*. Each *v*_s is a smooth solution of the Klein–Gordon equation. At *t* = *s* the substitution of −**k** for **k** in the second term shows *v*_s(*s*, **x**) = 0, and ∂ₜ*v*_s(*s*, **x**) = −i*c*² ∫ exp(i**k**·**x**) *G*_s(**k**) *d*³*k* = −i(2π)³*c*² *g*(*s*, **x**), since *ω* = *ck*⁰ cancels the factor 1/(2*k*⁰) of the two equal terms. Let *x* = (*t*, **x**₀) be a point such that no point of the support of *g* lies within or on its light cone. Then for each *s*, *g*(*s*, **y**) = 0 for all **y** with |**y** − **x**₀| ≤ *c*|*t* − *s*|, and Lemma II.5.3 with *R* = *c*|*t* − *s*| gives *v*_s(*t*, **x**₀) = 0. Hence *u*(*x*) = 0 at every such point, and *K*(*f*, *g*) = 0 under the hypothesis of (W5).

(W6) The first statement is Definition II.5.1(c), (d) with Λ = 1, and it defines the *P*^μ as the operators of multiplication by ℏ(*k*₁^μ + ⋯ + *k*_n^μ) on ℋ_n. Each *k*_j satisfies *k*_j⁰ > |**k**_j|, and by the triangle inequality the sum of vectors with *p*⁰ ≥ |**p**| has the same property; for *n* = 0 the value is 0. The joint spectrum of commuting multiplication operators is contained in the closure of the set of values of the multiplying functions, which lies in the closed cone. □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of Theorem II.5.4.** The Hilbert space of (W1) is built from the McGucken wavefunctions of Theorem II.8.3.3 with the norm conserved by the expansion of *x*₄ (Theorem II.8.4.1). The representation of (W1) and (W4) is the action of the transformations of §II.8.1 that leave *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* unchanged. The vacuum of (W2) is the state with no wavefunction present. The commutation of (W5) is Lemma II.5.3: an effect leaving an event stays within the McGucken Spheres of that event and of the events it reaches, which advance at the rate *c*. The forward cone of (W6) is the orientation +i*c*: every wavefunction has the positive frequency of exp(−i*ω*_C*t*). Two limits of the theorem are stated plainly. It concerns the free field; for interacting fields in four dimensions the construction of a theory with the six properties is the open problem of the constructive programme quoted in this section. And it concerns spin 0; the field of spin ½ is treated in Theorem II.5.7.

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* Reading of Cluster Decomposition, of the Reeh–Schlieder Property and of the Free Field of Spin ½, with Proofs.** **McGucken Principle anchor.** The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* states that the fourth dimension is expanding at the velocity of light in a spherically symmetric manner from every spacetime event, as a McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action *h*. The paper [140] states the six properties of Theorem II.5.4 as theorems of the Principle, adds cluster decomposition and the Reeh–Schlieder property (its Theorems 15.11.9a.1 and 15.11.9b.1), and names as its open target (G1) the construction of the fields on the Fock space of many particles. Theorem II.5.4 is that construction for the free scalar field. For the same field the Principle predicts that distant regions are uncorrelated in the vacuum (Theorem II.5.5), that the fields of any one region generate all states from the vacuum (Theorem II.5.6), and that the field of the Dirac equation of Theorem II.8.5.3 has the six properties with anticommutation at spacelike separation (Theorem II.5.7). The notation is that of Definition II.5.1.

**Theorem II.5.5 (Cluster decomposition).** Let *a* be a spacelike vector, *η*(*a*, *a*) > 0. (i) For all Ψ, Ψ′ in ℱ, ⟨Ψ, *V*(*λa*, 1)Ψ′⟩ tends to ⟨Ψ, Ω⟩⟨Ω, Ψ′⟩ as *λ* → ∞. (ii) Let *A* and *B* be products of fields Φ(*f*₁)⋯Φ(*f*_m) and Φ(*g*₁)⋯Φ(*g*_n), and let *B*_λ be the product of the fields Φ(*g*_j′) with *g*_j′(*x*) = *g*_j(*x* − *λa*). Then ⟨Ω, *AB*_λΩ⟩ tends to ⟨Ω, *A*Ω⟩⟨Ω, *B*Ω⟩ as *λ* → ∞.

*Proof.* (i) The boost along the direction of **a** with the velocity *v* = *ca*⁰/|**a**|, |*v*| < *c*, is a Lorentz transformation Λ with (Λ*a*)⁰ = 0. By the law of composition of Theorem II.5.4(W1), *V*(0, Λ)*V*(*λa*, 1)*V*(0, Λ)⁻¹ = *V*(*λ*Λ*a*, 1), and *V*(0, Λ) is unitary and fixes Ω; so it suffices to treat *a* = (0, **a**) with **a** ≠ 0. Then *V*(*λa*, 1) multiplies Ψ′_n by exp(−i*λ***a**·(**k**₁ + ⋯ + **k**_n)), and for *n* ≥ 1 the component *n* of the inner product is the integral of the function Ψ_n∗Ψ′_n/(2*k*₁⁰⋯2*k*_n⁰), integrable over ℝ^(3n) by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, against exp(−i*λ***a**·(**k**₁ + ⋯ + **k**_n)). This is the Fourier transform of an integrable function at the point *λ*(**a**, …, **a**), whose length tends to infinity, so it tends to 0 by the Riemann–Lebesgue lemma (Lemma II.5.8(a)). Each component is bounded in modulus by ‖Ψ_n‖‖Ψ′_n‖, whose sum over *n* is finite, so the sum of the components with *n* ≥ 1 tends to 0. The component *n* = 0 is Ψ₀∗Ψ′₀ = ⟨Ψ, Ω⟩⟨Ω, Ψ′⟩. (ii) By (W4) and (W2) of Theorem II.5.4, *B*_λΩ = *V*(*λa*, 1)*BV*(*λa*, 1)⁻¹Ω = *V*(*λa*, 1)*B*Ω, and by (W3), ⟨Ω, *AB*_λΩ⟩ = ⟨*A*′Ω, *V*(*λa*, 1)*B*Ω⟩ with *A*′ the product of the Φ(*f*_j∗) in the reverse order. By (i) this tends to ⟨*A*′Ω, Ω⟩⟨Ω, *B*Ω⟩ = ⟨Ω, *A*Ω⟩⟨Ω, *B*Ω⟩. □

**Theorem II.5.6 (The Reeh–Schlieder property of the free field).** Let *D* be a non-empty open region of spacetime. The vectors Φ(*f*₁)⋯Φ(*f*_n)Ω, with *n* ≥ 0 and every *f*_j vanishing outside *D*, span a dense subspace of ℱ.

*Proof.* Let Ψ be orthogonal to all these vectors; it is to be shown that Ψ = 0. Choose a non-empty open region *D*′ and *δ* > 0 such that *D*′ translated by any *a* whose four components are all smaller than *δ* in modulus lies in *D*. Fix *n* and functions *f*₁, …, *f*_n vanishing outside *D*′, and for vectors *ζ*₁, …, *ζ*_n of ℝ⁴ put *F*(*ζ*₁, …, *ζ*_n) = ⟨Ψ, *V*(*ζ*₁)Φ(*f*₁)*V*(*ζ*₂)Φ(*f*₂)⋯*V*(*ζ*_n)Φ(*f*_n)Ω⟩, where *V*(*ζ*) = *V*(*ζ*, 1). By (W4) and (W2) of Theorem II.5.4 the vector on the right is Φ(*g*₁)⋯Φ(*g*_n)Ω, where *g*_j(*x*) = *f*_j(*x* − *ζ*₁ − ⋯ − *ζ*_j). So *F* = 0 when all components of all *ζ*_j are smaller than *δ*/*n* in modulus. Step 1. Let *b* be a vector with *b*⁰ > |**b**|, let Ψ₂ be in 𝒟, and for a complex number *z* with Im *z* ≥ 0 let *W*(*z*)Ψ₂ be the vector obtained from Ψ₂ by multiplying its component of *m* particles by exp(i*zν*), where *ν* = *b*⁰(*k*₁⁰ + ⋯ + *k*_m⁰) − **b**·(**k**₁ + ⋯ + **k**_m). Since *k*_j⁰ > |**k**_j|, *ν* ≥ 0, so |exp(i*zν*)| ≤ 1; *W*(*z*)Ψ₂ lies in 𝒟, and for real *z* it is *V*(*zb*)Ψ₂. For a product *A* of operators *V*(*ζ*) and Φ(*f*), the function *h*(*z*) = ⟨Ψ, *AW*(*z*)Ψ₂⟩ is a finite sum of integrals of fixed integrable functions against factors exp(i*zν*) with *ν* ≥ 0 and their products with polynomials in the *k*_j; it is continuous for Im *z* ≥ 0 and analytic for Im *z* > 0. If *h* vanishes on an interval of the real axis, then by the reflection principle of Schwarz in the form of Lemma II.5.8(b), *h* = 0 for Im *z* ≥ 0, and in particular for all real *z*. Step 2. Apply Step 1 to *F* as a function of *ζ*_n = *ζ* + *zb*, with *ζ* and the other *ζ*_j small and Ψ₂ = *V*(*ζ*)Φ(*f*_n)Ω: *F* vanishes for *ζ*_n = *ζ* + *zb* with every real *z*. The vectors *b* with *b*⁰ > |**b**| form an open set, and every vector of ℝ⁴ is a difference of two of them; applying Step 1 twice, *F* vanishes for all *ζ*_n in ℝ⁴ and small *ζ*₁, …, *ζ*_(n−1). The same argument for *ζ*_(n−1), with Ψ₂ = *V*(*ζ*)Φ(*f*_(n−1))*V*(*ζ*_n)Φ(*f*_n)Ω and *ζ*_n now arbitrary, and so on down to *ζ*₁, shows that *F* = 0 for all *ζ*₁, …, *ζ*_n in ℝ⁴. Step 3. Hence Ψ is orthogonal to Φ(*g*₁)⋯Φ(*g*_n)Ω whenever each *g*_j vanishes outside some translate of *D*′. Every smooth function vanishing outside a bounded set is a finite sum of such functions, by a partition of unity, and by the estimate of (W3) these are dense, in the sense of that estimate, among the Schwartz functions; so Ψ is orthogonal to all the vectors of (W3) of Theorem II.5.4, which span a dense subspace. Therefore Ψ = 0. □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of Theorems II.5.5 and II.5.6.** Theorem II.5.5 rests on (W2): the vacuum is the only state unchanged by translation. Theorem II.5.6 rests on (W6): the factor exp(i*zν*) is bounded for Im *z* ≥ 0 because every wavefunction has the positive frequency of the orientation +i*c*, and it is this one-sided boundedness that carries a statement about one region to all of spacetime. With (W5), which forbids any effect outside the McGucken Spheres of a region, the two theorems hold together: the fields of a region generate every state from the vacuum, and they commute with the fields of every spacelike separated region.

**Theorem II.5.7 (The free field of spin ½ on the antisymmetric Fock space).** Let *γ*^μ be the matrices of Theorem II.8.5.3, *μ* = *mc*/ℏ > 0, and for *k* on the mass shell let *K*(*k*) = *γ*^μ*k*_μ = −*k*⁰*γ*⁰ + *k*¹*γ*¹ + *k*²*γ*² + *k*³*γ*³, *P*₊(*k*) = (*μ* − *K*(*k*))/(2*μ*) and *P*₋(*k*) = (*μ* + *K*(*k*))/(2*μ*). (i) *K*(*k*)² = *μ*², *γ*⁰*K*(*k*)†*γ*⁰ = *K*(*k*), and *P*₊, *P*₋ are projections with *P*₊ + *P*₋ = 1 and *P*₊*P*₋ = 0. A column *u* exp(i*k*·*x*) satisfies the Dirac equation (iℏ*γ*^μ∂_μ − *mc*)*ψ* = 0 exactly when *P*₊(*k*)*u* = *u*, and a column *u* exp(−i*k*·*x*) exactly when *P*₋(*k*)*u* = *u*. (ii) For *A* in SL(2, ℂ) let *S*(*A*) = *T* *S*₀(*A*) *T*, where *S*₀(*A*) has the diagonal blocks *A* and (*A*†)⁻¹ and *T* is 1/√2 times the matrix with the blocks 1, 1 in the first row and 1, −1 in the second. Then *S*(*AB*) = *S*(*A*)*S*(*B*), *S*(*A*)*K*(*k*)*S*(*A*)⁻¹ = *K*(Λ(*A*)*k*) with Λ(*A*) of Theorem II.6.38, and *S*(*A*)†*γ*⁰*S*(*A*) = *γ*⁰. (iii) *u*†*γ*⁰*u* > 0 for *u* ≠ 0 with *P*₊(*k*)*u* = *u*, and *u*†*γ*⁰*u* < 0 for *u* ≠ 0 with *P*₋(*k*)*u* = *u*. (iv) Let 𝒦₊ be the space of the functions *φ*(*k*) with values in ℂ⁴, *P*₊*φ* = *φ*, and ⟨*φ*, *χ*⟩ = ∫ *φ*†*γ*⁰*χ* *dμ* finite for *χ* = *φ*; let 𝒦₋ be the space of the *φ* with *P*₋*φ* = *φ* and the inner product −∫ *φ*†*γ*⁰*χ* *dμ*, taken with the conjugate multiplication by complex numbers and the conjugate inner product. On 𝒦₊ and 𝒦₋ let (*a*, *A*) act by *φ*(*k*) → exp(−i*k*·*a*)*S*(*A*)*φ*(Λ(*A*)⁻¹*k*) and *φ*(*k*) → exp(i*k*·*a*)*S*(*A*)*φ*(Λ(*A*)⁻¹*k*). These are unitary representations of the group *G* of Theorem II.6.38(v). (v) Let ℱ₋ be the antisymmetric Fock space over 𝒦 = 𝒦₊ ⊕ 𝒦₋, with vacuum Ω and with the operators *B*(*h*), *B*†(*h*) that remove and add a particle of wavefunction *h*; they are bounded and satisfy *B*(*h*)*B*†(*h*′) + *B*†(*h*′)*B*(*h*) = ⟨*h*, *h*′⟩ and *B*(*h*)*B*(*h*′) + *B*(*h*′)*B*(*h*) = 0. For a Schwartz function *f* on spacetime with values in ℂ⁴ let *E*₊*f*(*k*) = *P*₊(*k*) ∫ *f*(*x*) exp(−i*k*·*x*) *d*⁴*x* in 𝒦₊ and *E*₋*f*(*k*) = *P*₋(*k*) ∫ *f*(*x*) exp(i*k*·*x*) *d*⁴*x* in 𝒦₋, and let the field be Ψ(*f*) = *B*(*E*₊*f*) + *B*†(*E*₋*f*). Then: (W1) ℱ₋ is a separable Hilbert space with a strongly continuous unitary representation *V* of *G*, and *V*(0, −1) multiplies the states of *n* particles by 1 for even *n* and by −1 for odd *n*; (W2) vacuum: Ω is the only vector, up to multiples, fixed by all translations; (W3) fields: each Ψ(*f*) is a bounded operator, conjugate-linear in *f*, with ‖Ψ(*f*)‖² ≤ ‖*E*₊*f*‖² + ‖*E*₋*f*‖², and the vectors obtained from Ω by products of the Ψ(*f*) and their adjoints span a dense subspace; (W4) covariance: *V*(*a*, *A*)Ψ(*f*)*V*(*a*, *A*)⁻¹ = Ψ(*g*) for the function *g*(*x*) = *S*(*A*)*f*(Λ(*A*)⁻¹(*x* − *a*)); (W5) anticommutation: Ψ(*f*)Ψ(*g*) + Ψ(*g*)Ψ(*f*) = 0 for all *f*, *g*, and Ψ(*f*)Ψ(*g*)† + Ψ(*g*)†Ψ(*f*) = 0 when no point of the support of *f* lies within or on the light cone of any point of the support of *g*; (W6) spectrum: the joint spectrum of the energy-momentum operators lies in the closed forward cone.

*Proof.* (i) By the relation of Theorem II.8.5.3, *K*² = ½(*γ*^μ*γ*^ν + *γ*^ν*γ*^μ)*k*_μ*k*_ν = −*η*^μν*k*_μ*k*_ν = (*k*⁰)² − |**k**|² = *μ*². By Lemma II.8.7.2(iii), *γ*⁰(*γ*^μ)†*γ*⁰ = *γ*^μ, and the *k*_μ are real. From *K*² = *μ*²: *P*₊² = (*μ*² − 2*μK* + *K*²)/(4*μ*²) = *P*₊, likewise *P*₋² = *P*₋, and *P*₊*P*₋ = (*μ*² − *K*²)/(4*μ*²) = 0. Since ∂_μ exp(i*k*·*x*) = i*k*_μ exp(i*k*·*x*), the Dirac operator divided by ℏ applied to *u* exp(i*k*·*x*) gives (−*K* − *μ*)*u* exp(i*k*·*x*), which vanishes exactly when *Ku* = −*μu*, that is, *P*₊*u* = *u*; for exp(−i*k*·*x*) the sign of *K* is reversed. (ii) *T* is real, symmetric and *T*² = 1. Direct multiplication gives *Tγ*⁰*T* = the matrix with the blocks 0, 1 and 1, 0, and *Tγ*^j*T* = the matrix with the blocks 0, −σ_j and σ_j, 0. Hence *TK*(*k*)*T* has the blocks 0, −*M*(*k*) in the first row and −*N*(*k*), 0 in the second, where *M*(*k*) = *k*⁰ + *k*¹σ₁ + *k*²σ₂ + *k*³σ₃ is the matrix of Theorem II.6.38 and *N*(*k*) = *k*⁰ − *k*¹σ₁ − *k*²σ₂ − *k*³σ₃. Multiplying on the left by *S*₀(*A*) and on the right by its inverse replaces *M* by *AMA*† and *N* by (*A*†)⁻¹*NA*⁻¹. By Theorem II.6.38(i), *AM*(*k*)*A*† = *M*(Λ(*A*)*k*). Since *M*(*k*)*N*(*k*) = (*k*⁰)² − |**k**|² = *μ*², *N* = *μ*²*M*⁻¹, and (*A*†)⁻¹*M*(*k*)⁻¹*A*⁻¹ = (*AM*(*k*)*A*†)⁻¹ = *M*(Λ(*A*)*k*)⁻¹, so (*A*†)⁻¹*N*(*k*)*A*⁻¹ = *N*(Λ(*A*)*k*). This is the second statement. *S*₀(*AB*) = *S*₀(*A*)*S*₀(*B*) because ((*AB*)†)⁻¹ = (*A*†)⁻¹(*B*†)⁻¹. Finally *S*₀(*A*)† has the diagonal blocks *A*†, *A*⁻¹, and its product with the matrix of blocks 0, 1 and 1, 0 and with *S*₀(*A*) has the blocks 0, *A*†(*A*†)⁻¹ and *A*⁻¹*A*, 0. (iii) For *k*₀ = (*μ*, 0, 0, 0), *K*(*k*₀) = −*μγ*⁰, so *P*₊(*k*₀)*u* = *u* means *γ*⁰*u* = *u*, and then *u*†*γ*⁰*u* = *u*†*u* > 0. For any *k* on the mass shell there is, by Theorem II.6.38(iii), (iv), a matrix *A* with Λ(*A*)*k*₀ = *k*. By (ii), *P*₊(*k*)*S*(*A*) = *S*(*A*)*P*₊(*k*₀), so the *u* with *P*₊(*k*)*u* = *u* are the *S*(*A*)*w* with *γ*⁰*w* = *w*, and *u*†*γ*⁰*u* = *w*†*S*(*A*)†*γ*⁰*S*(*A*)*w* = *w*†*w*. The case of *P*₋ is the same with *γ*⁰*w* = −*w*.

(iv) By (ii), *K*(*k*)*S*(*A*)*φ*(Λ⁻¹*k*) = *S*(*A*)*K*(Λ⁻¹*k*)*φ*(Λ⁻¹*k*), so the two spaces are carried into themselves. The inner products are preserved because *S*(*A*)†*γ*⁰*S*(*A*) = *γ*⁰, the exponential has modulus 1, and the measure is invariant (Lemma II.5.2). The law of composition is verified as in Theorem II.5.4(W1), using *S*(*AB*) = *S*(*A*)*S*(*B*); on 𝒦₋ the conjugate multiplication makes exp(i*k*·*a*) act as the complex number exp(−i*k*·*a*). (v) The antisymmetric Fock space over a Hilbert space 𝒦 is the completion of the span of the products *h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n, antisymmetric in the *h*_j, *n* ≥ 0, with the inner product of *h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n and *h*₁′∧⋯∧*h*_n′ equal to the determinant of the numbers ⟨*h*_i, *h*_j′⟩ and products of different length orthogonal; Ω is the empty product. Lemma II.5.8(c) proves that the determinant defines a positive definite inner product and that the two operators which follow are well defined. *B*†(*h*) sends *h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n to *h*∧*h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n, and *B*(*h*) sends it to the sum over *j* of ±⟨*h*, *h*_j⟩ times the product with *h*_j left out, with the sign + for odd *j* and − for even *j*. Expanding the determinant along its first row shows that *B*(*h*) is the adjoint of *B*†(*h*). Applying *B*(*h*)*B*†(*h*′) to a product gives ⟨*h*, *h*′⟩ times the product minus the sum over *j*, with the same signs, of ⟨*h*, *h*_j⟩ times *h*′ followed by the product without *h*_j, and the last sum is *B*†(*h*′)*B*(*h*) applied to the product; this is the first relation, and the second follows from *h*∧*h*′ = −*h*′∧*h*. The first relation with *h* = *h*′ gives ‖*B*(*h*)Θ‖² + ‖*B*†(*h*)Θ‖² = ‖*h*‖²‖Θ‖² for every Θ in the span, so both operators are bounded by ‖*h*‖.

(W1), (W2), (W6). A unitary map of 𝒦 acts on the products factor by factor and preserves the determinants. Separability, strong continuity and the statements (W2), (W6) are proved as in Theorem II.5.4, every wavefunction of 𝒦₊ and of 𝒦₋ being multiplied by exp(−i*k*·*a*) with *k* on the mass shell, *k*⁰ ≥ *μ* > 0. Since *S*₀(−1) = −1, the element (0, −1) multiplies every *h* by −1, and a product of *n* of them by 1 or −1 as *n* is even or odd. (W3) *E*₊*f* and *E*₋*f* lie in 𝒦₊ and 𝒦₋ by (i), their integrands being Schwartz functions. *B*(*h*) is conjugate-linear in *h*; *E*₋*f* is linear in *f* as a function, hence conjugate-linear as an element of 𝒦₋ with its conjugate multiplication, and *B*† is linear. The bound follows from (v), the two summands of 𝒦 being orthogonal. For the density: the adjoint of Ψ(*f*) is *B*†(*E*₊*f*) + *B*(*E*₋*f*). As in the proof of Theorem II.5.4(W3), for a smooth function *φ* of **k** with values in ℂ⁴, vanishing outside a bounded set, there is *f* whose Fourier transform is *φ*(**k**)*χ*(*k*⁰ − (|**k**|² + *μ*²)^(1/2)); then *E*₊*f* = *P*₊*φ* and *E*₋*f* = 0, so Ψ(*f*)† = *B*†(*P*₊*φ*), and with −*k*⁰ in place of *k*⁰ one obtains Ψ(*f*) = *B*†(*P*₋*φ*). The *P*₊*φ* and *P*₋*φ* are dense in 𝒦₊ and 𝒦₋, the operators *B*† are bounded, and the products *h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n span a dense subspace. (W4) Covariance. For the function *g* of the statement, the substitution *y* = Λ⁻¹(*x* − *a*) gives ∫ *g*(*x*) exp(−i*k*·*x*) *d*⁴*x* = exp(−i*k*·*a*)*S*(*A*) ∫ *f*(*y*) exp(−i(Λ⁻¹*k*)·*y*) *d*⁴*y*, and by (ii) the matrix *P*₊(*k*)*S*(*A*) equals *S*(*A*)*P*₊(*l*) with *l* = Λ⁻¹*k*; so *E*₊*g* is the image of *E*₊*f* under the action of (iv) on 𝒦₊. Likewise *E*₋*g* is the image of *E*₋*f*; and *V* *B*(*h*) *V*⁻¹ = *B*(*Vh*).

(W5) Ψ(*f*)Ψ(*g*) + Ψ(*g*)Ψ(*f*) = [*B*(*E*₊*f*)*B*†(*E*₋*g*) + *B*†(*E*₋*g*)*B*(*E*₊*f*)] + [*B*†(*E*₋*f*)*B*(*E*₊*g*) + *B*(*E*₊*g*)*B*†(*E*₋*f*)] = ⟨*E*₊*f*, *E*₋*g*⟩ + ⟨*E*₊*g*, *E*₋*f*⟩ = 0, the two summands of 𝒦 being orthogonal and the other pairs anticommuting by (v). For the second expression, in the same way, Ψ(*f*)Ψ(*g*)† + Ψ(*g*)†Ψ(*f*) = ⟨*E*₊*f*, *E*₊*g*⟩ + ⟨*E*₋*g*, *E*₋*f*⟩, where the last inner product is that of 𝒦₋ with its conjugation, so that the sum is ∫ *F*†*γ*⁰*P*₊*G* *dμ* − ∫ *F*′†*γ*⁰*P*₋*G*′ *dμ*, with *F*, *G* the integrals of *f*, *g* against exp(−i*k*·*x*) and *F*′, *G*′ those against exp(i*k*·*x*); here *P*₊†*γ*⁰*P*₊ = *γ*⁰*P*₊² = *γ*⁰*P*₊ by (i). Since *K* exp(i*k*·(*x* − *y*)) = −i*γ*^μ∂_μ exp(i*k*·(*x* − *y*)) and *K* exp(−i*k*·(*x* − *y*)) = i*γ*^μ∂_μ exp(−i*k*·(*x* − *y*)), the derivatives being with respect to *x*, the sum equals 1/(2*μ*) times the integral over *x* and *y* of *f*(*x*)†*γ*⁰(*μ* + i*γ*^μ∂_μ) applied to ∫ [exp(i*k*·(*x* − *y*)) − exp(−i*k*·(*x* − *y*))] *dμ*(*k*), times *g*(*y*). Integrating by parts in *x*, this is 1/(2*μ*) times the sum over the four components *β* of *K*(*F*_β, *g*_β), where *K*( , ) is the number of the proof of Theorem II.5.4(W5) and *F*_β is the component *β* of the row *μf*†*γ*⁰ − i∂_μ(*f*†*γ*⁰*γ*^μ). Each *F*_β vanishes outside the support of *f*, so by Theorem II.5.4(W5) each term is zero under the stated condition. □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of Theorem II.5.7.** The two projections *P*₊ and *P*₋ separate the solutions of the Dirac equation of Theorem II.8.5.3 with the phase exp(i*k*·*x*) from those with exp(−i*k*·*x*); the first are the particles of the orientation +i*c*, and the second enter the field through the operator that adds a particle of the conjugate space, so that every state has positive energy. The sign of *V*(0, −1), −1 for an odd number of particles, is the sign of Theorem II.8.3.7 under a turn by 2π, now for states of *n* particles. The anticommutation at spacelike separation is reduced in (W5) to the commutation function of the scalar field and so to Lemma II.5.3, the bound *c* on propagation. The theorem shows that the field of spin ½ with anticommutation has the six properties. That this field of spin ½ fails to commute at spacelike separation when it is given the Bose rule is proved in Theorem II.5.11(b); the spin–statistics theorem for arbitrary fields remains an external theorem [116]. The paper [140] constructs the Fock space of fermions in its §I.9 and lists the extension to interacting fields of all sectors as open; so does the present paper.

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* Reading of the Connection Between Spin and Statistics for the Free Fields, of Cluster Decomposition and the Reeh–Schlieder Property for Spin ½, and of the Three Lemmas of Analysis Used in §II.5, with Proofs.** **McGucken Principle anchor.** The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* states that the fourth dimension is expanding at the velocity of light in a spherically symmetric manner from every spacetime event, as a McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action *h*. Theorems II.5.5 and II.5.6 use the Riemann–Lebesgue lemma and the reflection principle of Schwarz, and Theorem II.5.7 uses the determinant inner product; Lemma II.5.8 proves the three. For the field of spin ½ of Theorem II.5.7 the Principle predicts what it predicts for the scalar field: distant regions are uncorrelated in the vacuum, and the fields of any one region generate all states from the vacuum (Corollary II.5.9). The orientation +i*c* gives every wavefunction positive frequency, and Lemma II.5.3 bounds propagation by *c*. From these two facts the Principle predicts which rule of exchange each free field obeys: Theorem II.5.11 proves that the scalar field with the exclusion rule, and the field of spin ½ with the Bose rule, each fail both to commute and to anticommute at spacelike separation.

**Lemma II.5.8 (The three lemmas of analysis used in Theorems II.5.5, II.5.6 and II.5.7).** (a) Riemann–Lebesgue: let *F* be an integrable function on ℝ^N and let *G*(*ξ*) = ∫ *F*(*y*) exp(−i*ξ*·*y*) *d*^N*y*. Then *G*(*ξ*) tends to 0 as |*ξ*| → ∞. (b) Reflection: let *h* be a function continuous for Im *z* ≥ 0 and analytic for Im *z* > 0, with *h*(*x*) = 0 for every real *x* of an open interval. Then *h* = 0. (c) Determinant inner product: let 𝒦 be a complex Hilbert space, with ⟨ , ⟩ conjugate-linear in the first argument. For *h*₁, …, *h*_n in 𝒦 let *h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n be the function of *n* vectors *g*₁, …, *g*_n of 𝒦 whose value is the determinant of the numbers ⟨*g*_i, *h*_j⟩ divided by the square root of *n*!, and let 𝒜_n be the vector space of functions spanned by these products. Then: (c1) there is exactly one inner product on 𝒜_n for which the inner product of *h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n and *l*₁∧⋯∧*l*_n is the determinant of the numbers ⟨*h*_i, *l*_j⟩, and it is positive definite; (c2) the squared norm of *h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n is at most *n*! ‖*h*₁‖²⋯‖*h*_n‖²; (c3) for *h* in 𝒦 and for a bounded operator *T* on 𝒦, the rules *h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n → *h*∧*h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n, *h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n → the sum over *j* of ±⟨*h*, *h*_j⟩ times the product with *h*_j left out (the sign + for odd *j*, − for even *j*), and *h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n → *Th*₁∧⋯∧*Th*_n define linear maps *B*†(*h*) from 𝒜_n to 𝒜_(n+1), *B*(*h*) from 𝒜_n to 𝒜_(n−1), and a linear map of 𝒜_n to itself. The antisymmetric Fock space over 𝒦 is the space of sequences (Θ₀, Θ₁, Θ₂, …), with Θ₀ a complex number, Θ_n in the completion of 𝒜_n, and the sum of the ‖Θ_n‖² finite.

*Proof.* (a) Let *F* be the function equal to 1 on a box, the product of intervals from *α*_j to *β*_j, and 0 elsewhere. Then *G*(*ξ*) is the product over *j* of the integrals of exp(−i*ξ*_j*y*) from *α*_j to *β*_j. Each of these has modulus at most *β*_j − *α*_j, and for *ξ*_j ≠ 0 it equals (exp(−i*ξ*_j*α*_j) − exp(−i*ξ*_j*β*_j))/(i*ξ*_j), of modulus at most 2/|*ξ*_j|. Some component has |*ξ*_j| ≥ |*ξ*|/*N*^(1/2), so |*G*(*ξ*)| ≤ *C*/|*ξ*| with *C* depending on the box, and the statement holds for every finite combination of such functions. For a general integrable *F* and *ε* > 0 there is, by the construction of the integral, such a combination *F*₁ with ∫ |*F* − *F*₁| *d*^N*y* ≤ *ε*. Since |exp(−i*ξ*·*y*)| = 1, the functions *G* and *G*₁ differ by at most *ε* at every *ξ*, and *G*₁(*ξ*) → 0; so |*G*(*ξ*)| ≤ 2*ε* for all large |*ξ*|.

(b) Let *x*₀ be a point of the interval and *Q* the open disc about *x*₀ of a radius *r* so small that the real points of the closed disc lie in the interval. On the closed disc put *H*(*z*) = *h*(*z*) for Im *z* ≥ 0 and *H*(*z*) = *h*(*z*∗)∗ for the other *z*. The two expressions agree on the real points, where *h* = 0, so *H* is continuous. *H* is analytic at the points of *Q* above the real axis, and at those below it, where its difference quotient at *z* is the complex conjugate of the difference quotient of *h* at *z*∗. Fix *z* in *Q* with Im *z* > 0, and for small *ε* > 0 let *Q*₁ be the part of the closed disc with Im *w* ≥ *ε* and *Q*₂ the part with Im *w* ≤ −*ε*. On *Q*₁, *H* is the function *h*(*w*), analytic in the open half plane Im *w* > 0, which contains *Q*₁; on *Q*₂, *H* is the function *h*(*w*∗)∗, analytic in the open half plane below the real axis, which contains *Q*₂. By the integral formula of Cauchy, 2πi*H*(*z*) is the integral of *H*(*w*)/(*w* − *z*) around the boundary of *Q*₁; by the theorem of Cauchy, the integral of the same function around the boundary of *Q*₂ is 0. As *ε* → 0 the two integrals along the chords at the heights *ε* and −*ε* tend, by the uniform continuity of *H* on the closed disc, to the integrals along the real diameter in the two opposite directions, whose sum is 0, and the two arcs tend to the whole circle. Hence 2πi*H*(*z*) = ∮ *H*(*w*)/(*w* − *z*) *dw* over the circle of radius *r*. The same holds for Im *z* below 0 by the same argument, and for real *z* in *Q* because both sides are continuous in *z*. The right side may be differentiated with respect to *z* under the integral sign, so *H* is analytic in *Q*. *H* vanishes on the real diameter, so all its derivatives at *x*₀, computed along the real axis, vanish; its power series about *x*₀ is 0, and *H* = 0 in *Q*. Thus *h* vanishes on an open subset of the half plane Im *z* > 0, which is connected; by the identity theorem for analytic functions *h* = 0 there, and by continuity for Im *z* ≥ 0.

(c) Take finitely many products, and let *e*₁, …, *e*_r be an orthonormal basis of the span of all the vectors that occur in them. For a set *I* of *n* of the indices 1, …, *r* and a function *D* of 𝒜_n write *D*(*I*) for the value of *D* at the vectors *e*_i, *i* in *I*, taken in increasing order. Let Π = *h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n and Π′ = *l*₁∧⋯∧*l*_n be two of the products, and put *c*(*m*, *i*) = ⟨*e*_m, *h*_i⟩, *d*(*m*, *j*) = ⟨*e*_m, *l*_j⟩; then ⟨*h*_i, *l*_j⟩ is the sum over *m* of *c*(*m*, *i*)∗*d*(*m*, *j*). Expanding the determinant as the sum over the permutations *σ* of sgn(*σ*) times the product over *i* of ⟨*h*_i, *l*_j⟩ with *j* = *σ*(*i*), and then each factor as the sum over *m*, gives the sum, over all sequences *m*(1), …, *m*(*n*) of indices, of the product over *i* of *c*(*m*(*i*), *i*)∗ times the determinant of the numbers *d*(*m*(*i*), *j*). This determinant is 0 when two of the *m*(*i*) are equal. The sequences without repetition are the rearrangements of the sets *I*: if *m* is the rearrangement of *I* by a permutation *ρ*, the determinant is sgn(*ρ*) times the square root of *n*! times Π′(*I*), and the sum over *ρ* of sgn(*ρ*) times the product of the *c*(*m*(*i*), *i*)∗ is the square root of *n*! times Π(*I*)∗. Therefore the determinant of the numbers ⟨*h*_i, *l*_j⟩ equals *n*! times the sum over *I* of Π(*I*)∗Π′(*I*). It follows that for two finite combinations *D* and *D*′ of products, the corresponding combination of determinants equals *n*! times the sum over *I* of *D*(*I*)∗*D*′(*I*). This number depends on the functions *D* and *D*′ alone, which gives the inner product of (c1) and its uniqueness; for *D*′ = *D* it is a sum of squared moduli. If it is 0, then *D*(*I*) = 0 for every *I*; *D* is conjugate-linear in each argument and changes sign when two arguments are exchanged, so *D* vanishes whenever its arguments lie in the span of the *e*_i; and *D* is unchanged when each argument is replaced by its orthogonal projection on that span, since ⟨*g*, *h*⟩ is unchanged for *h* in the span. So *D* = 0. (c2) The determinant of the numbers ⟨*h*_i, *h*_j⟩ is a sum of *n*! terms, each a product of *n* inner products in which every *h*_j occurs once on the left and once on the right; by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality each term has modulus at most ‖*h*₁‖²⋯‖*h*_n‖². (c3) Expanding the determinant of *h*∧*h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n along the column of *h* shows that its value at *g*₀, …, *g*_n is (*n* + 1)^(−1/2) times the sum over *i* of ±⟨*g*_i, *h*⟩ times the value of *h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n at the *g* with *g*_i left out, the sign being + for even *i* and − for odd *i*. Expanding the determinant of *h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n at the arguments *h*, *g*₁, …, *g*_(n−1) along the row of *h* shows that *n*^(1/2) times this value is the value at *g*₁, …, *g*_(n−1) of the sum stated in (c3). The value of *Th*₁∧⋯∧*Th*_n at *g*₁, …, *g*_n is the value of *h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n at *T*†*g*₁, …, *T*†*g*_n. In each of the three cases the image is expressed through the function *h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n alone, by a linear rule; so the rule extends to a linear map on combinations. □

Lemma II.5.8 uses, without proof, the theorem of Cauchy, the integral formula of Cauchy and the identity theorem of the theory of analytic functions of one variable, and, from the theory of the integral, the approximation of integrable functions by combinations of functions of boxes and the theorem of dominated convergence.

**Corollary II.5.9 (Cluster decomposition and the Reeh–Schlieder property for the free field of spin ½).** Let ℱ₋, *V* and Ψ(*f*) be as in Theorem II.5.7. (i) For a spacelike vector *a* and all Θ, Θ′ in ℱ₋, ⟨Θ, *V*(*λa*, 1)Θ′⟩ tends to ⟨Θ, Ω⟩⟨Ω, Θ′⟩ as *λ* → ∞. (ii) Let *X* and *Y* be products of operators Ψ(*f*) and Ψ(*f*)†, and let *Y*_λ be *Y* with every function *f*(*x*) replaced by *f*(*x* − *λa*). Then ⟨Ω, *XY*_λΩ⟩ tends to ⟨Ω, *X*Ω⟩⟨Ω, *Y*Ω⟩ as *λ* → ∞. (iii) Let *D* be a non-empty open region of spacetime. The vectors obtained from Ω by products of operators Ψ(*f*) and Ψ(*f*)†, with every *f* a smooth function vanishing outside a bounded subset of *D*, span a dense subspace of ℱ₋.

*Proof.* (i) As in Theorem II.5.5(i), with a matrix *A* of SL(2, ℂ) for which Λ(*A*) is the boost used there (Theorem II.6.38), it suffices to treat *a* = (0, **a**) with **a** ≠ 0. Let Θ = *h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n and Θ′ = *l*₁∧⋯∧*l*_n with *n* ≥ 1. By Lemma II.5.8(c), ⟨Θ, *V*(*λa*, 1)Θ′⟩ is the determinant of the numbers ⟨*h*_i, *V*₁*l*_j⟩, where *V*₁ is the action of (*λa*, 1) on 𝒦. Write *h*′ and *h*″ for the parts of a vector *h* of 𝒦 in 𝒦₊ and 𝒦₋. By Theorem II.5.7(iv), ⟨*h*, *V*₁*l*⟩ is the sum of ∫ *h*′†*γ*⁰*l*′ exp(−i*λ***a**·**k**) *dμ* and of the complex conjugate of −∫ *h*″†*γ*⁰*l*″ exp(i*λ***a**·**k**) *dμ*. By Theorem II.5.7(iii) the form *u*†*γ*⁰*w* is positive definite on the columns with *P*₊(*k*)*u* = *u* and negative definite on those with *P*₋(*k*)*u* = *u*, so the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality holds for it at each *k*, and again for the integrals; the two integrands without the exponentials are therefore integrable functions of **k**. By Lemma II.5.8(a) both integrals tend to 0. Every entry of the determinant tends to 0, so the determinant does. Products of different lengths are orthogonal, and for *n* = 0 the inner product is Θ₀∗Θ′₀ = ⟨Θ, Ω⟩⟨Ω, Θ′⟩. So (i) holds for finite combinations of products. For general Θ, Θ′ and combinations Θ₁, Θ₁′, the difference of ⟨Θ, *V*Θ′⟩ and ⟨Θ₁, *V*Θ₁′⟩ is at most ‖Θ − Θ₁‖‖Θ′‖ + ‖Θ₁‖‖Θ′ − Θ₁′‖ for every unitary *V*, and the same bound holds for the limits; the combinations are dense, so (i) holds in general. (ii) By Theorem II.5.7(W4) and (W2), *Y*_λΩ = *V*(*λa*, 1)*YV*(*λa*, 1)⁻¹Ω = *V*(*λa*, 1)*Y*Ω, and ⟨Ω, *XY*_λΩ⟩ = ⟨*X*†Ω, *V*(*λa*, 1)*Y*Ω⟩, the operators being bounded. By (i) this tends to ⟨*X*†Ω, Ω⟩⟨Ω, *Y*Ω⟩.

(iii) Let Θ be orthogonal to all the vectors named; it is to be shown that Θ = 0. Step 1. Let *b* be a vector with *b*⁰ > |**b**| and *ν*(*k*) = *b*⁰*k*⁰ − **b**·**k**, which is positive on the mass shell. For Im *z* ≥ 0 let *W*₁(*z*) be the operator on 𝒦 that multiplies each vector at each *k* by the complex number exp(i*zν*(*k*)), in 𝒦₋ in the sense of its conjugate multiplication. Its modulus is at most 1, so *W*₁(*z*) is bounded by 1, and for real *z* it is the action of the translation by *zb*, because *k*·*b* = −*ν*(*k*). For *g*, *l* in 𝒦 the number ⟨*g*, *W*₁(*z*)*l*⟩ is the integral of exp(i*zν*) against a fixed integrable function; it is continuous for Im *z* ≥ 0 by dominated convergence, and analytic for Im *z* > 0, where the derivative may be taken under the integral sign because *ν* exp(−*ν* Im *z*) is bounded. Let *W*(*z*) be the map of Lemma II.5.8(c3) for *T* = *W*₁(*z*), and let Θ₂ be a finite combination of products. For a product Θ₁, ⟨Θ₁, *W*(*z*)Θ₂⟩ is a combination of determinants of such numbers, hence continuous for Im *z* ≥ 0 and analytic for Im *z* > 0. By Lemma II.5.8(c2), ‖*W*(*z*)Θ₂‖ is bounded by a constant not depending on *z*. Hence for every Θ₁ in ℱ₋ the function ⟨Θ₁, *W*(*z*)Θ₂⟩ is a uniform limit of such functions, and so is continuous for Im *z* ≥ 0 and, by the integral formula of Cauchy, analytic for Im *z* > 0. For a product *X* of operators *V*(*ζ*, 1), Ψ(*f*) and Ψ(*f*)†, which are bounded, the function *h*(*z*) = ⟨Θ, *XW*(*z*)Θ₂⟩ = ⟨*X*†Θ, *W*(*z*)Θ₂⟩ has these properties. By Lemma II.5.8(b), if *h* vanishes on an interval of the real axis it vanishes for all real *z*. Steps 2 and 3 are those of Theorem II.5.6, with each Φ(*f*_j) replaced by Ψ(*f*_j) or Ψ(*f*_j)†; the vectors to which Step 1 is applied are finite combinations of products, because the operators *V*, *B* and *B*† map such combinations to such combinations. The result is that Θ is orthogonal to the vectors obtained from Ω by products of Ψ(*f*) and Ψ(*f*)† with every *f* smooth and vanishing outside a bounded set. Step 4. Let *F* be the integral of *f* against exp(−i*k*·*x*). Each matrix *γ*^μ has norm 1, so *K*(*k*) has norm at most 4*k*⁰ and *P*₊(*k*) at most (*μ* + 4*k*⁰)/(2*μ*); since *k*⁰ ≥ *μ*, ‖*E*₊*f*‖² = ∫ *F*†*γ*⁰*P*₊*F* *dμ* is at most 5/(4*μ*) times ∫ |*F*|² *d*³*k*. As in Theorem II.5.4(W3), (1 + |**k**|²)|*F*| is at most the integral of |(1 − ∇²)*f*| over spacetime. So ‖*E*₊*f*‖, and in the same way ‖*E*₋*f*‖, is at most a constant times that integral. For a Schwartz function *f* and a smooth function *χ* equal to 1 near the origin and vanishing outside a bounded set, the integral of |(1 − ∇²)(*f* − *f*_R)| with *f*_R(*x*) = *f*(*x*)*χ*(*x*/*R*) tends to 0 as *R* → ∞. By the bound of Theorem II.5.7(W3), Ψ(*f*_R) tends to Ψ(*f*) in norm. So Θ is orthogonal to all the vectors of Theorem II.5.7(W3), which span a dense subspace, and Θ = 0. □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of Corollary II.5.9.** The proof of (iii) uses the same one fact as Theorem II.5.6: the factor exp(i*zν*) is bounded for Im *z* ≥ 0 because *ν* is positive on the mass shell, in 𝒦₊ and in 𝒦₋ alike. In 𝒦₋ this is the statement that the solutions with the phase exp(−i*k*·*x*) enter the field through the operator that adds a particle of the conjugate space, so that every state has the positive frequency of the orientation +i*c* (Theorem II.5.7(W6)).

**Lemma II.5.10 (The wavefunctions made in one region are dense; two spacelike separated regions with correlated wavefunctions).** (i) Let *D* be a non-empty open region of spacetime. The functions *Ef* of Definition II.5.1(f), with *f* smooth and vanishing outside a bounded subset of *D*, are dense in ℋ₁. (ii) Let *D*₁ be the set of the points *x* = (*x*⁰, **x**) with (*x*⁰)² + |**x**|² less than 1, and *D*₂ the set of the points with (*x*⁰)² + |**x** − **q**|² less than 1, where **q** = (4, 0, 0). No point of *D*₁ lies within or on the light cone of any point of *D*₂. There are real smooth functions *φ*, vanishing outside *D*₁, and *ψ*, vanishing outside *D*₂, such that ⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩ is a real number different from 0 and *Eφ*, *Eψ* are linearly independent.

*Proof.* (i) Let *χ* in ℋ₁ be orthogonal to all these *Ef*, and apply the proof of Theorem II.5.6 with *n* = 1, with the region *D*′ of that proof chosen bounded, to the vector Ψ of ℱ whose only component is Ψ₁ = *χ*. The functions of that proof, smooth and vanishing outside translates of *D*′ that lie in *D*, are then among the *f* named in (i). Since Φ(*f*)Ω has the only component *Ef*, the hypothesis of that proof holds for *n* = 1, and its conclusion is that *χ* is orthogonal to *Ef* for every Schwartz function *f*. By the proof of Theorem II.5.4(W3) these *Ef* include every smooth function of **k** vanishing outside a bounded set, and such functions are dense in ℋ₁. So *χ* = 0. (ii) For *x* in *D*₁ and *y* in *D*₂, |*x*⁰ − *y*⁰| is less than 2 and |**x** − **y**| is greater than 2, so *η*(*x* − *y*, *x* − *y*) > 0. *E* is linear, and ℋ₁ has more than one dimension. By (i) for *D*₂ there is a complex *f* vanishing outside *D*₂ with *Ef* ≠ 0; its real or its imaginary part is a real *ψ* with *Eψ* ≠ 0. By (i) for *D*₁ there is a complex *f* vanishing outside *D*₁ with ⟨*Ef*, *Eψ*⟩ ≠ 0, and its real or its imaginary part is a real *φ* with ⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩ ≠ 0. By (i) again, the *Ef* for *D*₁ are not all multiples of *Eψ*, so, taking real and imaginary parts, there is a real *φ*₁ vanishing outside *D*₁ with *Eφ*₁ not a multiple of *Eψ*. If *Eφ* is a multiple of *Eψ*, replace *φ* by *φ* + *tφ*₁ with a real *t* ≠ 0 so small that the inner product with *Eψ* remains different from 0. Finally, for real *φ* and *ψ* the number *K*(*φ*, *ψ*) of the proof of Theorem II.5.4(W5) is ⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩ − ⟨*Eψ*, *Eφ*⟩, and it is 0 by that theorem; so ⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩ equals its complex conjugate. □

**Theorem II.5.11 (The connection between spin and statistics for the free fields of spin 0 and spin ½).** Let *φ*, *ψ* be the functions of Lemma II.5.10(ii). (a) Spin 0 with the exclusion rule. Let ℱ₋ be the antisymmetric Fock space over ℋ₁ (Lemma II.5.8(c)), with the operators *B*, *B*† of Theorem II.5.7(v), and let Φ_a(*f*) = *B*(*E*(*f*∗)) + *B*†(*Ef*). Then Φ_a(*f*) is bounded, its adjoint is Φ_a(*f*∗), Φ_a((□ − *μ*²)*f*) = 0, and *V*(*a*, Λ)Φ_a(*f*)*V*(*a*, Λ)⁻¹ = Φ_a(*f*′) with *f*′ as in Theorem II.5.4(W4) and with *V* acting on ℱ₋ through *V*₁ on each factor. For all *f*, *g*, Φ_a(*f*)Φ_a(*g*) + Φ_a(*g*)Φ_a(*f*) is the number *J*(*f*, *g*) = ⟨*E*(*f*∗), *Eg*⟩ + ⟨*E*(*g*∗), *Ef*⟩. For the spacelike separated *φ*, *ψ*: *J*(*φ*, *ψ*) = 2⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩ ≠ 0, and (Φ_a(*φ*)Φ_a(*ψ*) − Φ_a(*ψ*)Φ_a(*φ*))Ω = 2 *Eφ*∧*Eψ* ≠ 0. The field Φ_a neither anticommutes nor commutes at spacelike separation. (b) Spin ½ with the Bose rule. Let 𝒦 = 𝒦₊ ⊕ 𝒦₋, *E*₊, *E*₋ be as in Theorem II.5.7. Let ℱ₁ be any Hilbert space with a unit vector Ω and, for every *h* in 𝒦, operators *A*(*h*), conjugate-linear in *h*, and *A*†(*h*), linear in *h*, which map a dense subspace containing Ω into itself and satisfy there ⟨Θ, *A*(*h*)Θ′⟩ = ⟨*A*†(*h*)Θ, Θ′⟩, *A*(*h*)Ω = 0, *A*(*h*)*A*†(*l*) − *A*†(*l*)*A*(*h*) = ⟨*h*, *l*⟩ and *A*(*h*)*A*(*l*) = *A*(*l*)*A*(*h*). Let Ψ_s(*f*) = *A*(*E*₊*f*) + *A*†(*E*₋*f*), with the adjoint Ψ_s(*f*)† = *A*†(*E*₊*f*) + *A*(*E*₋*f*). Let *e* be a column with *γ*⁰*e* = *e* and *e*†*e* = 1, and *f* = *φe*, *g* = *ψe*. Then Ψ_s(*f*)Ψ_s(*g*)† − Ψ_s(*g*)†Ψ_s(*f*) is the number

⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩ + (i/*μ*) ∫ *k*⁰ Im((*Eφ*)∗(*Eψ*)) *dμ*(*k*),

whose real part ⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩ is different from 0, and (Ψ_s(*f*)Ψ_s(*g*)† + Ψ_s(*g*)†Ψ_s(*f*))Ω ≠ 0. The field Ψ_s neither commutes nor anticommutes at spacelike separation. (c) Summary. Of the two rules of exchange, the free scalar field built on the positive-frequency wavefunctions has the property (W5) with the Bose rule alone (Theorem II.5.4), and the free field of spin ½ built on them has it with the exclusion rule alone (Theorem II.5.7).

*Proof.* (a) The operators *B*(*h*) and *B*†(*h*) are bounded and adjoint to each other by Theorem II.5.7(v), whose proof holds for every Hilbert space 𝒦; so Φ_a(*f*) is bounded with the adjoint *B*†(*E*(*f*∗)) + *B*(*Ef*) = Φ_a(*f*∗). *E*((□ − *μ*²)*f*) = 0 by Theorem II.5.4(W3), for *f* and for *f*∗. A unitary operator of ℋ₁ acting on each factor carries *B*(*h*) to *B*(*V*₁*h*) and *B*†(*h*) to *B*†(*V*₁*h*), and *Ef*′ = *V*₁(*a*, Λ)*Ef* by Theorem II.5.4(W4). By the relations of Theorem II.5.7(v), in Φ_a(*f*)Φ_a(*g*) + Φ_a(*g*)Φ_a(*f*) the two operators *B* give 0, the two operators *B*† give 0, and the mixed terms give ⟨*E*(*f*∗), *Eg*⟩ + ⟨*E*(*g*∗), *Ef*⟩. For real *φ*, *ψ* this is ⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩ + ⟨*Eψ*, *Eφ*⟩ = 2⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩ by Lemma II.5.10(ii). Next, Φ_a(*ψ*)Ω = *Eψ*, a product of length 1, and Φ_a(*φ*)*Eψ* = ⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩Ω + *Eφ*∧*Eψ*. Exchanging *φ* and *ψ* and subtracting, the multiples of Ω cancel, ⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩ being real, and *Eφ*∧*Eψ* − *Eψ*∧*Eφ* = 2 *Eφ*∧*Eψ*. Its squared norm is 4(‖*Eφ*‖²‖*Eψ*‖² − |⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩|²), which is positive for linearly independent vectors.

(b) Since the operators *A* commute, so do their adjoints *A*†. Hence the commutator is [*A*(*E*₊*f*)*A*†(*E*₊*g*) − *A*†(*E*₊*g*)*A*(*E*₊*f*)] + [*A*†(*E*₋*f*)*A*(*E*₋*g*) − *A*(*E*₋*g*)*A*†(*E*₋*f*)] = ⟨*E*₊*f*, *E*₊*g*⟩ − ⟨*E*₋*g*, *E*₋*f*⟩, the inner products being those of 𝒦₊ and of 𝒦₋. By Lemma II.8.7.2(iii) with (*γ*⁰)² = 1, *γ*⁰ is Hermitian, and *γ*^j*γ*⁰ = −*γ*⁰*γ*^j for *j* = 1, 2, 3; so *e*†*γ*^j*e* = *e*†*γ*^j*γ*⁰*e* = −*e*†*γ*⁰*γ*^j*e* = −*e*†*γ*^j*e*, that is, *e*†*γ*^j*e* = 0, and *e*†*K*(*k*)*e* = −*k*⁰. With *P*₊†*γ*⁰*P*₊ = *γ*⁰*P*₊ and the same for *P*₋ (Theorem II.5.7(i)), *e*†*γ*⁰*P*₊(*k*)*e* = (*μ* + *k*⁰)/(2*μ*) and *e*†*γ*⁰*P*₋(*k*)*e* = (*μ* − *k*⁰)/(2*μ*). Since *φ* is real, the integral of *φ*(*x*) exp(i*k*·*x*) is (*Eφ*)∗; so *E*₊*f* = (*Eφ*)*P*₊*e* and *E*₋*f* = (*Eφ*)∗*P*₋*e*, and likewise for *g*. Therefore ⟨*E*₊*f*, *E*₊*g*⟩ = ∫ (*Eφ*)∗(*Eψ*)(*μ* + *k*⁰)/(2*μ*) *dμ*, and, the inner product of 𝒦₋ being the conjugate of −∫ *u*†*γ*⁰*w* *dμ*, ⟨*E*₋*g*, *E*₋*f*⟩ = −∫ (*Eφ*)(*Eψ*)∗(*μ* − *k*⁰)/(2*μ*) *dμ*. The commutator is the first minus the second: the terms with *μ* give Re⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩, which is ⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩ by Lemma II.5.10(ii), and the terms with *k*⁰ give the second term of the statement. The integrals converge because *Eφ* and *Eψ* are Schwartz functions. For the last statement, Ψ_s(*g*)†Ω = *A*†(*E*₊*g*)Ω and Ψ_s(*f*)Ω = *A*†(*E*₋*f*)Ω, and applying Ψ_s(*f*) to the first and Ψ_s(*g*)† to the second gives, by the relations, a multiple of Ω plus 2Θ with Θ = *A*†(*E*₊*g*)*A*†(*E*₋*f*)Ω. Write *h* = *E*₊*g* and *l* = *E*₋*f*; they are orthogonal in 𝒦. Then ⟨Ω, Θ⟩ = ⟨*A*(*l*)*A*(*h*)Ω, Ω⟩ = 0, and ‖Θ‖² = ⟨Ω, *A*(*l*)*A*(*h*)*A*†(*h*)*A*†(*l*)Ω⟩ = ‖*h*‖²‖*l*‖², because *A*(*h*)*A*†(*h*) = ‖*h*‖² + *A*†(*h*)*A*(*h*) and *A*(*h*)*A*†(*l*)Ω = ⟨*h*, *l*⟩Ω = 0. Here ‖*h*‖² = ∫ |*Eψ*|²(*μ* + *k*⁰)/(2*μ*) *dμ* and ‖*l*‖² = ∫ |*Eφ*|²(*k*⁰ − *μ*)/(2*μ*) *dμ*; both are positive, because *Eφ* and *Eψ* are continuous and not identically 0, and *k*⁰ exceeds *μ* except at **k** = 0. So Θ ≠ 0, and it is orthogonal to Ω. The supports of *f* and *g* are those of *φ* and *ψ*. (c) is (a) and (b) with Theorems II.5.4(W5) and II.5.7(W5). □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of Theorem II.5.11.** Both fields are built on the wavefunctions of positive frequency, the orientation +i*c*. The exchange relations then produce one of two functions of two points: the difference of exp(i*k*·(*x* − *y*)) and exp(−i*k*·(*x* − *y*)) integrated over the mass shell, or their sum. The difference is a solution of the Klein–Gordon equation with vanishing value at equal times, and by Lemma II.5.3, the bound *c* on propagation, it vanishes at spacelike separation; the sum does not (Lemma II.5.10(ii)). For spin 0 the Bose rule gives the difference. For spin ½ the form *u*†*γ*⁰*w* is negative on the solutions with the phase exp(−i*k*·*x*) (Theorem II.5.7(iii)); this sign appears above as the factor (*μ* − *k*⁰)/(2*μ*), and with it the exclusion rule gives the difference and the Bose rule the sum. Scope of the theorem: it is proved for the free fields of mass *m* > 0 and of spin 0 and ½ constructed in Theorems II.5.4 and II.5.7, by computation of the two exchange functions. Pauli [141] treated free fields of every spin; his abstract concludes “the necessity of Fermi-Dirac statistics for particles with arbitrary half-integral spin” from the positivity of the energy, and Einstein–Bose statistics for integral spin from commutation at spacelike separation. The theorem for arbitrary fields with the six properties, free or interacting, is that of [116]; it is not proved in the present paper.

**Interacting fields: exact statement of what is open.** Theorems II.5.4–II.5.11 concern free fields. For interacting fields in four spacetime dimensions no construction with the six properties is known. The problem statement of the Clay Mathematics Institute [142] asks for a proof that for any compact simple gauge group a non-trivial quantum Yang–Mills theory exists on ℝ⁴ and has a mass gap, existence including axiomatic properties at least as strong as those of Streater and Wightman [116] and of Osterwalder and Schrader; its authors write that there is not yet “a mathematically complete example of a quantum gauge theory in four-dimensional space-time”. The paper [140] lists as open its target (G1), the fields on the Fock space of many particles across the interactions of the Standard Model, and its target (G3), covariance for all the interactions of the Standard Model. The present paper proves the six properties, cluster decomposition, the Reeh–Schlieder property and the connection between spin and statistics for the free fields of spin 0 and ½, and claims nothing for interacting fields.

**Haag–Kastler (1964).** Rudolf Haag and Daniel Kastler abstracted the Wightman framework into the language of local nets of operator algebras. To each open region *O* of Minkowski space is assigned a C*-algebra *A*(*O*) of observables localized in *O*; the assignment satisfies isotony, locality, covariance, and the spectrum condition. This algebraic quantum field theory programme isolates the algebraic structure of relativistic QFT from the choice of representation, but again presupposes Minkowski space as the indexing manifold. The arena remains exogenous.

**Constructive QFT (Glimm–Jaffe, 1968–present).** James Glimm and Arthur Jaffe, with collaborators including Edward Nelson and Konrad Osterwalder, undertook the construction of interacting quantum field theories satisfying the Wightman or Haag–Kastler axioms. They succeeded in low-dimensional models — φ⁴ in two and three spacetime dimensions, the Yukawa model, the sine-Gordon model. The four-dimensional case — the existence of interacting Yang–Mills theory with a mass gap — remains a Clay Millennium Problem, with active work continuing into the 2020s. This programme treats the axiomatization as a target to be matched by construction; it does not attempt to derive the axioms themselves from anything more fundamental.

**Hydrodynamic limits (Deng–Hani–Ma, 2025).** Hilbert himself, in his 1900 lecture, singled out the kinetic theory of gases as a paradigmatic subgoal of the Sixth Problem, writing of “Boltzmann’s work on the principles of mechanics” and the need to derive “the limiting processes … which lead from the atomistic view to the laws of motion of continua.” Over the twentieth century this became known as Hilbert’s sixth problem in the narrow sense: a rigorous derivation of the Euler and Navier–Stokes equations of fluid dynamics from Newton’s laws for individual particles, by way of the Boltzmann equation as the intermediate mesoscopic theory. Partial results were obtained by Lanford (1975) for short timescales in the Boltzmann–Grad limit, by Bardos–Golse–Levermore (1991, 1993) and Saint-Raymond (2009) on the Boltzmann-to-fluid passage, and by Gallagher–Saint-Raymond–Texier (2014) on the analogous problem for the linear Boltzmann equation.

On 3 March 2025, Yu Deng, Zaher Hani, and Xiao Ma posted to the arXiv (2503.01800) a paper titled *Hilbert’s Sixth Problem: Derivation of Fluid Equations via Boltzmann’s Kinetic Theory,* in which they rigorously derive the compressible Euler and incompressible Navier–Stokes–Fourier equations from hard-sphere particle systems undergoing elastic collisions on 2D and 3D tori. The proof closes the Newton-to-Boltzmann passage by extending their earlier work (arXiv:2408.07818) to control the cumulative effect of recollisions over long histories; combined with the Bardos–Golse–Levermore and Saint-Raymond Boltzmann-to-fluid theorems, this yields a single derivation of macroscopic fluid dynamics from microscopic particle mechanics. The authors themselves write, in their abstract, that the work “resolves Hilbert’s sixth problem, as it pertains to the program of deriving the fluid equations from Newton’s laws by way of Boltzmann’s kinetic theory.” This is the strongest contemporary partial answer in the kinetic-theory branch of the Sixth Problem and the most significant advance on this branch since Lanford 1975.

The achievement is, however, a stride *within* a fixed mechanical arena: Newton’s laws, the toroidal Euclidean three-space on which the particles move, and the deterministic Hamiltonian dynamics are taken as inputs. The Deng–Hani–Ma result thus closes one of the cleanest classical subgoals of the Sixth Problem on its kinetic-theory side; by its authors’ explicit qualification (“as it pertains to”), it does not address the broader axiomatization of physics, the generation of spacetime itself, or the unification of relativity and quantum theory. It is the kind of regional rigorous derivation Hilbert had in mind on the classical-statistical-mechanics side, complementary to the arena-generating programme of the present paper. §II.8.6 returns to this comparison in the context of the McGucken derivation of the second law from the McGucken Operator D_M acting on the McGucken Sphere Σ_M(τ).

**Other contemporary programmes.** Causal set theory (Bombelli, Lee, Meyer, Sorkin, 1987) generates Lorentzian spacetime from a discrete partial order, with two primitives (a set and an order relation), but does not unify with quantum theory. Twistor theory (Penrose, 1967) recasts 4-dimensional Lorentzian geometry in terms of complex projective 3-space, but the relationship to quantum amplitudes is a correspondence rather than a derivation. Spectral triples (Connes, 1980s–1990s) encode geometry in a triple (*A*, *H*, *D*) of an algebra, a Hilbert space, and a Dirac operator—three independent inputs, none derived from a single law. Loop quantum gravity, spin foam models, geometric algebra, and the topos-theoretic programme of Döring–Isham each address fragments of the Hilbertian demand without supplying its source-law.

The pattern is consistent across all of these programmes—and across the Deng–Hani–Ma 2025 hydrodynamic-limits breakthrough as well: each axiomatizes or rigorously derives a *region*—probability, non-relativistic QM, relativistic QFT, discrete spacetime, gauge structure, fluid dynamics from Newton—and each takes the arena of that region as primitive. None has supplied the source-law that generates the arena. The McGucken Axiom *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*, presented in §II.4, is the law that fills this gap: it generates the arena and the dynamics from a single line, and the six regional programmes surveyed above appear, retrospectively, as the shadows it casts on probability, on non-relativistic QM, on QFT, on causal sets, on gauge structure, and on the Newton-to-Boltzmann-to-fluid chain. The remainder of this paper makes that statement precise.

### II.6. Establishing a New Categorical Foundation for Mathematical Physics which Completes the Erlangen Programme

The McGucken Principle asserts that the fourth dimension *x*₄ is expanding at the velocity of light: *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*, with *x*₄ = i*ct*, as a spherically symmetric McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action *h* and whose volume defines a nonlocality.

### **From Erlangen 1872 to the Source-Law of 2026.**

On 7 October 1872, the twenty-three-year-old Felix Klein delivered his inaugural address at the University of Erlangen, the document now known as the *Vergleichende Betrachtungen über neuere geometrische Forschungen* — the Erlangen Programme. The proposal was radical for its time: every geometry, Klein argued, is the theory of the *invariants* of a transformation group acting on a homogeneous space. Each geometry is therefore captured by a Klein pair (*G*, *H*) — *G* the group of motions, *H* the stabilizer of a chosen point — with the underlying space realized as the coset space *G*/*H*. Euclidean, affine, projective, conformal, hyperbolic, and elliptic geometries, which had grown up as separate disciplines through the nineteenth century, were thereby unified inside a single conceptual frame. The Erlangen Programme was the first time geometry was organized by a meta-principle rather than by a list of axioms about points and lines.

The programme’s influence was, and remains, enormous. Sophus Lie’s continuous groups gave Klein pairs their analytic backbone. Henri Poincaré’s work on automorphic functions and the foundations of relativity adopted Klein’s group-theoretic stance. Élie Cartan in the 1920s generalized homogeneous spaces to *Cartan geometries*, in which the Klein pair becomes the local model of a curved geometry — the language in which general relativity was eventually rewritten. Hermann Weyl’s 1918 and 1929 papers on gauge invariance, Charles Ehresmann’s 1950 theory of fibre bundles and connections, and the entire Yang–Mills programme of the 1950s and beyond all sit inside the Erlangen frame. In a parallel development, Saunders Mac Lane and Samuel Eilenberg in 1945 introduced category theory, and Alexander Grothendieck in the 1960s reorganized algebraic geometry around it; this raised the natural question whether geometry, like algebra, ought to be organized as a category whose objects are spaces and whose morphisms preserve the relevant structure. To that question the Erlangen Programme had no answer of its own.

What Klein’s programme accomplished — and what it never attempted — must be stated cleanly. It accomplished the *classification* of geometries by their symmetry groups. It never attempted to *generate* the Klein pair from a deeper law. The group *G* and the stabilizer *H* are posited externally; the geometry begins once they are chosen. For 154 years no source was offered. Cartan, Weyl, Ehresmann, and Yang–Mills extended the geometric vocabulary that follows from a Klein pair, but they presupposed the pair. Mac Lane, Eilenberg, and Grothendieck supplied the categorical vocabulary, but the spaces and operators inhabiting their categories were still posited, not generated.
