The Erlangen Programme thus stood as a *frame without a source*, awaiting completion by some law that could simultaneously produce the space, the group, and the operator algebra of physics.

This is the gap that the McGucken Principle closes. The single law *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* generates, in one stroke, both members of the Klein pair on which mathematical physics rests. The completion is not metaphorical: there is a theorem that the law produces the symmetry group, and a theorem that the law produces the categorical structure, and these two theorems are independent routes from the same axiom. After 154 years the Erlangen Programme finally has a source.

### **The Source-Pair (McGucken Space ℳ_G, McGucken Operator D_M) and the Category McG**.

The McGucken Principle supplies the missing source explicitly. From the single law *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* one obtains *simultaneously* two structures — the McGucken Space ℳ_G and the McGucken Operator D_M — neither prior to the other, neither posited as a separate axiom, each generating and containing the other in a reciprocal pair [88, 91]. The Theorem of Section II.4 establishes this co-generation rigorously: the McGucken Space ℳ_G is the integrated kernel of the source law and the McGucken Operator D_M is the unique normalized tangent operator to that kernel; the same one-line equation produces both, with no auxiliary postulate.

This co-generation defines a new category — here written **McG** — whose objects are pairs (*X*, *D*) of a space co-generated with its operator algebra by an instance of the source law, and whose morphisms are the maps that preserve the source-pair structure. In its current form [130, Definitions 3.1–3.2] the category is the Six-Object McGucken Category McG₆. Its objects are the six members of the McGucken Source-Tuple, namely the McGucken Sphere, the Moving-Dimension Manifold, the McGucken Space, the McGucken Operator, the McGucken Symmetry and the McGucken Action, together with a distinguished source-axiom point representing *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*. Its morphisms are the canonical extractions recovering *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* from each object, the construction rules building each object from *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*, and the 30 generation procedures between ordered pairs of distinct objects. The category **McG** is not a renaming of an existing category; the source law itself is part of the data of every object, and the morphisms must intertwine the source-law action. In **McG**, Minkowski space with its derivation operator, Hilbert space with its evolution generator, phase space with its Poisson bracket, and gauge bundle with its covariant derivative are *objects*, not separately axiomatized arenas.

### **The Source-Pair Theorems.**

Three theorems establish the category **McG** as a genuine foundation rather than a renaming of standard structure [91]:

**(T1) Mutual Containment.** Every state of the McGucken Space ℳ_G determines an action of the McGucken Operator D_M, and every action of the McGucken Operator D_M realizes a state of the McGucken Space ℳ_G. Neither structure is a sub-object of an external ambient category; each is contained in the other through the source law. The theorem is MCC₆, Generalized Mutual Containment (Theorem 4.1 of [130]): for every object *X* of the McGucken Source-Tuple there exists a canonical extraction procedure Π_X : *X* → *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* that recovers the McGucken Axiom in full from *X* alone, using only standard mathematical operations.

**(T2) Reciprocal Generation.** The map the McGucken Space ℳ_G → McGucken Operator D_M induced by *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* is invertible at the level of generators. The space generates the operator and the operator generates the space; there is no asymmetry of priority, and the inversion is not a separate postulate but a consequence of the single law. The theorem is the Reciprocal Generation Theorem (Theorem 3.7 of [130]): the source-pair (McGucken Space ℳ_G, McGucken Operator D_M) generated by *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* satisfies (R1) every point *p* of the McGucken Space generates a McGucken Operator at *p* uniquely; (R2) the family of all pointwise McGucken Operators generates the McGucken Space as a whole, the carrier, the constraint function up to a nonzero scalar, the global operator and the McGucken Sphere wavefront structure each being reconstructed (Operator-to-Space Theorem, Theorem 3.6 of [130]); (R3) the generations R1 and R2 are simultaneous and reciprocal, with no temporal or logical priority of point over operator or operator over point.

**(T3) Containment of the Klein Pair.** The following Klein pairs (*G*, *H*) are proved in this paper to descend from (McGucken Space ℳ_G, McGucken Operator D_M): the pair (ISO⁺(1,3), SO⁺(1,3)) of spacetime (Theorems II.6.28 and II.6.30), the unitary pair of the space of states (Theorem II.6.33), the symplectic pair of phase space (Theorem II.6.34), the Euclidean pair of a spatial slice (Theorem II.6.36), the Galilean pair as the limit of large *c* (Theorem II.6.37), the spin pair (Theorem II.6.38), the conformal pair of the null cone (Theorems II.6.39 and II.6.41) and the de Sitter and anti-de Sitter pairs (Theorem II.6.43); for a curved spacetime the group of Theorem II.6.28 acts at each event (Theorem II.6.44). The gauge pairs enter through the covariantization of the McGucken Operator D_M (Theorems II.8.5.4 and II.8.7.8), the selection of the group U(1) × SU(2) × SU(3) being Conjecture 31 of [89] and the subject of [133]. The Erlangen programme is thereby contained in McG, not adjoined to it [88, 91]. For the relativistic pair this is the McGucken Symmetry object of McG₆, the invariance group of *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*, from which the Klein pair (ISO(1,3), SO⁺(1,3)) descends [130, Table 3.1]. The three source-pair theorems are stated and proved in full below as the Mutual Containment Theorem (Theorem II.6.16), the Reciprocal Generation Theorem (Theorem II.6.20) and the Containment-Generation Equivalence (Theorem II.6.23), the descent of the Klein pair of spacetime as Theorems II.6.28 and II.6.30, and the further pairs as Theorems II.6.33, II.6.34, II.6.36–II.6.39 and II.6.41–II.6.44.

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* Reading of the Source-Pair: Definitions, Lemmas, and the Mutual Containment Theorem, the Reciprocal Generation Theorem and the Containment-Generation Equivalence, with Proofs.**

**McGucken Principle anchor.** The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* states that the fourth dimension is expanding at the velocity of light in a spherically symmetric manner from every spacetime event, as a McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action *h*. For the pair formed by the McGucken Space and the McGucken Operator the Principle predicts three facts: each member of the pair holds the law *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* in full, each member produces the other by an explicit procedure, and these two facts are equivalent. The statements and proofs below are those of [91], §§II.2–5, reproduced in full so that each step can be checked here.

**Definition II.6.1 (Four-coordinate carrier; Definition 2.2 of [91]).** The four-coordinate carrier is *E*₄ = {(*x*₁, *x*₂, *x*₃, *x*₄) : *x*₁, *x*₂, *x*₃ ∈ ℝ, *x*₄ ∈ ℂ}, with **x** = (*x*₁, *x*₂, *x*₃) the spatial three-vector and *t* ∈ ℝ the temporal parameter against which the advance of the fourth coordinate is measured.

**Definition II.6.2 (McGucken constraint and constraint surface; Definition 2.3 of [91]).** The McGucken constraint function is Φ_M(*t*, *x*₄) = *x*₄ − i*ct*. The McGucken constraint surface is the zero set Φ_M = 0, that is, the set of (*t*, *x*₄) ∈ ℝ × ℂ with *x*₄ = i*ct*. It is the surface *C_M* = 0 of §II.2, since *C_M* = (i/*c*) Φ_M.

**Definition II.6.3 (McGucken Operator; Definition 2.4 of [91]).** The McGucken Operator is the first-order linear differential McGucken Operator D_M = ∂ₜ + i*c* ∂₄, acting on functions *Ψ*(*t*, **x**, *x*₄) [differentiable in *t* and holomorphic in *x*₄] by the McGucken Operator D_M *Ψ* = ∂ₜ*Ψ* + i*c* ∂₄*Ψ*.

**Definition II.6.4 (The McGucken Sphere; Definition 2.5 of [91]).** To each event *p* of the constraint surface the spherically symmetric expansion of *x*₄ at rate *c* from *p* assigns the McGucken Sphere of *p*: at parameter time *t* it is the 2-sphere of radius *c*(*t* − *t*₀) centered at *p*, where *t*₀ is the time of *p*.

**Definition II.6.5 (McGucken Space; Definition 2.6 of [91]).** The McGucken Space ℳ_G is the four-tuple formed by the carrier *E*₄, the constraint function Φ_M, the McGucken Operator D_M, and the assignment of the McGucken Sphere to every event. The McGucken Space is a space together with its law: the constraint, the flow operator and the spherical propagation are constituent data.

**Definition II.6.6 (Source-pair; Definition 2.7 of [91]).** The McGucken source-pair is the ordered pair (McGucken Space ℳ_G, McGucken Operator D_M).

**Definition II.6.7 (Containment, constructive sense; Definition 2.9 of [91]).** An object *X* contains a relation *R* if there is a definite procedure that extracts *R* from *X* explicitly.

**Definition II.6.8 (Generation, constructive sense; Definition 2.10 of [91]).** An object *X* generates an object *Y* if there is a definite procedure that produces *Y* from *X* explicitly.

**Lemma II.6.9 (Tangency of the McGucken Operator; Lemma 3.1 of [91]).** The McGucken Operator D_M Φ_M = 0.

*Proof.* By Definition II.6.2, Φ_M = *x*₄ − i*ct*, so ∂ₜΦ_M = −i*c* and ∂₄Φ_M = 1. Therefore the McGucken Operator D_M Φ_M = ∂ₜΦ_M + i*c* ∂₄Φ_M = −i*c* + i*c* = 0. Hence the McGucken Operator D_M is tangent to every level set of Φ_M, in particular to the constraint surface. □

**Lemma II.6.10 (Characteristic invariance; Lemma 3.2 and Corollary 3.3 of [91]).** For every differentiable function *F* of one complex variable, *Ψ*(*t*, *x*₄) = *F*(*x*₄ − i*ct*) satisfies the McGucken Operator D_M *Ψ* = 0. Conversely, every *Ψ*(*t*, *x*₄) [differentiable in *t* and holomorphic in *x*₄] with the McGucken Operator D_M *Ψ* = 0 is a function of *u* = *x*₄ − i*ct* alone.

*Proof.* Let *u* = *x*₄ − i*ct* and *Ψ* = *F*(*u*). The chain rule gives ∂ₜ*Ψ* = −i*c* *F*′(*u*) and ∂₄*Ψ* = *F*′(*u*), so the McGucken Operator D_M *Ψ* = −i*c* *F*′(*u*) + i*c* *F*′(*u*) = 0. Conversely, change variables from (*t*, *x*₄) to (*t*, *u*) and write *Ψ*(*t*, *x*₄) = *G*(*t*, *u*). Then ∂ₜ*Ψ* = ∂ₜ*G* − i*c* ∂_u*G* and ∂₄*Ψ* = ∂_u*G*, so the McGucken Operator D_M *Ψ* = ∂ₜ*G*. Hence the McGucken Operator D_M *Ψ* = 0 exactly when *G* is independent of *t*, that is, when *Ψ* depends on (*t*, *x*₄) through *u* alone. □

**Lemma II.6.11 (Generator equivalence; Lemma 3.4 of [91]).** (a) The integral curves of the McGucken Operator D_M, viewed as the vector field (1, i*c*) on the (*t*, *x*₄)-plane, satisfy *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*. (b) The chain-rule derivative along any curve satisfying *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* equals the McGucken Operator D_M acting on the function being differentiated.

*Proof.* (a) The integral curves of the vector field (1, i*c*) satisfy *dt*/*ds* = 1 and *dx*₄/*ds* = i*c*, where *s* is the curve parameter. Dividing, *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*. (b) For *Ψ*(*t*, *x*₄) and any curve satisfying *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*, *dΨ*/*dt* = ∂ₜ*Ψ* + (*dx*₄/*dt*) ∂₄*Ψ* = ∂ₜ*Ψ* + i*c* ∂₄*Ψ* = McGucken Operator D_M *Ψ*. □

**Theorem II.6.12 (Containment of *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* in the McGucken Operator; Theorem 5.1 of [91]).** Let *L* = *a* ∂ₜ + *b* ∂₄ be a first-order linear differential operator on the (*t*, *x*₄)-plane with *a*, *b* ∈ ℂ. The following two conditions together force *L* = McGucken Operator D_M: (i) *L* is tangent to the constraint surface, *L*Φ_M = 0; (ii) *L* is normalized in the temporal direction, *a* = 1. Under these conditions *b* = i*c*, and the ratio of coefficients *b*/*a* = i*c* recovers the law *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*. The law is therefore contained in the McGucken Operator D_M as the ratio of its coefficients.

*Proof.* Tangency requires *L*Φ_M = *a* ∂ₜΦ_M + *b* ∂₄Φ_M = −i*ac* + *b* = 0, so *b* = i*ac*. With *a* = 1 this gives *b* = i*c*, hence *L* = ∂ₜ + i*c* ∂₄ = McGucken Operator D_M. The ratio *b*/*a* = i*c* is the slope *dx*₄/*dt* of the integral curves of *L* by Lemma II.6.11(a). The extraction procedure is: read off the coefficients, take their ratio, and read the result as *dx*₄/*dt*. □

**Theorem II.6.13 (Operator-containment of *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* in the McGucken Space; Theorem 5.3 of [91]).** The law *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* is contained in the McGucken Space ℳ_G through its third component the McGucken Operator D_M.

*Proof.* By Definition II.6.5, the McGucken Space ℳ_G has the McGucken Operator D_M as its third component. By Theorem II.6.12, the McGucken Operator D_M contains *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* as the ratio of its coefficients. Containment in the sense of Definition II.6.7 is transitive: if *X* contains *Y* and *Y* contains *Z*, composing the two extraction procedures extracts *Z* from *X*. The procedure here is: project onto the third component to obtain the McGucken Operator D_M; read off its coefficients; take their ratio. The result is i*c* = *dx*₄/*dt*. □

**Theorem II.6.14 (Constraint-containment of *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* in the McGucken Space; Theorem 5.4 of [91]).** The law *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* is contained in the McGucken Space ℳ_G through its second component, the constraint function Φ_M = *x*₄ − i*ct*.

*Proof.* Differentiating Φ_M along a curve (*t*, *x*₄(*t*)) gives *d*Φ_M/*dt* = *dx*₄/*dt* − i*c*. A curve lying in the constraint surface has Φ_M = 0 at every *t*, hence *d*Φ_M/*dt* = 0 along it, and therefore *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*. The extraction procedure is: project onto the second component to obtain Φ_M; differentiate with respect to *t* along a curve in the zero set; set the result to zero. □

**Corollary II.6.15 (Two-fold containment; Corollary 5.5 of [91]).** The McGucken Space contains *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* in two ways at once, through the McGucken Operator D_M (Theorem II.6.13) and through Φ_M (Theorem II.6.14), and both extractions give *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*.

*Proof.* Theorems II.6.13 and II.6.14 establish the two extractions independently, and each ends in *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*. □

**Theorem II.6.16 (Mutual Containment Theorem; Theorem 5.7 of [91]).** Each member of the source-pair (McGucken Space ℳ_G, McGucken Operator D_M) contains the law *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* in full: (a) the McGucken Operator contains it as the ratio of its coefficients under tangency and normalization; (b) the McGucken Space contains it both through the McGucken Operator D_M as third component and through Φ_M as second component.

*Proof.* (a) is Theorem II.6.12. (b) is Corollary II.6.15. □

**Theorem II.6.17 (Operator-to-Space generation procedure; Theorem 5.9 of [91]).** There is an explicit four-step procedure that produces the McGucken Space ℳ_G from the McGucken Operator D_M alone.

*Proof (by construction).* The sole input is the McGucken Operator D_M = ∂ₜ + i*c* ∂₄. *Step 1 (carrier).* The McGucken Operator D_M differentiates with respect to *t* and *x*₄ and acts on functions of (*t*, **x**, *x*₄). The carrier is therefore the four-coordinate space *E*₄ of Definition II.6.1 with parameter *t*. *Step 2 (kernel).* By Lemma II.6.10 the solutions of the McGucken Operator D_M *Ψ* = 0 are the functions *F*(*x*₄ − i*ct*, **x**): they depend on (*t*, *x*₄) through the combination *u* = *x*₄ − i*ct* alone. *Step 3 (constraint).* The kernel consists of the functions constant on the level sets of *u*. [The kernel determines these level sets; among the functions of *u*, the normalization ∂₄Φ_M = 1 and Φ_M(0, 0) = 0 selects Φ_M = *u*.] The constraint function is Φ_M = *x*₄ − i*ct*, and the constraint surface is its zero set. *Step 4 (the McGucken Sphere).* On the constraint surface *x*₄ = i*ct*, so *dx*₄ = i*c* *dt* and *dx*₄² = −*c*²*dt*². Substituting into the four-coordinate Euclidean interval *dx*₁² + *dx*₂² + *dx*₃² + *dx*₄² gives the Lorentzian interval *d***x**² − *c*²*dt*². Its vanishing, |*d***x**| = *c* *dt*, defines from each event *p* the set of points at distance *r* = *c*(*t* − *t*₀): the McGucken Sphere of Definition II.6.4. Steps 1–4 give the carrier, the constraint function, the operator and the McGucken Sphere at every event, which together are the McGucken Space ℳ_G. □

**Theorem II.6.18 (Space-to-Operator generation procedure; Theorem 5.11 of [91]).** There is an explicit three-step procedure that produces the McGucken Operator D_M from the McGucken Space ℳ_G, using only the carrier and the constraint function.

*Proof (by construction).* *Step 1 (differentiate the constraint).* Read Φ_M = *x*₄ − i*ct* from the McGucken Space ℳ_G. By the proof of Theorem II.6.14, a curve in the constraint surface satisfies *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*. *Step 2 (chain rule).* For *Ψ*(*t*, *x*₄, **x**) along a curve satisfying *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*, *dΨ*/*dt* = ∂ₜ*Ψ* + (*dx*₄/*dt*) ∂₄*Ψ* = ∂ₜ*Ψ* + i*c* ∂₄*Ψ*. *Step 3 (identify the operator).* The right-hand side is the action of ∂ₜ + i*c* ∂₄ = McGucken Operator D_M. The procedure does not use the third component of the McGucken Space ℳ_G as input; it produces the McGucken Operator D_M as output. □

**Theorem II.6.19 (Mutual inverseness; Theorem 5.13 of [91]).** The procedure of Theorem II.6.17 followed by the procedure of Theorem II.6.18 returns the McGucken Operator D_M, and the procedure of Theorem II.6.18 followed by the procedure of Theorem II.6.17 returns the McGucken Space ℳ_G.

*Proof.* *From the McGucken Space and back.* Start from the McGucken Space ℳ_G. Theorem II.6.18 gives the McGucken Operator D_M. Apply Theorem II.6.17 to the McGucken Operator D_M: Step 1 recovers the carrier *E*₄ from the coordinates on which the McGucken Operator D_M acts, which is the carrier of the McGucken Space ℳ_G; Steps 2 and 3 recover Φ_M = *x*₄ − i*ct*, which is the constraint function of the McGucken Space ℳ_G; Step 4 constructs the McGucken Sphere at every event from the constraint, which is the assignment in the McGucken Space ℳ_G. The composition returns the McGucken Space ℳ_G. *From the McGucken Operator and back.* Start from the McGucken Operator D_M. Theorem II.6.17 gives the McGucken Space ℳ_G with constraint function Φ_M = *x*₄ − i*ct*. Apply Theorem II.6.18: Step 1 yields *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*; Step 2 yields the chain-rule operator ∂ₜ + i*c* ∂₄; Step 3 identifies it as the McGucken Operator D_M. The composition returns the McGucken Operator D_M. □

**Theorem II.6.20 (Reciprocal Generation Theorem; Theorem 5.14 of [91]).** Each member of the source-pair (McGucken Space ℳ_G, McGucken Operator D_M) generates the other: (a) the four-step procedure of Theorem II.6.17 produces the McGucken Space ℳ_G from the McGucken Operator D_M; (b) the three-step procedure of Theorem II.6.18 produces the McGucken Operator D_M from the McGucken Space ℳ_G; (c) the two procedures are mutually inverse.

*Proof.* (a), (b) and (c) are Theorems II.6.17, II.6.18 and II.6.19. □

**Theorem II.6.21 (Containment implies generation; Theorem 5.16 of [91]).** If *X* contains a relation *R* in the sense of Definition II.6.7, then *X* generates whatever follows from *R* by a definite construction. For the source-pair, the Mutual Containment Theorem implies the Reciprocal Generation Theorem.

*Proof.* By Definition II.6.7 there is a procedure extracting *R* from *X*. If *Y* follows from *R* by a definite construction, composing the extraction with that construction is a definite procedure producing *Y* from *X*, so *X* generates *Y* by Definition II.6.8. Apply this to the source-pair. By Theorem II.6.16, the McGucken Operator D_M contains *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* and the McGucken Space ℳ_G contains *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*. The law *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* produces the McGucken Space ℳ_G by a definite construction (integration with *x*₄(0) = 0 to *x*₄ = i*ct*, the constraint Φ_M = *x*₄ − i*ct*, the carrier, and the McGucken Sphere by Step 4 of Theorem II.6.17), and it produces the McGucken Operator D_M by the chain rule of Lemma II.6.11(b). Hence the McGucken Operator D_M generates the McGucken Space ℳ_G and the McGucken Space ℳ_G generates the McGucken Operator D_M. □

**Theorem II.6.22 (Generation implies containment; Theorem 5.17 of [91]).** If *X* generates *Y* in the sense of Definition II.6.8, then *X* contains the defining data of *Y*. For the source-pair, the Reciprocal Generation Theorem implies the Mutual Containment Theorem.

*Proof.* By Definition II.6.8 there is a definite procedure producing *Y* from *X*; that procedure extracts *Y* from *X*, which is containment in the sense of Definition II.6.7. Apply this to the source-pair. The procedure of Theorem II.6.17 produces from the McGucken Operator D_M the function Φ_M (Steps 2 and 3), and *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* is extracted from Φ_M by Theorem II.6.14; so the McGucken Operator D_M contains *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*. The procedure of Theorem II.6.18 produces *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* from Φ_M in its first step; so the McGucken Space ℳ_G contains *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* through Φ_M, and through the McGucken Operator D_M by Theorem II.6.13. □

**Theorem II.6.23 (Containment-Generation Equivalence; Theorem 5.18 of [91]).** For the source-pair (McGucken Space ℳ_G, McGucken Operator D_M), the Mutual Containment Theorem and the Reciprocal Generation Theorem are equivalent: each member of the pair contains *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* in full if and only if each member generates the other.

*Proof.* The Mutual Containment Theorem implies the Reciprocal Generation Theorem by Theorem II.6.21. The Reciprocal Generation Theorem implies the Mutual Containment Theorem by Theorem II.6.22. □

**Corollary II.6.24 (The pair is a single object; Corollary 5.19 of [91]).** The source-pair (McGucken Space ℳ_G, McGucken Operator D_M) is the law *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* written in two notations: the geometric notation the McGucken Space ℳ_G, with its carrier, constraint and McGucken Sphere, and the operator notation the McGucken Operator D_M = ∂ₜ + i*c* ∂₄.

*Proof.* By Theorem II.6.16 the law is present in both members. By Theorem II.6.20 each member determines the other through mutually inverse procedures. A pair in which each member holds the same law and each is recovered from the other carries one piece of data, *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*. □

### **The Double Completion of the Erlangen Programme.**

The most striking feature of the completion is that it occurs in two derivationally independent ways at once. There are two routes from *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* to the Klein pair, and each route completes the Erlangen Programme in its own language [89, 90]. The theorem is the Erlangen Double-Completion (Theorem 21 of [89]; Theorems 1–3 of [90]; Theorem 7.1 of [130]), with Route 1 proved as Theorem 6 of [89], Route 2 as Theorem 11 of [89], and the independence of the two routes as Theorem 14 of [89]: Route 1 (group-theoretic, Klein-internal) supplies the physical generator that selects the relativistic Klein pair (ISO(1,3), SO⁺(1,3)) from within Klein’s group-invariant architecture; Route 2 (category-theoretic, Klein-deepening) replaces Klein’s primitive group-space pair (*G*, *X*) with the co-generated source-pair (McGucken Space ℳ_G, McGucken Operator D_M) and replaces the Klein category with McG₆ through the six-object source-tuple and its three adjunctions. Both routes are rooted in the same axiom (in the mathematical reading) and the same physical principle (in the physical reading); their independence is the independence of the two languages, not of two postulates.

**Route 1: The Group-Theoretic Route.** The first route works in the language Klein himself used. The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*, the statement that *x*₄ expands at rate *c* from every event, is preserved by exactly the transformations that intermix (*t*, **x**) while leaving *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* invariant. That invariance group is the Lorentz/Poincaré group; it is derived, not posited. The stabilizer of any chosen event is the Lorentz subgroup; the homogeneous space *G*/*H* is Minkowski space; the Klein pair (Iso(1,3), SO(1,3)) emerges as the unique pair that leaves the source law invariant [89]. In Klein’s own language, then, the axiom *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* singles out the geometry of relativity by the symmetry-criterion of 1872. The theorems are these [74]. Under the labelled hypotheses A1–A5 of [74] (a connected, oriented, smooth event four-manifold; local orthonormal coordinates in which the Principle reads *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*; the Euclidean four-coordinate inner product *dx*₁² + *dx*₂² + *dx*₃² + *dx*₄²; event homogeneity; the +i*c* orientation), substituting *dx*₄ = i*c* *dt* gives the form η = *dx*₁² + *dx*₂² + *dx*₃² − *c*²*dt*² of signature (3,1) (Lemma 1 of [74]), and the connected component of its real-linear isometry group is the proper orthochronous Lorentz group SO⁺(1,3) (Theorem 24 of [74]). With flat completeness added (Hypothesis A6 of [74]), the connected group of affine isometries is the Poincaré group ISO⁺(1,3) = **R**^(1,3) ⋊ SO⁺(1,3) (Theorem 27 of [74]).

**Route 2: The Category-Theoretic Route.** The second route works in the language of Mac Lane and Eilenberg. The McGucken Principle, viewed as the simultaneous generator of the source-pair (McGucken Space ℳ_G, McGucken Operator D_M), defines the category **McG** directly. The Klein pairs proved in this paper — Lorentz/Poincaré (Theorem II.6.30), unitary (Theorem II.6.33), symplectic (Theorem II.6.34), Euclidean (Theorem II.6.36), Galilean (Theorem II.6.37), spin (Theorem II.6.38) and conformal (Theorem II.6.39) — are obtained from the source-pair, and the morphisms between their arenas are the correspondences (Wick rotation, geometric quantization, gauge covariantization) that physicists construct by hand in the standard formulation [90]. The source law plays the role of the universal morphism: every arrow in **McG** is an instance of *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* acting on its co-generated source-pair.

**Why the Two Routes Agree.** The two routes are not two competing derivations whose agreement requires explanation; they are two faithful translations of the same source law into two languages. The group route fixes the symmetry of a single physical region by isolating the invariance group of *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*. The category route fixes the relations *between* regions by exhibiting **McG** as the category in which those regions appear as objects and those relations as morphisms. Yet both descend, step by step, from the same one-line law: in Route 1 the law is the invariance criterion; in Route 2 the law is the source of objects and morphisms. They agree on the recovered geometry because they cannot disagree — the recovered geometry is, in both cases, the geometry of *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* itself.

Together, the two routes constitute a *double completion*: the Erlangen Programme is finished simultaneously in group theory and in category theory, by the same axiom [89, 90]. This is not a coincidence between independent results but a feature of the source law — that one equation is rich enough to fix the geometry under both criteria, the symmetry-classification of 1872 and the categorical organization of 1945.

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* Reading of Klein’s Erlangen Programme: The Two Routes to the Klein Pair (ISO⁺(1,3), SO⁺(1,3)), with Proofs.**

**McGucken Principle anchor.** The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* states that the fourth dimension is expanding at the velocity of light in a spherically symmetric manner from every spacetime event, as a McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action *h*. For Klein’s programme the Principle predicts the group and the subgroup that Klein’s rule takes as given: the interval generated by *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* has the Poincaré group as its invariance group and the Lorentz group as the stabilizer of an event. The statements and proofs below are those of [89] (Lemmas 4 and 5, Theorems 6, 9, 11 and 14) and [91] (Theorem 7.18).

**Lemma II.6.25 (Lorentzian signature from *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*; Lemma 4 of [89]).** The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* generates the Lorentzian line element *ds*² = *dx*₁² + *dx*₂² + *dx*₃² − *c*²*dt*² of signature (−, +, +, +).

*Proof.* From *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*, *dx*₄ = i*c* *dt* along any integral curve. Squaring, *dx*₄² = (i*c*)² *dt*² = −*c*²*dt*². The four-coordinate carrier *E*₄ has line element *dx*₁² + *dx*₂² + *dx*₃² + *dx*₄². Substituting gives *ds*² = *dx*₁² + *dx*₂² + *dx*₃² − *c*²*dt*². The minus sign is generated by i² = −1 and the factor *c*² by the rate in the Principle. (§II.9.1 gives the same computation as the pullback of the quadratic form along the embedding of the constraint surface.) □

**Lemma II.6.26 (Invariance group of the interval; Lemma 5 of [89]).** The group of invertible affine transformations of ℝ⁴ with coordinates *x*^μ = (*ct*, *x*₁, *x*₂, *x*₃) preserving the line element *ds*² = *η*_μν *dx*^μ *dx*^ν, *η* = diag(−1, 1, 1, 1), is the group ISO(1,3) of maps *x* ↦ *Λx* + *a* with *Λ*ᵀ*ηΛ* = *η* and *a* ∈ ℝ⁴. Its connected component of the identity is the Poincaré group ISO⁺(1,3) = ℝ^(1,3) ⋊ SO⁺(1,3). The stabilizer of an event in ISO⁺(1,3) is SO⁺(1,3), and ℝ^(1,3) ≅ ISO⁺(1,3)/SO⁺(1,3).

*Proof.* Under an affine map *x* ↦ *Λx* + *a* the differentials transform as *dx* ↦ *Λ* *dx*, so *ds*² ↦ *dx*ᵀ *Λ*ᵀ*ηΛ* *dx*. This equals *dx*ᵀ *η* *dx* for all *dx* exactly when *Λ*ᵀ*ηΛ* = *η*, because a symmetric matrix is determined by its quadratic form. The matrices with *Λ*ᵀ*ηΛ* = *η* form the group O(1,3); with the translations they form ISO(1,3). Taking determinants gives (det *Λ*)² = 1, and the 00 component of *Λ*ᵀ*ηΛ* = *η* gives (*Λ*⁰₀)² = 1 + Σ_i (*Λ*ⁱ₀)² ≥ 1; the two signs of det *Λ* and of *Λ*⁰₀ separate O(1,3) into four pieces, and the piece with det *Λ* = 1 and *Λ*⁰₀ ≥ 1, which contains the identity, is SO⁺(1,3). This piece is connected: for *Λ* in it, the boost *B* that carries the unit time vector *e*₀ to *Λe*₀ lies on a one-parameter subgroup through the identity, and *B*⁻¹*Λ* fixes *e*₀ and has determinant 1, hence is a rotation of the orthogonal three-space, an element of the connected group SO(3). The translations ℝ⁴ are connected, so the identity component of ISO(1,3) is ℝ^(1,3) ⋊ SO⁺(1,3). An element *x* ↦ *Λx* + *a* fixes the event *x* = 0 exactly when *a* = 0, so the stabilizer of that event is SO⁺(1,3), and the stabilizer of any other event is conjugate to it by a translation. The translations carry *x* = 0 to every event, so the action is transitive, and the map sending the coset of an element to the image of *x* = 0 identifies ISO⁺(1,3)/SO⁺(1,3) with ℝ^(1,3). □

**Lemma II.6.27 (The isometries of the interval are affine).** Let *X* = *X*^μ ∂_μ be a vector field on ℝ⁴ whose flow preserves *η*_μν *dx*^μ *dx*^ν. Then *X*_μ = *a*_μ + *ω*_μν *x*^ν with *a*_μ constant and *ω*_μν = −*ω*_νμ constant. The flows of these fields are the translations and the one-parameter subgroups of SO⁺(1,3).

*Proof.* With *X*_μ = *η*_μν *X*^ν, preservation of the interval by the flow is the Killing equation ∂_μ *X*_ν + ∂_ν *X*_μ = 0. Differentiating, ∂_ρ ∂_μ *X*_ν + ∂_ρ ∂_ν *X*_μ = 0. Write *T*_ρμν = ∂_ρ ∂_μ *X*_ν, which is symmetric in its first two indices; the equation states *T*_ρμν = −*T*_ρνμ. Then *T*_ρμν = −*T*_ρνμ = −*T*_νρμ = *T*_νμρ = *T*_μνρ = −*T*_μρν = −*T*_ρμν, using the two properties alternately, so *T*_ρμν = 0. Hence each *X*_ν is a polynomial of degree at most one, *X*_μ = *a*_μ + *ω*_μν *x*^ν, and the Killing equation gives *ω*_μν + *ω*_νμ = 0. The flow of the constant field *a* is translation by multiples of *a*. The flow of *ω*^μ_ν *x*^ν is *x* ↦ exp(*sω*) *x*, and (*ηω*)ᵀ = −*ηω* gives exp(*sω*)ᵀ *η* exp(*sω*) = *η*, with exp(*sω*) connected to the identity through *s* → 0, so exp(*sω*) ∈ SO⁺(1,3). □

**Theorem II.6.28 (Route 1, symmetry route; Theorem 6 of [89]).** Let the McGucken Principle be the primitive physical relation *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*. Then the Klein pair (ISO⁺(1,3), SO⁺(1,3)) of relativistic spacetime is derived from the McGucken Principle.

*Proof.* By Lemma II.6.25, the McGucken Principle generates the Lorentzian line element. By Lemma II.6.26, the invariance group of this line element is ISO(1,3), with identity component the Poincaré group ISO⁺(1,3), the stabilizer of an event is SO⁺(1,3), and ℝ^(1,3) ≅ ISO⁺(1,3)/SO⁺(1,3). Each step is forced by the Principle: the signature by i² = −1, the rate by the factor *c*, the invariance group by the resulting line element, the stabilizer by the action at a fixed event. □

**Theorem II.6.29 (Source-pair co-generation along the flow; Theorem 9 of [89]).** (i) The level sets of Φ_M = *x*₄ − i*ct* are the integral curves of the vector field *V* = ∂ₜ + i*c* ∂₄ in the (*t*, *x*₄)-plane. (ii) The directional derivative along *V* is the McGucken Operator D_M. (iii) Both arise from the single relation *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*, as its integral form and its differential form.

*Proof.* (i) The integral curves of *V* satisfy *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* by Lemma II.6.11(a). Integrating from a point (*t*₀, *x*₄⁰) gives *x*₄(*t*) − *x*₄⁰ = i*c*(*t* − *t*₀), that is, Φ_M(*t*, *x*₄) = Φ_M(*t*₀, *x*₄⁰): each integral curve is a level set of Φ_M, and each level set is such a curve. (ii) The directional derivative along *V* is ∂ₜ + i*c* ∂₄ = McGucken Operator D_M. (iii) *V* is the vector field with unit *t*-component whose integral curves satisfy *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*; the level sets of Φ_M and the McGucken Operator D_M are both read from *V*. □

**Theorem II.6.30 (Route 2, source-pair route; Theorem 11 of [89] and Theorem 7.18 of [91]).** The Klein pair (ISO⁺(1,3), SO⁺(1,3)) is obtained from the source-pair (McGucken Space ℳ_G, McGucken Operator D_M) in four steps, each determined by *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*.

*Proof.* *Step 1, from the law to the constraint surface.* *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* with *x*₄(0) = 0 integrates to *x*₄ = i*ct*, the zero set of Φ_M (Theorem II.6.29(i)). *Step 2, from the constraint surface to the Lorentzian metric.* Parametrize the constraint surface, taken with the three spatial coordinates, by (*t*, **x**) ↦ (**x**, i*ct*). The pullback of *dx*₁² + *dx*₂² + *dx*₃² + *dx*₄² along this parametrization is *dx*₁² + *dx*₂² + *dx*₃² − *c*²*dt*² by Lemma II.6.25. *Step 3, from the metric to the isometry group.* By Lemma II.6.27 every one-parameter group of isometries of this metric consists of the affine maps of Lemma II.6.26, and by Lemma II.6.26 the connected group they generate is ISO⁺(1,3) = ℝ^(1,3) ⋊ SO⁺(1,3). The orientation +i*c* of the Principle is the time orientation preserved by SO⁺(1,3). *Step 4, from the group to the Klein pair.* Choose the event *p*₀ at the origin of the expansion. By Lemma II.6.26 its stabilizer is SO⁺(1,3) and ISO⁺(1,3)/SO⁺(1,3) ≅ ℝ^(1,3). The chain is *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* → Φ_M → Lorentzian metric → ISO⁺(1,3) → (ISO⁺(1,3), SO⁺(1,3)). In this route the source-pair is constructed before any group is introduced, and the Klein pair is read from the metric that the source-pair carries. □

**Theorem II.6.31 (Independence of the two routes; Theorem 14 of [89]).** (i) Route 1 (Theorem II.6.28) has the Klein pair as its object and constructs no source-pair. (ii) Route 2 (Theorem II.6.30) has the source-pair as its object and reaches the Klein pair after the source-pair is constructed. (iii) The two routes share the input *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* and the endpoint (ISO⁺(1,3), SO⁺(1,3)).

*Proof.* (i) The proof of Theorem II.6.28 uses Lemmas II.6.25 and II.6.26 only: it derives a metric and then that metric’s invariance group. Neither the McGucken Space ℳ_G nor the McGucken Operator D_M appears in it. (ii) The proof of Theorem II.6.30 begins with Theorem II.6.29, which constructs Φ_M and the McGucken Operator D_M from the flow before any group action is introduced, and it reaches the group in Step 3. (iii) Both proofs start from *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* and end at the same pair; both use Lemma II.6.25 for the metric, which is where the two routes meet. □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of Klein’s rule.** Klein’s 1872 rule takes a group *G* and a subgroup *H* as given and studies the invariants of *G* on *G*/*H*. Theorems II.6.28 and II.6.30 derive the pair for relativistic spacetime, *G* = ISO⁺(1,3) and *H* = SO⁺(1,3), from *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*. The scope is stated in Theorem 4 of [90]: “The paper proves that the physical Lorentzian Klein pair arises from the McGucken Principle along two independent routes.”

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* Reading of the Unitary and Symplectic Klein Pairs of Mechanics, with Proofs.** **McGucken Principle anchor.** The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* states that the fourth dimension is expanding at the velocity of light in a spherically symmetric manner from every spacetime event, as a McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action *h*. Theorems II.6.28 and II.6.30 obtain from it the Klein pair of spacetime as the group that preserves the interval. The same Principle gives two further quantities, and it predicts a Klein pair for each: the total of the Born density, ∫ |*f*|² *d*³*x*, conserved by the expansion of *x*₄ (Theorem II.8.4.1), whose group is the unitary group acting on the space of states (Theorem II.6.33); and the commutator i*ℏ* of position and momentum (Theorem II.8.3.5), whose group is the affine symplectic group acting on phase space (Theorem II.6.34).

**Definition II.6.32 (Klein pair).** A Klein pair (*G*, *H*) with space *X* consists of a group *G* acting on a set *X* so that every point of *X* is carried to every other by some element of *G*, and the subgroup *H* of the elements that fix a chosen point *x*₀. The map that sends *g* to *gx*₀ takes the same value on *g* and *g*′ exactly when *g*⁻¹*g*′ lies in *H*, so it identifies *X* with the set of cosets *gH*. In Theorem II.6.28, *X* is spacetime, *G* = ISO⁺(1,3) and *H* = SO⁺(1,3).

**Theorem II.6.33 (The unitary Klein pair of the space of states).** Let ℋ be the Hilbert space of Theorem II.8.4.3 with the inner product ⟨*f*|*g*⟩ of Theorem II.8.4.2 and *N*(*f*) = ⟨*f*|*f*⟩. A state is a ray [*φ*], the set of the vectors *λφ* with |*λ*| = 1, for a vector *φ* with *N*(*φ*) = 1; by Proposition II.9.3.2 these are the wavefunctions of one emission referred to the different origins of *x*₄. Let *X* be the set of states and let U(ℋ) be the set of linear maps *W* of ℋ onto itself with ⟨*Wf*|*Wg*⟩ = ⟨*f*|*g*⟩ for all *f*, *g*. (i) A linear map of ℋ onto itself that preserves *N* preserves the inner product; so U(ℋ) is the group of the linear bijections that preserve the conserved total *N*. (ii) U(ℋ) acts on *X* by [*φ*] → [*Wφ*], and the number |⟨*φ*|*ψ*⟩|², which depends on the two states alone, is unchanged by the action. (iii) For any two states there is an element of U(ℋ) carrying the first to the second. (iv) Fix a state [*φ*₀] and let *K* be the set of the vectors *g* with ⟨*φ*₀|*g*⟩ = 0. The elements of U(ℋ) that fix [*φ*₀] are the maps *W*(*aφ*₀ + *g*) = *λaφ*₀ + *W*′*g*, with |*λ*| = 1 and *W*′ a unitary map of *K* onto itself; this subgroup is U(1) × U(*K*). Hence (U(ℋ), U(1) × U(*K*)) is a Klein pair with space *X*. (v) The evolutions of Theorem II.8.4.3(i) belong to U(ℋ). The conjugation *f* → *f*∗ carries states to states and leaves |⟨*φ*|*ψ*⟩|² unchanged, and it does not belong to U(ℋ).

*Proof.* (i) For a form that is conjugate-linear in the first argument and linear in the second, 4⟨*f*|*g*⟩ = *N*(*f* + *g*) − *N*(*f* − *g*) − i*N*(*f* + i*g*) + i*N*(*f* − i*g*): the first two terms give 4 Re⟨*f*|*g*⟩, and *N*(*f* + i*g*) − *N*(*f* − i*g*) = 4 Re(i⟨*f*|*g*⟩) = −4 Im⟨*f*|*g*⟩. A linear map *W* with *N*(*Wf*) = *N*(*f*) for all *f* preserves each of the four terms, since *W*(*f* ± *g*) = *Wf* ± *Wg* and *W*(*f* ± i*g*) = *Wf* ± i*Wg*. The composition of two such maps and the inverse of one are such maps. (ii) If *N*(*φ*) = 1 then *N*(*Wφ*) = 1, and *W*(*λφ*) = *λWφ*, so the ray of *φ* is carried into the ray of *Wφ*. Replacing *φ* by *λφ* and *ψ* by *λ*′*ψ* multiplies ⟨*φ*|*ψ*⟩ by a number of modulus 1, and ⟨*Wφ*|*Wψ*⟩ = ⟨*φ*|*ψ*⟩.

(iii) Let *N*(*φ*) = *N*(*ψ*) = 1. Choose *λ* with |*λ*| = 1 so that *χ* = *λψ*, a vector of the ray of *ψ*, has ⟨*φ*|*χ*⟩ = *s* real and non-negative. If *χ* = *φ*, the identity map serves. Otherwise *N*(*φ* − *χ*) = 2 − 2*s* > 0; put *w* = (*φ* − *χ*)/(2 − 2*s*)^(1/2), so that *N*(*w*) = 1, and *Wf* = *f* − 2⟨*w*|*f*⟩*w*. This map is linear, and *W*(*Wf*) = *f* − 4⟨*w*|*f*⟩*w* + 4⟨*w*|*f*⟩⟨*w*|*w*⟩*w* = *f*, so it is a bijection. It preserves the inner product: ⟨*Wf*|*Wg*⟩ = ⟨*f*|*g*⟩ − 2⟨*f*|*w*⟩⟨*w*|*g*⟩ − 2⟨*f*|*w*⟩⟨*w*|*g*⟩ + 4⟨*f*|*w*⟩⟨*w*|*g*⟩ = ⟨*f*|*g*⟩. And ⟨*w*|*φ*⟩ = (1 − *s*)/(2 − 2*s*)^(1/2), so 2⟨*w*|*φ*⟩*w* = 2(1 − *s*)(*φ* − *χ*)/(2 − 2*s*) = *φ* − *χ* and *Wφ* = *χ*. (iv) Let *W* be in U(ℋ) with *Wφ*₀ = *λφ*₀. If ⟨*φ*₀|*g*⟩ = 0 then ⟨*λφ*₀|*Wg*⟩ = ⟨*Wφ*₀|*Wg*⟩ = 0, so *W* maps *K* into *K*; the inverse of *W* fixes the same ray, so it maps *K* into *K* too, and *W* maps *K* onto *K*, preserving the inner product. Every vector is *aφ*₀ + *g* with *a* = ⟨*φ*₀|*f*⟩ and *g* in *K*, in exactly one way, which gives the stated form. Conversely each map of that form is linear, onto, and preserves *N*, since *N*(*aφ*₀ + *g*) = |*a*|² + *N*(*g*). The correspondence of *W* with the pair (*λ*, *W*′) respects composition. With (iii) and Definition II.6.32 this is the Klein pair. (v) The first statement is Theorem II.8.4.3(i). For the conjugation, ⟨*f*∗|*g*∗⟩ = ⟨*f*|*g*⟩∗, which has the same modulus; but (i*f*)∗ = −i*f*∗, so the conjugation is not a linear map. □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of Theorem II.6.33.** The invariant of this geometry is |⟨*φ*|*ψ*⟩|², the Born probability of §II.9.3, as the interval is the invariant of the geometry of Theorem II.6.28. The group is fixed by the Principle through part (i): it is the group of the linear maps that preserve the total conserved by the expansion of *x*₄. The linearity is with respect to the complex numbers, whose unit i is the i of *x*₄ = i*ct*; the conjugation of part (v) is the conjugate expansion *x*₄∗ = −i*ct* of Definition II.9.3.1, and it lies outside the group. Wigner’s theorem [138], that every bijection of the states preserving |⟨*φ*|*ψ*⟩|² comes from a unitary map or from a unitary map followed by the conjugation, is an external theorem and is not used.

**Theorem II.6.34 (The symplectic Klein pair of phase space).** Let *q*₁, *q*₂, *q*₃ and *p*₁, *p*₂, *p*₃ be the operators of position and momentum of Theorem II.8.3.5 on the Schwartz functions, and write *r* for the column of these six operators in this order. Let *J* be the 6 × 6 matrix with the blocks 0, 1 in the first row and −1, 0 in the second, each block 3 × 3, and for columns *z*, *z*′ of six real numbers let *ω*(*z*, *z*′) = *z*ᵀ*Jz*′. (i) The canonical relations are *r*_m*r*_l − *r*_l*r*_m = i*ℏ* *J*_ml for *m*, *l* = 1, …, 6. For *z* = (*a*, *b*) put *L*(*z*) = Σ_j (*a*_j*q*_j + *b*_j*p*_j); then *L*(*z*)*L*(*z*′) − *L*(*z*′)*L*(*z*) = i*ℏ* *ω*(*z*, *z*′). (ii) Let *S* be a real 6 × 6 matrix and *ζ* a column of six real numbers, and let *r*′ = *Sr* + *ζ*, the six operators *r*′_m = Σ_l *S*_ml*r*_l + *ζ*_m. The operators *r*′ satisfy the canonical relations if and only if *SJS*ᵀ = *J*. (iii) *SJS*ᵀ = *J* if and only if *S*ᵀ*JS* = *J*, that is, *ω*(*Sz*, *Sz*′) = *ω*(*z*, *z*′) for all *z*, *z*′. The matrices with this property form a group, the real symplectic group Sp(6, ℝ). (iv) The maps *z* → *Sz* + *ζ* of the phase space ℝ⁶ of the values of position and momentum, with *S* in Sp(6, ℝ), form a group *G* under composition, and carrying out two transformations of (ii) in succession corresponds to composing the two maps. *G* carries every point of ℝ⁶ to every other, and the elements of *G* fixing the origin are those with *ζ* = 0. Hence (*G*, Sp(6, ℝ)) is a Klein pair with space ℝ⁶.

*Proof.* (i) By Theorem II.8.3.5(ii), *q*_j*p*_k − *p*_k*q*_j = i*ℏ* δ_jk. Two multiplications by coordinates commute, and two partial derivatives of a Schwartz function commute, so *q*_j*q*_k − *q*_k*q*_j = 0 and *p*_j*p*_k − *p*_k*p*_j = 0. In the order of the column *r* these are the stated relations: *J* has the entry 1 in the row of *q*_j and the column of *p*_j, the entry −1 in the row of *p*_j and the column of *q*_j, and zeros elsewhere. Since *L*(*z*) = Σ_m *z*_m*r*_m, the commutator of *L*(*z*) and *L*(*z*′) is Σ_m Σ_l *z*_m*z*′_l i*ℏ* *J*_ml = i*ℏ* *z*ᵀ*Jz*′. (ii) Multiples of the identity commute with every operator, so the commutator of *r*′_m and *r*′_n is Σ_l Σ_k *S*_ml*S*_nk(*r*_l*r*_k − *r*_k*r*_l) = i*ℏ* Σ_l Σ_k *S*_ml*J*_lk*S*_nk = i*ℏ*(*SJS*ᵀ)_mn. The canonical relations for *r*′ state that this equals i*ℏ* *J*_mn for all *m*, *n*. (iii) *J*² = −1, so *J* is invertible, and *SJS*ᵀ = *J* shows that the determinant of *S* is not zero. From *SJS*ᵀ = *J*, *JS*ᵀ = *S*⁻¹*J*, hence *S*ᵀ = *J*⁻¹*S*⁻¹*J* and *S*ᵀ*JS* = *J*⁻¹*S*⁻¹*J*²*S* = −*J*⁻¹ = *J*. The converse is the same computation applied to *S*ᵀ. Since *ω*(*Sz*, *Sz*′) = *z*ᵀ*S*ᵀ*JSz*′, the condition *S*ᵀ*JS* = *J* is the invariance of *ω*. If *S* and *T* satisfy it, so do *ST*, because (*ST*)ᵀ*J*(*ST*) = *T*ᵀ(*S*ᵀ*JS*)*T* = *J*, and *S*⁻¹, because multiplying *S*ᵀ*JS* = *J* by the inverses on both sides gives *J* = (*S*⁻¹)ᵀ*JS*⁻¹. (iv) The composition of *z* → *Sz* + *ζ* after *z* → *Tz* + *ξ* is *z* → *STz* + *Sξ* + *ζ*, and the inverse of *z* → *Sz* + *ζ* is *z* → *S*⁻¹*z* − *S*⁻¹*ζ*; by (iii) both have their matrix in Sp(6, ℝ). Applying (ii) with (*T*, *ξ*) and then with (*S*, *ζ*) to the result gives *S*(*Tr* + *ξ*) + *ζ* = *STr* + *Sξ* + *ζ*. The map *z* → *z* + (*z*₂ − *z*₁) carries *z*₁ to *z*₂, and *S*·0 + *ζ* = 0 exactly when *ζ* = 0. With Definition II.6.32 this is the Klein pair. □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of Theorem II.6.34.** The form *ω* is the commutator of Theorem II.8.3.5 divided by i*ℏ*: the i is the i of *x*₄ = i*ct* and ℏ is the action of one wavelength of the McGucken Sphere divided by 2π. Part (ii) shows that the group of this geometry is fixed by that commutator, as the group of Theorem II.6.28 is fixed by the interval: the affine symplectic group, with the translations of phase space in the place of the translations of spacetime and Sp(6, ℝ) in the place of SO⁺(1,3). Its elements are the linear canonical transformations of Hamilton’s mechanics. The theorem is stated for one body; for *n* coordinates the same proof gives Sp(2*n*, ℝ). The construction of unitary operators on ℋ that carry out these transformations is not part of the theorem.

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* Reading of the Euclidean, Galilean, Spin and Conformal Klein Pairs, with Proofs.** **McGucken Principle anchor.** The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* states that the fourth dimension is expanding at the velocity of light in a spherically symmetric manner from every spacetime event, as a McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action *h*. From the interval *dx*₁² + *dx*₂² + *dx*₃² + *dx*₄² that the Principle gives (§II.9.1) and the group of Theorem II.6.28, it predicts four further Klein pairs: the Euclidean pair of the three dimensions *x*₁, *x*₂, *x*₃ at one value of *t* (Theorem II.6.36); the Galilean pair, reached when the rate *c* is taken large compared with every other velocity (Theorem II.6.37); the spin pair, whose group acts on the two-component amplitudes of Theorem II.8.3.7 and covers the group of Theorem II.6.28 twice (Theorem II.6.38); and the conformal pair, whose invariant is the null cone *ds*² = 0 traced by the McGucken Spheres of an event (Theorem II.6.39). Throughout, *x* = (*x*⁰, **x**) with *x*⁰ = *ct*, *η* = diag(−1, 1, 1, 1), and *η*(*x*, *y*) = *η*_μν*x*^μ*y*^ν.

**Lemma II.6.35 (The group SO⁺(1,3) by its conditions).** Let 𝒟₊ be the set of the real 4 × 4 matrices Λ with Λᵀ*η*Λ = *η*, Λ⁰₀ ≥ 1 and det Λ = 1. (i) Every rotation of ℝ³ is a product of rotations about the coordinate axes. (ii) Every Λ in 𝒟₊ is a product of a rotation, a boost along the first axis (Theorem II.8.1.7) and a rotation. (iii) 𝒟₊ is the connected component of the identity among the matrices with Λᵀ*η*Λ = *η*; it is the group SO⁺(1,3) of Lemma II.6.26.

*Proof.* (i) Let ρ be a rotation and **v** = ρ**e**₃, with **e**₃ the unit vector of the third axis. By the polar angles of **v** there are a rotation *Y* about the second axis and a rotation *Z* about the third axis with *ZY***e**₃ = **v**. Then (*ZY*)⁻¹ρ is a rotation that fixes **e**₃, hence a rotation about the third axis, and ρ = *ZY*(*ZY*)⁻¹ρ. (ii) This is shown in the proof of Lemma II.5.2. (iii) For every matrix with Λᵀ*η*Λ = *η*, the determinant is 1 or −1, and the component 00 of the equation gives (Λ⁰₀)² = 1 + Σ_j (Λ^j₀)² ≥ 1. The determinant and Λ⁰₀ are continuous functions of the matrix, so along a continuous path of such matrices the determinant cannot pass from 1 to −1 nor Λ⁰₀ from the values ≥ 1 to the values ≤ −1: a path that begins at the identity stays in 𝒟₊. Conversely, by (i) and (ii) each Λ in 𝒟₊ is a product of rotations about coordinate axes by angles *θ*₁, …, *θ*_n and one boost with a velocity *v*; replacing the angles by *τθ*₁, …, *τθ*_n and the velocity by *τv* gives, for 0 ≤ *τ* ≤ 1, a continuous path of matrices with Λᵀ*η*Λ = *η* from the identity to Λ. □

**Theorem II.6.36 (The Euclidean Klein pair of a spatial slice).** (i) The elements of ISO⁺(1,3) that leave the time *t* of every event unchanged are the maps (*t*, **x**) → (*t*, ρ**x** + **a**) with ρ a rotation matrix, ρᵀρ = 1 and det ρ = 1, and **a** a vector of ℝ³. (ii) They form a group, ISO(3), which acts on each slice *t* = constant, carries every point of the slice to every other, and preserves the distance |**x** − **y**|, which is the interval of two events of the slice. The elements fixing the point **x** = 0 are the rotations. Hence (ISO(3), SO(3)) is a Klein pair with space ℝ³.

*Proof.* (i) An element of ISO⁺(1,3) is *x* → Λ*x* + *b* with Λᵀ*η*Λ = *η*, Λ⁰₀ ≥ 1 and det Λ = 1 (Lemma II.6.26 and Lemma II.6.35). The time is unchanged for every event exactly when *b*⁰ = 0, Λ⁰₀ = 1 and Λ⁰_j = 0. The component 00 of Λᵀ*η*Λ = *η* reads −(Λ⁰₀)² + Σ_j (Λ^j₀)² = −1, so Λ^j₀ = 0. Then Λ consists of the entry 1 and a 3 × 3 block ρ, the spatial components of Λᵀ*η*Λ = *η* read ρᵀρ = 1, and det Λ = det ρ = 1. Conversely such a Λ satisfies the three conditions. (ii) The composition of **x** → ρ**x** + **a** after **x** → ρ′**x** + **a**′ is **x** → ρρ′**x** + ρ**a**′ + **a**, and the inverse is **x** → ρ⁻¹**x** − ρ⁻¹**a**; both are of the same form. The translation by **y** − **x** carries **x** to **y**. Since ρᵀρ = 1, |ρ**x** − ρ**y**| = |**x** − **y**|, and for two events with the same *t* the interval is −*c*²·0 + |**x** − **y**|². The map fixes **x** = 0 exactly when **a** = 0. □

**Theorem II.6.37 (The Galilean Klein pair as the limit of large *c*).** (i) For a rotation ρ, vectors **u**, **a** of ℝ³ and a real number *s*, let Γ(*s*, **a**, **u**, ρ) be the map (*t*, **x**) → (*t* + *s*, ρ**x** + **u***t* + **a**) of the set ℝ⁴ of events. These maps form a group, the Galilean group. It carries every event to every other, and the elements fixing the event (0, 0) are the maps (*t*, **x**) → (*t*, ρ**x** + **u***t*), which form the group ℝ³ ⋊ SO(3) of the pairs (**u**, ρ). Hence the Galilean group with this subgroup is a Klein pair with space ℝ⁴. (ii) Every Galilean map preserves the difference of the times of two events, and the distance |**x** − **y**| of two events with the same time. (iii) Let **u** have the length *w* and the direction of the first axis, and let *B*_c be the transformation of Theorem II.8.1.7 with the velocity −*w*: *t*′ = *γ*(*t* + *wx*₁/*c*²), *x*₁′ = *γ*(*x*₁ + *wt*), *x*₂′ = *x*₂, *x*₃′ = *x*₃, with *γ* = (1 − *w*²/*c*²)^(−1/2). For *c* ≥ 2*w*, |*t*′ − *t*| ≤ (*w*²/*c*²)|*t*| + 2*w*|*x*₁|/*c*² and |*x*₁′ − *x*₁ − *wt*| ≤ (*w*²/*c*²)(|*x*₁| + *w*|*t*|). Hence for each fixed event, as *c* → ∞ with *w* fixed, *B*_c(*t*, **x**) tends to (*t*, **x** + **u***t*). (iv) For every Galilean map Γ there are elements *P*_c of ISO⁺(1,3), defined for all *c* > |**u**|, such that *P*_c(*t*, **x**) tends to Γ(*t*, **x**) for every event as *c* → ∞.

*Proof.* (i) The composition of Γ(*s*, **a**, **u**, ρ) after Γ(*s*′, **a**′, **u**′, ρ′) sends (*t*, **x**) to (*t* + *s*′ + *s*, ρρ′**x** + (ρ**u**′ + **u**)*t* + ρ**a**′ + **u***s*′ + **a**), which is Γ(*s* + *s*′, ρ**a**′ + **u***s*′ + **a**, ρ**u**′ + **u**, ρρ′). Solving *t*′ = *t* + *s*, **x**′ = ρ**x** + **u***t* + **a** for (*t*, **x**) gives the inverse Γ(−*s*, ρ⁻¹(**u***s* − **a**), −ρ⁻¹**u**, ρ⁻¹). The map Γ(*s*, **a**, 0, 1) carries (0, 0) to (*s*, **a**), so with inverses every event is carried to every other. Γ fixes (0, 0) exactly when *s* = 0 and **a** = 0, and by the law of composition these elements multiply as (**u**, ρ)(**u**′, ρ′) = (ρ**u**′ + **u**, ρρ′). (ii) The times of two events both increase by *s*. For two events with the same *t* the images differ by ρ**x** − ρ**y**, of length |**x** − **y**|. (iii) From *γ*² − 1 = *γ*²*w*²/*c*², *γ* − 1 = *γ*²(*w*²/*c*²)/(*γ* + 1). For *c* ≥ 2*w*, *γ*² ≤ 4/3, so *γ* ≤ 2 and *γ* − 1 ≤ *w*²/*c*². Then *t*′ − *t* = (*γ* − 1)*t* + *γwx*₁/*c*² and *x*₁′ − *x*₁ − *wt* = (*γ* − 1)(*x*₁ + *wt*) give the two bounds, and both bounds tend to 0 as *c* → ∞. (iv) Let σ be a rotation that turns **u** into the direction of the first axis, and let *w* = |**u**|. By Theorem II.6.36 the rotations and the translations in space and time belong to ISO⁺(1,3), and so does *B*_c for *c* > *w* (Theorem II.8.1.7). Put *P*_c = *T* σ⁻¹ *B*_c σ ρ, where ρ and σ act on **x** alone and *T* is the translation by (*s*, **a**). By (iii) and the continuity of σ⁻¹ and *T*, *P*_c(*t*, **x**) tends to the image under *T* of the event with the time *t* and the place σ⁻¹ applied to σρ**x** + *wt***e**₁, where **e**₁ is the unit vector of the first axis. Since σ⁻¹ carries *w***e**₁ to **u**, that place is ρ**x** + **u***t*, and the image under *T* is (*t* + *s*, ρ**x** + **u***t* + **a**). □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of Theorem II.6.37.** The rate *c* of the expansion of *x*₄ is finite, and the group of nature is that of Theorem II.6.28. The Galilean group is what that group becomes for motions slow compared with *c*: by part (iii) the difference between *B*_c and the Galilean map is of the order *w*²/*c*² and *wx*₁/*c*². Its two invariants of part (ii) are what remains of the one interval: the interval divided by −*c*² tends to the square of the difference of the times, and for equal times the interval is the square of the distance.

**Theorem II.6.38 (The spin Klein pair: SL(2, ℂ) covers SO⁺(1,3) twice).** Let σ₁, σ₂, σ₃ be the Pauli matrices and, for an event *x*, let *M*(*x*) = *x*⁰ + *x*¹σ₁ + *x*²σ₂ + *x*³σ₃, a Hermitian 2 × 2 matrix; every Hermitian 2 × 2 matrix is *M*(*x*) for exactly one *x*, and det *M*(*x*) = −*η*(*x*, *x*). Let SL(2, ℂ) be the group of complex 2 × 2 matrices *A* with det *A* = 1. (i) For *A* in SL(2, ℂ) there is exactly one real 4 × 4 matrix Λ(*A*) with *AM*(*x*)*A*† = *M*(Λ(*A*)*x*) for all *x*; it belongs to SO⁺(1,3), and Λ(*AB*) = Λ(*A*)Λ(*B*). (ii) Λ(*A*) = 1 exactly when *A* = 1 or *A* = −1. (iii) For a unit vector **n** and a real *θ*, the matrix *A* = cos(*θ*/2) − i sin(*θ*/2)(*n*₁σ₁ + *n*₂σ₂ + *n*₃σ₃) has Λ(*A*) equal to the rotation by the angle *θ* about **n**, and the diagonal matrix *A* with the entries exp(*s*/2), exp(−*s*/2) has Λ(*A*) equal to the boost *x*⁰ → *x*⁰ cosh *s* + *x*³ sinh *s*, *x*³ → *x*⁰ sinh *s* + *x*³ cosh *s*. (iv) Every element of SO⁺(1,3) is Λ(*A*) for exactly two matrices, *A* and −*A*. (v) The pairs (*a*, *A*), with *a* in ℝ⁴ and *A* in SL(2, ℂ), acting on spacetime by *x* → Λ(*A*)*x* + *a*, form a group *G* with the law (*a*, *A*)(*b*, *B*) = (*a* + Λ(*A*)*b*, *AB*). *G* carries every event to every other, and the elements fixing *x* = 0 are the (0, *A*). Hence (*G*, SL(2, ℂ)) is a Klein pair with space the spacetime of Theorem II.6.28, and (*a*, *A*) → (*a*, Λ(*A*)) maps *G* onto ISO⁺(1,3), two elements to one.

*Proof.* The matrices 1, σ₁, σ₂, σ₃ are a basis of the real vector space of Hermitian 2 × 2 matrices, and *M*(*x*) has the rows (*x*⁰ + *x*³, *x*¹ − i*x*²) and (*x*¹ + i*x*², *x*⁰ − *x*³), with determinant (*x*⁰)² − (*x*¹)² − (*x*²)² − (*x*³)². (i) *AM*(*x*)*A*† is Hermitian and depends linearly on *x*, so it is *M*(Λ*x*) for one real matrix Λ = Λ(*A*). Its determinant is |det *A*|² det *M*(*x*) = det *M*(*x*), so *η*(Λ*x*, Λ*x*) = *η*(*x*, *x*) for all *x*, and by the identity 4*η*(*x*, *y*) = *η*(*x* + *y*, *x* + *y*) − *η*(*x* − *y*, *x* − *y*), Λᵀ*η*Λ = *η*. Since (*AB*)*M*(*AB*)† = *A*(*BMB*†)*A*†, Λ(*AB*) = Λ(*A*)Λ(*B*). The component 0 of *x* is half the trace of *M*(*x*), so Λ(*A*)⁰₀ is half the trace of *AA*†, which is positive; with Λᵀ*η*Λ = *η* this gives Λ⁰₀ ≥ 1. For the determinant three facts about 2 × 2 matrices are used; each rests on part (iii), whose proof below does not use the determinant. First, a Hermitian matrix is *α* + *rN* with *α* real, *r* ≥ 0 and *N* = *n*₁σ₁ + *n*₂σ₂ + *n*₃σ₃ for a unit vector **n**, because 1, σ₁, σ₂, σ₃ are a basis. A rotation about an axis perpendicular to **n** and to the third axis carries **n** to the unit vector of the third axis; by (iii) the corresponding matrix *C* satisfies *CNC*† = σ₃, and *CC*† = 1 because *C* = cos(*θ*/2) − i sin(*θ*/2)*N*′ with (*N*′)² = 1. So *C*(*α* + *rN*)*C*† is diagonal with the entries *α* + *r*, *α* − *r*. If the Hermitian matrix is positive, both entries are positive, and *C*† times the diagonal matrix of their square roots times *C* is a positive Hermitian matrix whose square is the given one. Second, a unitary matrix *V* with det *V* = 1 has the rows (*u*, −*v*∗) and (*v*, *u*∗) with |*u*|² + |*v*|² = 1, because its inverse is both *V*† and, the determinant being 1, the matrix with the rows (*V*₂₂, −*V*₁₂) and (−*V*₂₁, *V*₁₁). With *u* = *a* − i*s*₃ and *v* = *s*₂ − i*s*₁, *a* and the *s*_j real, this is *V* = *a* − i(*s*₁σ₁ + *s*₂σ₂ + *s*₃σ₃) with *a*² + *s*₁² + *s*₂² + *s*₃² = 1, that is, *V* = cos(*θ*/2) − i sin(*θ*/2)*N* for some *θ* and unit vector. Third, since *N*² = 1, (cos *a* − i sin *a* *N*)(cos *b* − i sin *b* *N*) = cos(*a* + *b*) − i sin(*a* + *b*)*N*, and det(cos *a* − i sin *a* *N*) = cos²*a* + sin²*a* = 1; so *V* is the square of the matrix cos(*θ*/4) − i sin(*θ*/4)*N* of SL(2, ℂ). Now let *A* be in SL(2, ℂ). *A*†*A* is Hermitian, and it is positive because the product of a column *z* ≠ 0 with *A*†*Az* is the squared length of *Az*, which is not zero. Let *Q* be its positive Hermitian square root and *V* = *AQ*⁻¹; then *V*†*V* = *Q*⁻¹*A*†*AQ*⁻¹ = 1. Since det *Q* > 0, |det *V*| = 1 and det *V* det *Q* = 1, both determinants are 1. *Q* then has the diagonal form with entries *d*, 1/*d*, and it is the square of *C*† times the diagonal matrix with the entries *d*^(1/2), *d*^(−1/2) times *C*, a matrix of SL(2, ℂ). So *A* = *VQ* is a product of two squares of elements of SL(2, ℂ), and det Λ(*A*), a product of two squares of numbers ±1, is 1.

(ii) If *AM*(*x*)*A*† = *M*(*x*) for all *x*, then *x* = (1, 0, 0, 0) gives *AA*† = 1, hence *AM* = *MA* for every Hermitian *M*, and for every complex 2 × 2 matrix, since each is *M*₁ + i*M*₂ with *M*₁, *M*₂ Hermitian. A matrix commuting with all matrices is a multiple *λ* of the identity, and det *A* = *λ*² = 1. (iii) Write *N* = *n*₁σ₁ + *n*₂σ₂ + *n*₃σ₃ and, for a vector **x**, *S* = *x*¹σ₁ + *x*²σ₂ + *x*³σ₃. From σ_jσ_k = δ_jk + i Σ_l ε_jkl σ_l follow *N*² = 1, *NS* − *SN* = 2i times the matrix of **n** × **x**, and *NSN* = 2(**n**·**x**)*N* − *S*. With *A* = cos(*θ*/2) − i sin(*θ*/2)*N* and *A*† = cos(*θ*/2) + i sin(*θ*/2)*N*, *ASA*† = cos²(*θ*/2)*S* + sin²(*θ*/2)*NSN* − i sin(*θ*/2) cos(*θ*/2)(*NS* − *SN*), which is the matrix of the vector cos*θ* **x** + (1 − cos*θ*)(**n**·**x**)**n** + sin*θ* **n** × **x**; and *A*·1·*A*† = 1, so *x*⁰ is unchanged. For **n** along the third axis this vector is (*x*¹cos*θ* − *x*²sin*θ*, *x*¹sin*θ* + *x*²cos*θ*, *x*³), the rotation by *θ*; for a general **n** the vector is unchanged if **x** is along **n** and is turned by *θ* in the plane perpendicular to **n**. For the diagonal matrix, *AM*(*x*)*A*† multiplies *x*⁰ + *x*³ by exp(*s*), *x*⁰ − *x*³ by exp(−*s*) and leaves *x*¹ ± i*x*² unchanged, which is the stated boost.

(iv) By (iii) the image of Λ contains the rotations about the three axes. By Lemma II.6.35(i) every rotation is a product of such. By (iii) the image contains the boosts along the third axis, with every value of tanh *s* between −1 and 1, and hence, conjugating by a rotation, the boosts along the first axis. By Lemma II.6.35(ii) every element of SO⁺(1,3) is a product of a rotation, a boost along the first axis and a rotation. So Λ maps SL(2, ℂ) onto SO⁺(1,3), and by (i) and (ii) the matrices Λ(*A*) and Λ(*B*) are equal exactly when *B*⁻¹*A* is 1 or −1. (v) The composition of *x* → Λ(*A*)*x* + *a* after *x* → Λ(*B*)*x* + *b* is *x* → Λ(*AB*)*x* + Λ(*A*)*b* + *a*, by (i); the inverse of (*a*, *A*) is (−Λ(*A*⁻¹)*a*, *A*⁻¹). The element (*y* − *x*, 1) carries *x* to *y*, and (*a*, *A*) fixes 0 exactly when *a* = 0. The last statement is (iv). □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of Theorem II.6.38.** In the matrices of Theorem II.8.5.3, *γ*¹*γ*² consists of two blocks −iσ₃, so the rotation *R*(*θ*) = cos(*θ*/2) + *γ*¹*γ*² sin(*θ*/2) of Theorem II.8.3.7 consists of two blocks cos(*θ*/2) − i sin(*θ*/2)σ₃, the matrix *A* of part (iii) for the third axis. The two-to-one covering of part (iv) is the fact of Theorem II.8.3.7 that a turn by 2π multiplies the amplitude by −1 and a turn by 4π restores it: *A* = −1 for *θ* = 2π, and Λ(−1) = 1. The matrix *M*(*x*) holds the interval as its determinant, so the group of the spin pair is fixed by the interval of §II.9.1, with the i of the Pauli matrix σ₂ the i of *x*₄ = i*ct*.

**Theorem II.6.39 (The conformal Klein pair of the null cone).** Let ℝ⁶ consist of the triples *ξ* = (*y*, *κ*, *ν*) with *y* in ℝ⁴ and *κ*, *ν* real, with the symmetric form ⟨*ξ*, *ξ*′⟩ = *η*(*y*, *y*′) − ½(*κν*′ + *κ*′*ν*), so that ⟨*ξ*, *ξ*⟩ = *η*(*y*, *y*) − *κν*. Let O(2,4) be the group of the linear maps *g* of ℝ⁶ with ⟨*gξ*, *gξ*′⟩ = ⟨*ξ*, *ξ*′⟩, and let *X* be the set of null lines, the sets of the multiples of a *ξ* ≠ 0 with ⟨*ξ*, *ξ*⟩ = 0. For an event *x* put *j*(*x*) = (*x*, 1, *η*(*x*, *x*)). (i) The form has two negative and four positive squares. *j*(*x*) is null, the null lines with *κ* ≠ 0 are exactly the lines of the *j*(*x*), each for one *x* = *y*/*κ*, and ⟨*j*(*x*), *j*(*x*′)⟩ = −½*η*(*x* − *x*′, *x* − *x*′). Two events have interval zero exactly when their null lines are orthogonal. (ii) O(2,4) acts on *X* and preserves orthogonality of null lines. It contains maps that act on the events through *j* as the translations *x* → *x* + *a*, the Lorentz transformations *x* → Λ*x*, the dilations *x* → *λx* with *λ* > 0, and the inversion *x* → *x*/*η*(*x*, *x*), defined for *η*(*x*, *x*) ≠ 0. (iii) O(2,4) carries every null line to every other. With *H* the subgroup fixing the line of *j*(0), (O(2,4), *H*) is a Klein pair with space *X*; *H* contains the Lorentz transformations, the dilations and the maps obtained by performing the inversion, a translation and the inversion. (iv) For *g* in O(2,4) and an event *x*, write *gj*(*x*) = (*y*, *κ*, *ν*) and, when *κ* ≠ 0, *g*·*x* = *y*/*κ* and *κ*_g(*x*) = *κ*. Then for events *x*, *x*′ with *κ*_g(*x*), *κ*_g(*x*′) ≠ 0,

*η*(*g*·*x* − *g*·*x*′, *g*·*x* − *g*·*x*′) = *η*(*x* − *x*′, *x* − *x*′)/(*κ*_g(*x*) *κ*_g(*x*′)).

(v) Consequently *x* → *g*·*x* carries events with interval zero to events with interval zero, and its derivative Δ at *x* satisfies *η*(Δ*h*, Δ*h*) = *η*(*h*, *h*)/*κ*_g(*x*)² for every *h*: it multiplies the interval of neighbouring events by a positive factor. (vi) If *ψ* satisfies (□ − *μ*²)*ψ* = 0, then *ψ*(*x*/*λ*) satisfies the same equation with *μ*/*λ* in place of *μ*; the dilations preserve the equation exactly when *μ* = 0.

*Proof.* (i) −*κν* = ((*κ* − *ν*)/2)² − ((*κ* + *ν*)/2)², so with the one negative and three positive squares of *η* the form has two negative and four positive squares. ⟨*j*(*x*), *j*(*x*)⟩ = *η*(*x*, *x*) − *η*(*x*, *x*) = 0. A null *ξ* with *κ* ≠ 0 has *ν* = *η*(*y*, *y*)/*κ*, so *ξ* = *κ* *j*(*y*/*κ*), and *j*(*x*), *j*(*x*′) are proportional only for *x* = *x*′, their components *κ* both being 1. Finally ⟨*j*(*x*), *j*(*x*′)⟩ = *η*(*x*, *x*′) − ½(*η*(*x*′, *x*′) + *η*(*x*, *x*)) = −½*η*(*x* − *x*′, *x* − *x*′). (ii) A linear map preserving the form carries null vectors to null vectors, lines to lines and orthogonal vectors to orthogonal vectors. The following maps are linear and preserve ⟨*ξ*, *ξ*⟩, hence the form. Translation: (*y*, *κ*, *ν*) → (*y* + *κa*, *κ*, *ν* + 2*η*(*a*, *y*) + *κη*(*a*, *a*)), for which *η*(*y* + *κa*, *y* + *κa*) − *κ*(*ν* + 2*η*(*a*, *y*) + *κη*(*a*, *a*)) = *η*(*y*, *y*) − *κν*, and which sends *j*(*x*) to *j*(*x* + *a*). Lorentz transformation: (*y*, *κ*, *ν*) → (Λ*y*, *κ*, *ν*), which sends *j*(*x*) to *j*(Λ*x*). Dilation: (*y*, *κ*, *ν*) → (*y*, *κ*/*λ*, *λν*), which sends *j*(*x*) to (*x*, 1/*λ*, *λη*(*x*, *x*)) = (1/*λ*) *j*(*λx*). Inversion: (*y*, *κ*, *ν*) → (*y*, *ν*, *κ*), which sends *j*(*x*) to (*x*, *η*(*x*, *x*), 1) = *η*(*x*, *x*) *j*(*x*/*η*(*x*, *x*)) when *η*(*x*, *x*) ≠ 0, because *η*(*x*/*q*, *x*/*q*) = 1/*q* for *q* = *η*(*x*, *x*).

(iii) Let *ξ*, *ζ* be null vectors with ⟨*ξ*, *ζ*⟩ ≠ 0 and put *w* = *ξ* − *ζ*; then ⟨*w*, *w*⟩ = −2⟨*ξ*, *ζ*⟩ ≠ 0, and the reflection *r*(*θ*) = *θ* − 2⟨*w*, *θ*⟩*w*/⟨*w*, *w*⟩ is linear, preserves the form, and sends *ξ* to *ξ* − *w* = *ζ*, since ⟨*w*, *ξ*⟩ = −⟨*ζ*, *ξ*⟩ = ½⟨*w*, *w*⟩. Let *ζ* = (*z*, *κ*, *ν*) be any null vector other than 0. If *κ* ≠ 0 its line is that of *j*(*z*/*κ*). If *κ* = 0, then ⟨*j*(*x*), *ζ*⟩ = *η*(*x*, *z*) − ½*ν*, and this is not zero for a suitable *x*: for *x* = 0 if *ν* ≠ 0, and otherwise *z* ≠ 0 and *x* can be chosen with *η*(*x*, *z*) ≠ 0. The reflection just described then carries the line of *ζ* to the line of *j*(*x*). The translation by −*x* carries the line of *j*(*x*) to that of *j*(0). So every null line is carried to the line of *j*(0), and with inverses every null line to every other; by Definition II.6.32 this is a Klein pair. The Lorentz transformations and the dilations send *j*(0) = (0, 1, 0) to a multiple of itself. The inversion exchanges the lines of (0, 1, 0) and (0, 0, 1), and a translation fixes (0, 0, 1); so inversion, translation, inversion fixes the line of *j*(0).

(iv) By the definitions *gj*(*x*) = *κ*_g(*x*) *j*(*g*·*x*), since a null vector with *κ* ≠ 0 is *κ* times *j*(*y*/*κ*). Hence, by (i) twice, −½*η*(*x* − *x*′, *x* − *x*′) = ⟨*j*(*x*), *j*(*x*′)⟩ = ⟨*gj*(*x*), *gj*(*x*′)⟩ = *κ*_g(*x*)*κ*_g(*x*′) ⟨*j*(*g*·*x*), *j*(*g*·*x*′)⟩ = −½*κ*_g(*x*)*κ*_g(*x*′) *η*(*g*·*x* − *g*·*x*′, *g*·*x* − *g*·*x*′). (v) The first statement is (iv). The components of *gj*(*x*) are polynomials in *x*, so *g*·*x* is a quotient of polynomials, differentiable where *κ*_g(*x*) ≠ 0. Put *x*′ = *x* + *εh* in (iv), divide by *ε*² and let *ε* → 0: the left side tends to *η*(Δ*h*, Δ*h*) and the right side to *η*(*h*, *h*)/*κ*_g(*x*)². (vi) Each derivative of *ψ*(*x*/*λ*) is 1/*λ* times the derivative of *ψ* at *x*/*λ*, so □ applied to *ψ*(*x*/*λ*) is *λ*⁻² times (□*ψ*)(*x*/*λ*) = *λ*⁻²*μ*²*ψ*(*x*/*λ*). The equation is the same for every *λ* exactly when *μ*²/*λ*² = *μ*² for every *λ* > 0, that is, when *μ* = 0. □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of Theorem II.6.39.** The null cone of an event *p* is the set of events with interval zero from *p*; its section at the time *t* is the McGucken Sphere of *p* of radius *c*|*t* − *t*_p| (Theorem II.8.1.9). The invariant of this geometry is the relation of interval zero, that is, the family of all McGucken Spheres of all events, as the interval itself is the invariant of Theorem II.6.28; part (iv) shows that O(2,4) preserves it and part (v) that the interval is multiplied by a positive factor. Part (vi) states the range of the pair: the Compton frequency *ω*_C = *mc*²/ℏ of a body of rest mass *m* fixes a scale, so the group of a massive body is that of Theorem II.6.28, and the larger group belongs to the wavefront of the McGucken Sphere itself, the case *μ* = 0. The six-dimensional construction is that of Dirac [139]. That every map of a region of spacetime with the property of part (v) is one of the maps *x* → *g*·*x* is Liouville’s theorem, proved below as Theorem II.6.41; Corollary II.6.42 then determines the subgroup *H*.

**Lemma II.6.40 (Linear maps that preserve the null cone).** Let *M* be a real 4 × 4 matrix with *η*(*Mh*, *Mh*) = 0 for every *h* with *η*(*h*, *h*) = 0. Then *M*ᵀ*ηM* = *bη* with a real number *b*, and *b* > 0 if *M* is invertible.

*Proof.* Let *B* = *M*ᵀ*ηM*, a symmetric matrix with *h*ᵀ*Bh* = 0 for every null *h*. Let **e**₀, …, **e**₃ be the unit vectors of the axes. The vectors **e**₀ + **e**_j and **e**₀ − **e**_j are null, which gives *B*₀₀ + *B*_jj + 2*B*_0j = 0 and *B*₀₀ + *B*_jj − 2*B*_0j = 0; hence *B*_0j = 0 and *B*_jj = −*B*₀₀. For *j* ≠ *k* the vector **e**₀ + (**e**_j + **e**_k)/2^(1/2) is null, which gives *B*₀₀ + ½(*B*_jj + *B*_kk) + *B*_jk = 0, hence *B*_jk = 0. So *B* = −*B*₀₀*η*. If *M* is invertible, *B* is invertible, so *b* ≠ 0, and by Sylvester’s law of inertia *B* has one negative and three positive squares, as *η* has; *bη* with *b* < 0 has three negative squares. □

**Theorem II.6.41 (Liouville’s theorem for spacetime: O(2,4) is the largest group of the null cone).** Let *E* be a connected open region of spacetime and *f* a map of *E* into spacetime, four times continuously differentiable, whose derivative at every point is invertible and carries null vectors to null vectors. Then there is an element *g* of O(2,4) with *κ*_g(*x*) ≠ 0 on *E* and *f*(*x*) = *g*·*x* for all *x* in *E*, in the notation of Theorem II.6.39(iv).

*Proof.* Write *f*_i = ∂_i*f*, *f*_ij = ∂_i∂_j*f*, and ⟨*u*, *v*⟩ = *η*(*u*, *v*). Step 1. By Lemma II.6.40, ⟨*f*_i, *f*_j⟩ = *λ*²*η*_ij with a function *λ* > 0, and *λ*² = ⟨*f*₁, *f*₁⟩ is three times continuously differentiable. Put *σ* = ln *λ*, *σ*_i = ∂_i*σ*, *σ*_ij = ∂_i∂_j*σ*, *σ*^k = *η*^kl*σ*_l and *s* = *σ*^k*σ*_k. Differentiating ⟨*f*_i, *f*_j⟩ = *λ*²*η*_ij with respect to *x*^k gives ⟨*f*_ik, *f*_j⟩ + ⟨*f*_i, *f*_jk⟩ = 2*λ*²*σ*_k*η*_ij. Writing this for the three cyclic orders of *i*, *j*, *k*, adding two and subtracting the third, ⟨*f*_ij, *f*_k⟩ = *λ*²(*σ*_i*η*_jk + *σ*_j*η*_ik − *σ*_k*η*_ij). The vectors *f*₀, …, *f*₃ are a basis with ⟨*f*_k, *f*_l⟩ = *λ*²*η*_kl, so

*f*_ij = *σ*_i*f*_j + *σ*_j*f*_i − *η*_ij*σ*^k*f*_k. (L)

Step 2. Differentiate (L) with respect to *x*^k and replace the second derivatives of *f* on the right by (L). The term −*η*_ij*σ*^l*f*_lk becomes −*η*_ij(*s* *f*_k + *σ*_k*σ*^l*f*_l − *σ*_k*σ*^l*f*_l) = −*η*_ij *s* *f*_k, and the result is *f*_ijk = *σ*_ik*f*_j + *σ*_jk*f*_i − *η*_ij*σ*^l_k*f*_l + 2*σ*_i*σ*_j*f*_k + *σ*_i*σ*_k*f*_j + *σ*_j*σ*_k*f*_i − (*σ*_i*η*_jk + *σ*_j*η*_ik)*σ*^l*f*_l − *η*_ij *s* *f*_k, where *σ*^l_k = *η*^lm*σ*_mk. The left side is unchanged when *j* and *k* are exchanged. Subtracting the expression with *j*, *k* exchanged, and writing *S*_ij = *σ*_ij − *σ*_i*σ*_j and *S*^l_k = *η*^lm*S*_mk, gives (*S*_ik + *η*_ik *s*)*f*_j − (*S*_ij + *η*_ij *s*)*f*_k − *η*_ij*S*^l_k*f*_l + *η*_ik*S*^l_j*f*_l = 0. Since the *f*_l are a basis, the coefficient of each *f*_m vanishes: (*S*_ik + *η*_ik *s*)δ^m_j − (*S*_ij + *η*_ij *s*)δ^m_k − *η*_ij*S*^m_k + *η*_ik*S*^m_j = 0. Setting *m* = *j* and summing over *j* from 0 to 3, with *S*^j_j = *τ*: 4(*S*_ik + *η*_ik *s*) − (*S*_ik + *η*_ik *s*) − *S*_ik + *η*_ik*τ* = 0, that is, 2*S*_ik + 3*η*_ik *s* + *η*_ik*τ* = 0. Contracting with *η*^ik: 2*τ* + 12*s* + 4*τ* = 0, so *τ* = −2*s*, and therefore *S*_ik = −½ *s* *η*_ik.

Step 3. Let *ρ* = 1/*λ* = exp(−*σ*). Then ∂_i*ρ* = −*ρσ*_i and ∂_i∂_j*ρ* = *ρ*(*σ*_i*σ*_j − *σ*_ij) = −*ρS*_ij = *κη*_ij with *κ* = ½*ρs* = *η*^kl∂_k*ρ*∂_l*ρ*/(2*ρ*). Since *ρ* is three times continuously differentiable, ∂_k∂_i∂_j*ρ* = (∂_k*κ*)*η*_ij is unchanged when *j* and *k* are exchanged; taking *i* = *j* ≠ *k* gives ∂_k*κ* = 0. So *κ* is constant on the connected region *E*, the second derivatives of *ρ* are constant, and *ρ* is a polynomial of the second degree on *E*, determined by the values at one point of *ρ* and of its first derivatives, *κ* being fixed by these.

Step 4, uniqueness. Let *f* and *h* both satisfy the hypotheses on *E*, and at a point *p* let *f*(*p*) = *h*(*p*), let the derivatives of *f* and *h* be equal, and let the first derivatives of their functions *ρ* be equal. The values of *ρ* at *p* are equal, since *λ*² = ⟨*f*₁, *f*₁⟩. By Step 3 the two functions *ρ* are the same polynomial, so the functions *σ* are the same. Along a continuously differentiable path *x*(*τ*) from *p*, by (L) the four vectors *f*_j(*x*(*τ*)) satisfy the linear system of ordinary differential equations *df*_j/*dτ* = (*dx*^i/*dτ*)(*σ*_i*f*_j + *σ*_j*f*_i − *η*_ij*σ*^k*f*_k), and the *h*_j satisfy the same system with the same initial values. By the uniqueness of the solution of a linear system, *f*_j = *h*_j along the path. Every point of *E* is joined to *p* by such a path, so *f* − *h* has zero derivative on *E* and vanishes at *p*: *f* = *h*.

Step 5, existence. For elements of O(2,4), *g*₁*g*₂*j*(*x*) = *κ* *g*₁*j*(*g*₂·*x*), where *κ* is the number *κ*_g(*x*) belonging to the factor *g*₂, so (*g*₁*g*₂)·*x* = *g*₁·(*g*₂·*x*) and the functions *κ* multiply. By Theorem II.6.39(v) the map *x* → *g*·*x* satisfies the hypotheses where *κ*_g ≠ 0, with *ρ* = |*κ*_g|. Let *C*_b be the element inversion, translation by *b*, inversion of Theorem II.6.39(iii). It sends *j*(*x*) = (*x*, 1, *q*), *q* = *η*(*x*, *x*), successively to (*x*, *q*, 1), to (*x* + *qb*, *q*, 1 + 2*η*(*b*, *x*) + *qη*(*b*, *b*)) and to (*x* + *qb*, *P*(*x*), *q*) with *P*(*x*) = 1 + 2*η*(*b*, *x*) + *η*(*b*, *b*)*η*(*x*, *x*). So *C*_b·*x* = (*x* + *qb*)/*P*(*x*): it fixes 0, its derivative at 0 is the identity, and its *ρ* is *P* near 0, with first derivatives 2*η*_ik*b*^k at 0. Now fix *p* in *E*. The derivative *M* of *f* at *p* satisfies *M*ᵀ*ηM* = *λ*₀²*η* with *λ*₀ = *λ*(*p*), so Λ₀ = *M*/*λ*₀ preserves *η*. Let *b*^k = ½*λ*₀*η*^kl∂_l*ρ*(*p*), and let *g* be the product of the translation by −*p*, then *C*_b, then the Lorentz transformation Λ₀, then the dilation by *λ*₀, then the translation by *f*(*p*), all of Theorem II.6.39(ii). Its map is *h*(*x*) = *f*(*p*) + *λ*₀Λ₀ *C*_b·(*x* − *p*), and *κ*_g(*x*) = *P*(*x* − *p*)/*λ*₀, the dilation contributing the factor 1/*λ*₀. At *p*: *h*(*p*) = *f*(*p*), the derivative of *h* is *λ*₀Λ₀ = *M*, and the function *P*(*x* − *p*)/*λ*₀ has the value 1/*λ*₀ = *ρ*(*p*) and the first derivatives 2*η*_ik*b*^k/*λ*₀ = ∂_i*ρ*(*p*). This function is a polynomial of the second degree with second derivatives (2*η*(*b*, *b*)/*λ*₀)*η*_ij, and 2*η*(*b*, *b*)/*λ*₀ is the value of *η*^kl∂_k*ρ*∂_l*ρ*/(2*ρ*) at *p*, which is *κ*. By Step 3 it coincides with *ρ* on *E*. Hence *κ*_g = *ρ* > 0 on *E*, the map *h* is defined on all of *E* and satisfies the hypotheses there with the same *ρ*, and by Step 4, *f* = *h* on *E*. □

**Corollary II.6.42 (The kernel of the action and the subgroup fixing an event).** (i) An element of O(2,4) that fixes the null line of *j*(*x*) for every *x* in a non-empty open set is 1 or −1. Hence two elements of O(2,4) give the same map *x* → *g*·*x* on an open set exactly when they are equal or opposite, and the element *g* of Theorem II.6.41 is determined up to sign. (ii) The subgroup *H* of Theorem II.6.39(iii) consists of the elements ±*D*_λ*L*_Λ*C*_b, with *D*_λ a dilation, *λ* > 0, *L*_Λ a Lorentz transformation, Λᵀ*η*Λ = *η*, and *C*_b as in Step 5; *λ*, Λ and *b* are determined by the element. (iii) The group of the transformations of regions of spacetime that carry null directions to null directions is therefore the group of the maps *x* → *g*·*x*, which is O(2,4) with *g* and −*g* identified.

*Proof.* (i) Let *gj*(*x*) = *a*(*x*)*j*(*x*) for *x* in the open set *W*. For *x*, *x*′ in *W*, ⟨*gj*(*x*), *gj*(*x*′)⟩ = *a*(*x*)*a*(*x*′)⟨*j*(*x*), *j*(*x*′)⟩, so *a*(*x*)*a*(*x*′) = 1 whenever the interval of *x*, *x*′ is not zero (Theorem II.6.39(i)). The events with interval zero from a given event form the zero set of a polynomial that is not identically zero, so for *x*, *x*′ in *W* there is *x*″ in *W* with non-zero interval from both. From *a*(*x*)*a*(*x*″) = 1 and *a*(*x*′)*a*(*x*″) = 1 follows *a*(*x*) = *a*(*x*′); so *a* is constant on *W*, and its square is 1. A linear form on ℝ⁶ vanishing on all *j*(*x*), *x* in *W*, is a polynomial *η*(*α*, *x*) + *β* + *γη*(*x*, *x*) vanishing on an open set, so it is zero: the *j*(*x*) span ℝ⁶, and *g* = *a*. The second statement follows by applying this to the product of one element with the inverse of the other. (ii) Each of *D*_λ, *L*_Λ, *C*_b fixes the line of *j*(0) (Theorem II.6.39(iii)). Conversely let *g* be in *H*. Then *gj*(0) is a non-zero multiple of *j*(0), so *κ*_g(0) ≠ 0, and *x* → *g*·*x* fixes 0 and satisfies the hypotheses of Theorem II.6.41 on a ball about 0. By Step 5 of its proof with *p* = 0 and *f*(*p*) = 0, this map is that of *D*_λ*L*_Λ*C*_b for *λ* and Λ given by its derivative at 0 and *b* by the first derivatives of its *ρ* at 0, and by (i) *g* is this element or its opposite. (iii) is Theorem II.6.41 with (i). □

**Theorem II.6.43 (The de Sitter and anti-de Sitter Klein pairs).** (i) Let a real vector space carry a symmetric form ⟨ , ⟩, let *G* be the group of its linear maps preserving the form, and let *ξ*, *ζ* be vectors with ⟨*ξ*, *ξ*⟩ = ⟨*ζ*, *ζ*⟩ = *N* ≠ 0. Then some element of *G* carries *ξ* to *ζ*. (ii) On ℝ⁵ with the form of one negative and four positive squares, the group O(1,4) carries every point of the set *Y* of the vectors with ⟨*ξ*, *ξ*⟩ = *c*²/*H*² to every other, and the subgroup fixing a point is the group O(1,3) of its orthogonal complement. The map sending (*t*, *r*, **n**), with **n** a unit vector of ℝ³ and *r* < *c*/*H*, to the vector with the components *w* sinh *Ht*, *r***n**, *w* cosh *Ht*, where *w* = (*c*²/*H*² − *r*²)^(1/2), has its values in *Y*, and the form induces on them the metric of Theorem II.8.6.17(ii), the solution of the vacuum field equations with Λ = 3*H*²/*c*². Hence (O(1,4), O(1,3)) is a Klein pair whose space *Y* contains that solution, and each element of O(1,4) preserves the metric. (iii) On ℝ⁵ with the form of two negative and three positive squares, the same holds for O(2,3) and the set *Z* of the vectors with ⟨*ξ*, *ξ*⟩ = −*L*²: the subgroup fixing a point is O(1,3), and the map sending (*t*, *r*, **n**) to the vector with the components *w* sin(*ct*/*L*), *w* cos(*ct*/*L*), *r***n**, where *w* = (*L*² + *r*²)^(1/2), has its values in *Z* and induces the metric −*F* *c*²*dt*² + *dr*²/*F* + *r*²(*dθ*² + sin²*θ* *dφ*²) with *F* = 1 + *r*²/*L*², which satisfies the vacuum field equations with Λ = −3/*L*².

*Proof.* (i) For a vector *w* with ⟨*w*, *w*⟩ ≠ 0 the reflection *θ* → *θ* − 2⟨*w*, *θ*⟩*w*/⟨*w*, *w*⟩ is linear and preserves the form. Since ⟨*ξ* − *ζ*, *ξ* − *ζ*⟩ + ⟨*ξ* + *ζ*, *ξ* + *ζ*⟩ = 4*N* ≠ 0, one of the two terms is not zero. If the first, take *w* = *ξ* − *ζ*: then ⟨*w*, *ξ*⟩ = *N* − ⟨*ζ*, *ξ*⟩ = ½⟨*w*, *w*⟩ and the reflection sends *ξ* to *ξ* − *w* = *ζ*. If the second, take *w* = *ξ* + *ζ*: then ⟨*w*, *ξ*⟩ = ½⟨*w*, *w*⟩, the reflection sends *ξ* to −*ζ*, and the map −1 sends −*ζ* to *ζ*. (ii) The first statement is (i). An element fixing *ξ*₀ maps the orthogonal complement *E* of *ξ*₀ onto itself, and every vector is a multiple of *ξ*₀ plus a vector of *E*, since ⟨*ξ*₀, *ξ*₀⟩ ≠ 0; so the element is determined by its action on *E*, which may be any linear map of *E* preserving the form. As ⟨*ξ*₀, *ξ*₀⟩ > 0, the form on *E* has one negative and three positive squares. For the map: −(*w* sinh *Ht*)² + *r*² + (*w* cosh *Ht*)² = *w*² + *r*² = *c*²/*H*².
