Gauge covariantization *D*_μ → ∇_μ = ∂_μ + *A*_μ yields the gauge connection, in the convention of [87] in which the connection one-form *A*_μ absorbs the factor i*q*/ℏ of §II.9.4; the gauge-covariant McGucken operator is D_M^(A) = ∇_t + i*c*∇₄ = McGucken Operator D_M + *A_M*, with *A_M* = *A*_t + i*cA*₄ the connection component along the fourth-dimensional flow (Definition 12.1 of [87]). Each of these—the Schrödinger operator, the d’Alembertian, the Dirac operator, the gauge covariant derivative—is a downstream descendant of the McGucken Operator D_M, which is itself co-generated with the McGucken Space ℳ_G from the McGucken Axiom *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*. The operator hierarchy as a theorem chain—and the symmetry derivations on which it rests—are developed in McGucken [87, 74, 87, 91].

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* Reading of the Operator Hierarchy: The Quantized McGucken Operator, the d’Alembertian, the Dirac Factorization and the Gauge-Covariant McGucken Operator, with Proofs.**

**McGucken Principle anchor.** The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* states that the fourth dimension is expanding at the velocity of light in a spherically symmetric manner from every spacetime event, as a McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action *h*. For the operators of quantum theory the Principle predicts that each is obtained from the McGucken Operator D_M = ∂ₜ + i*c*∂₄, the derivative along the expansion: the energy operator by multiplying by iℏ, the wave operator by setting *x*₄ = i*ct* in the four-coordinate Laplacian, the Dirac operator as a first-order factor of the Klein–Gordon operator, and the gauge-covariant operator by requiring covariance under a change of phase from event to event. The statements follow Definitions 7.1, 8.1, 11.1 and 12.1 and Theorems 8.2 and 11.2 of [87]; the proofs are carried out here in full.

**Theorem II.8.5.1 (Quantized McGucken Operator; Definition 7.1 of [87]).** Put *Ĥ* = iℏ∂ₜ, *p̂*₄ = −iℏ∂₄ and *M̂* = iℏD_M. Then (i) *M̂* = *Ĥ* − i*c* *p̂*₄; (ii) for Ψ(*t*, *x*₄) differentiable in *t* and holomorphic in *x*₄, the three statements *M̂*Ψ = 0, *Ĥ*Ψ = i*c* *p̂*₄Ψ, and “Ψ is a function of *x*₄ − i*ct* alone” are equivalent.

*Proof.* (i) iℏ(∂ₜ + i*c*∂₄) = iℏ∂ₜ − *c*ℏ∂₄, and −i*c* *p̂*₄ = −i*c*(−iℏ∂₄) = −*c*ℏ∂₄. (ii) By (i), *M̂*Ψ = 0 is the same equation as *Ĥ*Ψ = i*c* *p̂*₄Ψ, and since iℏ ≠ 0 it is the same as the McGucken Operator D_MΨ = 0, which by Lemma II.6.10 holds exactly for the functions of *x*₄ − i*ct*. □

**Theorem II.8.5.2 (The d’Alembertian from the four-coordinate Laplacian; Definition 8.1 and Theorem 8.2 of [87]; QM Theorem 1 of [72]).** Let *G*(**x**, *x*₄) be twice differentiable in **x** and holomorphic in *x*₄, and let *g*(*t*, **x**) = *G*(**x**, i*ct*) be its values on the constraint surface. Then (∂₄²*G* + ∇²*G*)(**x**, i*ct*) = ∇²*g* − *c*⁻²∂ₜ²*g* = □*g*.

*Proof.* By the chain rule, ∂ₜ*g* = i*c* (∂₄*G*)(**x**, i*ct*), and again ∂ₜ²*g* = (i*c*)² (∂₄²*G*)(**x**, i*ct*) = −*c*² (∂₄²*G*)(**x**, i*ct*). Hence (∂₄²*G*)(**x**, i*ct*) = −*c*⁻²∂ₜ²*g*, and the spatial derivatives of *G* at *x*₄ = i*ct* are those of *g*. □

**Theorem II.8.5.3 (Dirac factorization of the Klein–Gordon operator; Definition 11.1 and Theorem 11.2 of [87]).** Write ∂₀ = *c*⁻¹∂ₜ, *η* = diag(−1, 1, 1, 1), so that □ = *η*^μν ∂_μ ∂_ν, and let *γ*⁰, *γ*¹, *γ*², *γ*³ be 4 × 4 matrices with *γ*^μ*γ*^ν + *γ*^ν*γ*^μ = −2*η*^μν. (i) Such matrices exist. (ii) (iℏ*γ*^μ∂_μ − *mc*)(iℏ*γ*^ν∂_ν + *mc*) = ℏ²(□ − *μ*²), with *μ* = *mc*/ℏ. (iii) Every solution *ψ* of the Dirac equation (iℏ*γ*^ν∂_ν + *mc*)*ψ* = 0, and every solution of (iℏ*γ*^μ∂_μ − *mc*)*ψ* = 0, has each of its four components satisfying the Klein–Gordon equation (□ − *μ*²)*ψ* = 0 of Theorem II.8.3.3.

*Proof.* (i) With *σ*¹, *σ*², *σ*³ the Pauli matrices, which satisfy *σ*^j*σ*^k + *σ*^k*σ*^j = 2δ_jk, take in 2 × 2 blocks *γ*⁰ = [[1, 0], [0, −1]] and *γ*^j = [[0, *σ*^j], [−*σ*^j, 0]]. Then (*γ*⁰)² = 1 = −*η*⁰⁰; *γ*^j*γ*^k = [[−*σ*^j*σ*^k, 0], [0, −*σ*^j*σ*^k]], so *γ*^j*γ*^k + *γ*^k*γ*^j = −2δ_jk = −2*η*^jk; and *γ*⁰*γ*^j = [[0, *σ*^j], [*σ*^j, 0]] = −*γ*^j*γ*⁰, so *γ*⁰*γ*^j + *γ*^j*γ*⁰ = 0 = −2*η*^0j. (ii) The matrices are constant, so the cross terms iℏ*mc* *γ*^μ∂_μ − *mc* iℏ*γ*^ν∂_ν cancel and the product is −ℏ²*γ*^μ*γ*^ν∂_μ∂_ν − *m*²*c*². Because ∂_μ∂_ν is symmetric, *γ*^μ*γ*^ν∂_μ∂_ν = ½(*γ*^μ*γ*^ν + *γ*^ν*γ*^μ)∂_μ∂_ν = −*η*^μν∂_μ∂_ν = −□. The product is ℏ²□ − *m*²*c*² = ℏ²(□ − *μ*²). (iii) Apply the other factor to the equation and use (ii), the two factors commuting. □

The sign in the relation *γ*^μ*γ*^ν + *γ*^ν*γ*^μ = −2*η*^μν matters. With +2*η*^μν in its place the same computation gives the product −ℏ²(□ + *μ*²), and the equation (□ + *μ*²)*ψ* = 0 has plane-wave solutions with *ω*² = *c*²|**k**|² − *ω*_C², which is the relation of Theorem II.8.3.2(iii) with the sign of the mass term reversed. The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* gives *ω*² = *c*²|**k**|² + *ω*_C², for which the group velocity *dω*/*d*|**k**| = *c*²|**k**|/*ω* is less than *c*; the relation −2*η*^μν is the one consistent with it.

**Theorem II.8.5.4 (Gauge-covariant McGucken Operator; Definition 12.1 of [87]).** Let *A*_t and *A*₄ be functions, put ∇_t = ∂ₜ + *A*_t, ∇₄ = ∂₄ + *A*₄ and D_M^(A) = ∇_t + i*c*∇₄. Then (i) D_M^(A) = McGucken Operator D_M + *A_M* with *A_M* = *A*_t + i*cA*₄; (ii) if Ψ is replaced by exp(i*α*)Ψ for a function *α*, and *A*_t, *A*₄ by *A*_t − i∂ₜ*α*, *A*₄ − i∂₄*α*, then D_M^(A)Ψ is replaced by exp(i*α*) D_M^(A)Ψ.

*Proof.* (i) ∂ₜ + *A*_t + i*c*(∂₄ + *A*₄) = (∂ₜ + i*c*∂₄) + (*A*_t + i*cA*₄). (ii) By the product rule, ∂ₜ(exp(i*α*)Ψ) = exp(i*α*)(∂ₜΨ + i(∂ₜ*α*)Ψ), so (∂ₜ + *A*_t − i∂ₜ*α*)(exp(i*α*)Ψ) = exp(i*α*)(∂ₜ + *A*_t)Ψ; the same holds with ∂₄ and *A*₄. Adding the first to i*c* times the second gives the statement. □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of the operator hierarchy.** The Schrödinger operator of Theorem II.8.3.4, the d’Alembertian of Theorem II.8.5.2, the Dirac operator of Theorem II.8.5.3 and the gauge-covariant operator of Theorem II.8.5.4 are each obtained from the McGucken Operator D_M = ∂ₜ + i*c*∂₄, which Theorem II.4.1 obtains from *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* as the derivative along the expansion of *x*₄.

**II.8.6 Thermodynamics, the Five Arrows of Time, and Black-Hole Entropy.** Thermodynamics, traditionally axiomatized independently of mechanics, is on the McGucken *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* framework a deductive consequence of the same source-law that generated the McGucken Sphere Σ_M(τ) and the McGucken Operator D_M. McGucken (2026e) presents the full chain as eighteen theorems in four groups; we organize them here by their position in the operator-space hierarchy.

**(a) Foundations on the McGucken Sphere Σ_M(τ) (Th 1–6).** The wave equation (1/*c*²)∂²*ψ*/∂*t*² − ∇²*ψ* = 0 follows (Th 1) by applying Huygens’ principle to the spherical expansion *R*(*t*) = *ct* driven by *x*₄. The spatial isometry group *ISO*(3) = *SO*(3) ⋉ **R**³ is the algebraic content (the *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* Algebraic Channel, Th 2); the Huygens-wavefront propagation on the McGucken Sphere Σ_M(τ) is the geometric content (the *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* Geometric Channel, Th 3). Compton coupling ω_C = *mc*²/ℏ (Th 4) connects rest mass to *x*₄-cycle rate. The spatial projection of the *x*₄-driven displacement is instantaneously isotropic (Th 5), and iterated isotropic displacements yield Brownian motion with Wiener variance Var(**r**(*t*)) = 6*Dt* (Th 6). All six are direct consequences of the McGucken Operator D_M acting on the McGucken Sphere Σ_M(τ).

**(b) Closure of Einstein’s three statistical-mechanical gaps (Th 7–10).** Einstein’s 1903–1911 attempts to derive the second law from mechanics left three gaps: **T1**, the choice of probability measure was postulated rather than derived; **T2**, the ergodic hypothesis is generically false (KAM theorem, 1954–1963); **T3**, the second law was statistical rather than strict, requiring an auxiliary Past Hypothesis. The McGucken *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* framework closes each:

• **T1 closed by Th 7.** The rotation group SO(3) is compact, and its unique invariant probability measure is the normalized Haar measure (Haar 1933) [39]; the translation subgroup **R**³ of ISO(3) = SO(3) ⋉ **R**³ is non-compact and carries Lebesgue measure, invariant and unique up to scale. On the McGucken Sphere Σ_M(*τ*) the SO(3)-invariant probability measure is the normalized area measure (Lemma 7.1.5 of [114], reproduced in §II.9.3), the rotationally invariant spherical measure used to define ⟨*ψ*|*φ*⟩ in §II.8.4. Extending from the displacement sphere to the spatial slice by the orbit decomposition **R**³ = ⊔_{*r* ≥ 0} *S*²(*r*), with translation invariance fixing the radial Lebesgue weight, gives the Lebesgue measure *d*³*q*, and the same argument on momentum space gives the Liouville measure *d*Γ = Π_i *d*³*q*_i *d*³*p*_i ([80], Theorem 7, Step 3). The Liouville measure is no longer postulated; it is fixed uniquely by the symmetry generated by the McGucken Operator D_M.

• **T2 closed by Th 8.** For any continuous observable *F* on phase space, the time-average along any trajectory equals the ensemble-average over the McGucken Sphere’s wavefront cross-section, taken in the SO(3)-invariant measure on the McGucken Sphere Σ_M(*τ*) ([80], Theorem 8); ergodicity is a geometric identity, independent of metric transitivity and unaffected by KAM-tori obstruction. The equidistribution follows from the spatial-projection isotropy of Th 5 by Kolmogorov’s strong law of large numbers; Birkhoff’s 1931 theorem [9], which requires metric transitivity, is not used.

• **T3 closed by Th 9 and Th 10.** By Th 5 the spatial projection of the McGucken Operator D_M-driven *x*₄-displacement is isotropic; by Th 6 iterated isotropic displacements give a Gaussian density *ρ*(**r**, *t*) = (4*πDt*)^−3/2 exp(−|**r**|²/(4*Dt*)). The Boltzmann–Gibbs entropy *S*(*t*) = −*k*_*B* ∫ *ρ* ln *ρ* *d*³*r* evaluates to (3/2)*k*_*B*[1 + ln(4*πDt*)], and direct differentiation gives *dS*/*dt* = (3/2)*k*_*B*/*t* > 0 *strictly*, for all *t* > 0. The second law is no longer statistical (“overwhelmingly probable to increase”); it is a geometric identity following from the Gaussian spreading forced by the McGucken Operator D_M. Th 10 supplies the photonic analogue: for a spherical photon shell of radius *ct* the entropy is *S*(*t*) = *k*_*B* ln(4*π*(*ct*)²), *dS*/*dt* = 2*k*_*B*/*t* > 0.

It is worth contrasting this derivation with the parallel programme of rigorous hydrodynamic limits. Deng, Hani, and Ma (arXiv:2503.01800, 3 March 2025) proved that Newton’s equations for a hard-sphere gas converge, on the 2D and 3D torus, to solutions of the Boltzmann equation; combined with the Boltzmann-to-fluid theorems of Bardos–Golse–Levermore [124] and Saint-Raymond [125], this yields the compressible Euler and incompressible Navier–Stokes–Fourier equations as scaling limits of microscopic Newtonian dynamics. The Deng–Hani–Ma derivation and the McGucken derivation of Theorems 7–10 above are addressing different layers of Hilbert’s charge: the former derives macroscopic fluid laws from microscopic Newton on a fixed Euclidean arena, while the latter derives the Liouville measure (Th 7), ergodicity (Th 8), and the strict second law (Th 9) from the McGucken Operator D_M acting on the McGucken Sphere Σ_M(τ)—an arena that is itself co-generated with the dynamics. The two results are complementary, and together cover both branches of the kinetic-theory subgoal Hilbert listed in 1900.

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* Reading of Statistical Mechanics: The Isotropic Step, Brownian Motion, the Liouville Measure, Ergodicity of Directions, and the Strict Second Law for Matter and for Light, with Proofs.**

**McGucken Principle anchor.** The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* states that the fourth dimension is expanding at the velocity of light in a spherically symmetric manner from every spacetime event, as a McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action *h*. For statistical mechanics the Principle predicts the following: because the expansion is spherically symmetric from every event, a displacement driven by it has no preferred direction, the only probability measure it allows on directions is the uniform one, repeated displacements spread a distribution as a Gaussian of growing width, and the entropy of that distribution increases at every instant. The statements follow Theorems 5–10 of [80]; every proof is carried out here in full.

**Hypotheses on the step (Theorems 4 and 5 of [80]).** A body coupled to the expansion of *x*₄ changes its position by successive steps. (S1) Each step has a direction **n** on the unit sphere, distributed by a probability measure invariant under the rotations SO(3), because the McGucken Sphere of each event has no preferred direction. (S2) The directions of different steps are independent, each step starting from its own event. (S3) Each step has length *ℓ* and duration Δ*t*. (S1) is the spherical symmetry of the Principle. (S2) and (S3) are the coupling of matter to the expansion, which Theorem 4 of [80] introduces as the Compton-coupling ansatz; the constant *D* = *ℓ*²/(6Δ*t*) that results is the diffusion constant.

**Theorem II.8.6.1 (The isotropic step; Theorem 5 of [80]).** Under (S1) the direction **n** is distributed by the normalized area measure *d*Ω/4*π* on the unit sphere. Its mean is zero, ∫ *n*_i *d*Ω/4*π* = 0, and its second moments are ∫ *n*_i *n*_j *d*Ω/4*π* = δ_ij/3.

*Proof.* The rotation-invariant probability measure on a round sphere is unique and equals the normalized area measure (the lemma on the invariant measure of the McGucken Sphere in §II.9.3). The rotation by the angle *π* about the *x*_j-axis, *j* ≠ *i*, sends *n*_i to −*n*_i and preserves the measure, so the mean of *n*_i equals its own negative and is zero. For *i* ≠ *j* the same rotation about the *x*_j-axis sends *n*_i *n*_j to −*n*_i *n*_j, so that moment is zero. A rotation by *π*/2 exchanging two axes shows that the three moments of *n*₁², *n*₂², *n*₃² are equal, and their sum is the moment of *n*₁² + *n*₂² + *n*₃² = 1; each is 1/3. □

**Theorem II.8.6.2 (Brownian motion; Theorem 6 of [80]).** Under (S1)–(S3) let **r**_N be the position after *N* steps from the origin, at time *t* = *N*Δ*t*, and put *D* = *ℓ*²/(6Δ*t*). (i) Exactly, the mean of **r**_N is zero and the mean of |**r**_N|² is *Nℓ*² = 6*Dt*. (ii) The characteristic function of **r**_N, the mean of exp(−i**k**·**r**_N), is (sin(*ℓk*)/(*ℓk*))^N with *k* = |**k**|. (iii) As Δ*t* → 0 with *D* and *t* fixed, it converges for every **k** to exp(−*Dk*²*t*), so the distribution of the position converges to the Gaussian density *ρ*(**r**, *t*) = (4*πDt*)^(−3/2) exp(−|**r**|²/(4*Dt*)). (iv) This density satisfies the diffusion equation ∂ₜ*ρ* = *D* ∇²*ρ*.

*Proof.* (i) **r**_N = *ℓ*(**n**₁ + ⋯ + **n**(*N*)). By Theorem II.8.6.1 each **n**(*a*) has mean zero, so **r**_N has mean zero. Expanding |**r**_N|² = *ℓ*² Σ_a Σ_b **n**(*a*)·**n**(*b*): for *a* = *b* the term is 1; for *a* ≠ *b*, by the independence (S2), the mean of **n**(*a*)·**n**(*b*) is the product of the means, which is zero. Hence the mean of |**r**_N|² is *Nℓ*². (ii) By (S2) the mean of the product exp(−i*ℓ***k**·**n**₁) ⋯ exp(−i*ℓ***k**·**n**(*N*)) is the product of the means. For one step, with *θ* the angle between **k** and **n** and *u* = cos *θ*, ∫ exp(−i*ℓk* cos *θ*) *d*Ω/4*π* = ½ ∫ exp(−i*ℓku*) *du* over −1 ≤ *u* ≤ 1, which equals sin(*ℓk*)/(*ℓk*). (iii) Put *x* = *ℓk*, so *x*² = 6*D*Δ*t* *k*² → 0. For |*x*| ≤ 1, sin *x*/*x* = 1 − *x*²/6 + *R*(*x*) with |*R*(*x*)| ≤ *x*⁴/120, and ln(1 − *y*) = −*y* + *Q*(*y*) with |*Q*(*y*)| ≤ *y*² for 0 ≤ *y* ≤ ½. Hence *N* ln(sin *x*/*x*) = −*Nx*²/6 + *N* *E*(*x*) with |*E*(*x*)| ≤ *x*⁴. Now *Nx*²/6 = (*t*/Δ*t*)(*D*Δ*t* *k*²) = *Dk*²*t*, and *Nx*⁴ = (*t*/Δ*t*)(6*D*Δ*t* *k*²)² = 36*D*²*k*⁴*t* Δ*t* → 0. So the characteristic function converges to exp(−*Dk*²*t*), which is the characteristic function of the stated Gaussian density, and by Lévy’s continuity theorem the distributions converge. (iv) Differentiating, ∂ₜ*ρ* = *ρ* (−3/(2*t*) + |**r**|²/(4*Dt*²)) and ∇²*ρ* = *ρ* (|**r**|²/(4*D*²*t*²) − 3/(2*Dt*)); the first is *D* times the second. □

**Theorem II.8.6.3 (The Liouville measure as the invariant measure of the Poincaré group; Theorem 7 of [80]).** The states of a free body of mass *m* are its straight worldlines, labelled by the position **q** at *t* = 0 and the momentum **p**. (i) The Poincaré group ISO⁺(1,3) of Lemma II.6.26 acts transitively on these states. (ii) The measure *d*³*q* *d*³*p* is invariant under this action. (iii) Every invariant measure that is finite on bounded sets is a constant multiple of *d*³*q* *d*³*p*.

*Proof.* (i) A translation carries **q** to any other position, and a rotation followed by a boost carries **p** to any other momentum, because the velocity of Theorem II.8.1.7 may be chosen freely with |**v**| < *c*. (ii) Translations add a constant to **q** and leave **p** unchanged; rotations rotate **q** and **p**; both preserve *d*³*q* *d*³*p*. For the boost of Theorem II.8.1.7 with velocity *u* along *x*, write *γ*_u = (1 − *u*²/*c*²)^(−1/2), *E* for the energy and **v** = **p***c*²/*E* for the velocity of the state. The momentum transforms as *p*′_x = *γ*_u(*p*_x − *uE*/*c*²), *p*′_y = *p*_y, *p*′_z = *p*_z, and since ∂*E*/∂*p*_x = *v*_x, the Jacobian is ∂*p*′_x/∂*p*_x = *γ*_u(1 − *uv*_x/*c*²). The worldline **x** = **q** + **v***t* meets the new surface *t*′ = 0, that is *t* = *ux*/*c*², at *x* = *q*_x/(1 − *uv*_x/*c*²), where *x*′ = *γ*_u(*x* − *ut*) = *γ*_u *x*(1 − *u*²/*c*²) = *q*_x/(*γ*_u(1 − *uv*_x/*c*²)); the coordinates *y*′ and *z*′ at that event are *q*_y and *q*_z plus multiples of *q*_x. So at fixed **p** the Jacobian of **q** ↦ **q**′ is 1/(*γ*_u(1 − *uv*_x/*c*²)). The momentum **p**′ does not depend on **q**, so the Jacobian of (**q**, **p**) ↦ (**q**′, **p**′) is the product of the two, which is 1. (iii) On a space on which a group acts transitively, an invariant measure finite on bounded sets is unique up to a constant factor when it exists [131]; by (ii) it exists. □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of the equal a priori probabilities.** Boltzmann and Gibbs postulated the uniform measure *d*³*q* *d*³*p* on phase space. Theorem II.8.6.3 derives it: the Poincaré group is the invariance group of the interval generated by *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* (Theorem II.6.28), and the measure invariant under it is the Liouville measure. For *N* bodies the measure is the product over the bodies.

**Theorem II.8.6.4 (Ergodicity of directions; Theorem 8 of [80]).** Under (S1) and (S2), for every continuous function *F* on the unit sphere, the time average of *F* along the sequence of step directions equals its average over the McGucken Sphere, with probability one: (1/*N*)(*F*(**n**₁) + ⋯ + *F*(**n**(*N*))) → ∫ *F* *d*Ω/4*π* as *N* → ∞.

*Proof.* Put *X*_a = *F*(**n**(*a*)) − ∫ *F* *d*Ω/4*π*. The *X*_a are independent, have mean zero, and satisfy |*X*_a| ≤ *K* for a constant *K*, since a continuous function on the sphere is bounded. Let *S*_N = *X*₁ + ⋯ + *X*_N. Expanding *S*_N⁴ and taking means, every term containing some *X*_a to the first power has mean zero by independence, leaving the *N* terms *X*_a⁴ and the 3*N*(*N* − 1) terms *X*_a²*X*_b² with *a* ≠ *b*; hence the mean of *S*_N⁴ is at most 3*N*²*K*⁴. By Markov’s inequality the probability that |*S*_N/*N*| > *ε* is at most 3*K*⁴/(*ε*⁴*N*²), and the sum of these bounds over *N* is finite. By the Borel–Cantelli lemma, with probability one |*S*_N/*N*| > *ε* for only finitely many *N*. Applying this for *ε* = 1, 1/2, 1/3, … gives *S*_N/*N* → 0 with probability one. □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of the ergodic hypothesis.** Boltzmann’s ergodic hypothesis asserts the equality of time averages and phase-space averages for the Hamiltonian flow, and Birkhoff’s theorem [9] proves it under metric transitivity, which the theorem of Kolmogorov, Arnold and Moser shows to fail on sets of positive measure. Theorem II.8.6.4 uses neither: the average over the McGucken Sphere is reached because the successive directions are independent and uniformly distributed, which is (S1) and (S2).

**Theorem II.8.6.5 (Strict second law for matter; Theorem 9 of [80]).** Let *ρ*(**r**, *t*) > 0 be a solution of ∂ₜ*ρ* = *D* ∇²*ρ* with ∫ *ρ* *d*³*r* = 1 that, with its derivatives, decays faster than any power of |**r**|, and let *S*(*t*) = −*k*_B ∫ *ρ* ln *ρ* *d*³*r*. (i) *dS*/*dt* = *k*_B *D* ∫ |∇*ρ*|²/*ρ* *d*³*r* > 0. (ii) For the Gaussian of Theorem II.8.6.2, *S*(*t*) = (3/2)*k*_B[1 + ln(4*πDt*)] and *dS*/*dt* = 3*k*_B/(2*t*).

*Proof.* (i) *dS*/*dt* = −*k*_B ∫ (∂ₜ*ρ*)(ln *ρ* + 1) *d*³*r*. Since ∫ ∂ₜ*ρ* *d*³*r* = (*d*/*dt*) ∫ *ρ* *d*³*r* = 0, this is −*k*_B *D* ∫ (∇²*ρ*) ln *ρ* *d*³*r*. Integrating by parts, with no boundary term because of the decay, −∫ (∇²*ρ*) ln *ρ* = ∫ ∇*ρ*·∇(ln *ρ*) = ∫ |∇*ρ*|²/*ρ*. The integrand is non-negative, and it is zero everywhere only if ∇*ρ* = 0 everywhere, which is impossible for a function with ∫ *ρ* = 1. (ii) ln *ρ* = −(3/2) ln(4*πDt*) − |**r**|²/(4*Dt*), so −∫ *ρ* ln *ρ* = (3/2) ln(4*πDt*) + (1/(4*Dt*)) ∫ |**r**|² *ρ*. By Theorem II.8.6.2(i) carried to the limit, ∫ |**r**|² *ρ* = 6*Dt*, so the second term is 3/2. Differentiating (3/2)*k*_B[1 + ln(4*πDt*)] gives 3*k*_B/(2*t*). □

**Theorem II.8.6.6 (Entropy of light on the McGucken Sphere; Theorem 10 of [80]).** Light emitted at an event with no preferred direction is, at the time *t* after emission, distributed uniformly on the McGucken Sphere of radius *ct*. Its entropy is *S*(*t*) = *k*_B ln(4*π*(*ct*)²), and *dS*/*dt* = 2*k*_B/*t* > 0.

*Proof.* By Theorem II.8.1.9 light from the event reaches exactly the points at distance *ct*, and by Theorem II.8.6.1 its distribution over directions is uniform. The density with respect to area on the sphere of area *A* = 4*π*(*ct*)² is 1/*A*, so −*k*_B ∫ (1/*A*) ln(1/*A*) *dA* = *k*_B ln *A*. Differentiating, *dS*/*dt* = *k*_B (2/*t*). □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of the second law.** Boltzmann’s *H*-theorem obtains *dS*/*dt* ≥ 0 from the assumption of molecular chaos, and Loschmidt objected that the underlying mechanics is reversible. In Theorems II.8.6.5 and II.8.6.6 the increase is strict at every instant and its source is named: the outward expansion of the McGucken Sphere, which has the orientation +i*c* of the Principle. The entropies here are those of a probability density; a change of the unit of length adds a constant to *S* and leaves *dS*/*dt* unchanged.

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* Reading of the Arrows of Time, the Diffusion Constant, and Black-Hole Thermodynamics: The Outgoing Wave, Reversible Equations and the Irreversible Expansion, the Earliest State, the Langevin Theorem, the Thermal Period, the Hawking Temperature and the Bekenstein–Hawking Entropy, with Proofs.**

**McGucken Principle anchor.** The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* states that the fourth dimension is expanding at the velocity of light in a spherically symmetric manner from every spacetime event, as a McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action *h*. The orientation is +i*c*: the McGucken Sphere of an event exists at the times after the event and its radius grows. For the arrows of time the Principle predicts that radiation leaves its source on expanding spheres, that entropy is least at the earliest time, and that the equations which are unchanged under reversal of *t* are those that do not contain the expansion. The statements follow Theorems 11–17 of [80]; every proof is carried out here in full.

**Theorem II.8.6.7 (The outgoing wave of a point source; Theorem 11, Step 4, of [80]).** Let *f* be a twice continuously differentiable function with *f*(*s*) = 0 for *s* ≤ 0, let *r* = |**x**|, and put *u*(*t*, **x**) = *f*(*t* − *r*/*c*)/(4*πr*). (i) For *r* > 0, □*u* = 0. (ii) The flux of ∇*u* through the sphere of radius *R* about the origin tends to −*f*(*t*) as *R* → 0, so *u* is the field of a point source of strength *f*(*t*) at the origin. (iii) *u* = 0 at every event with *t* < *r*/*c*, and the value of *u* at distance *r* at time *t* is fixed by the source at the time *t* − *r*/*c*: it is carried by the McGucken Sphere emitted at that time. (iv) The function *f*(*t* + *r*/*c*)/(4*πr*) also satisfies (i), and it is different from zero at times before the source begins to act.

*Proof.* (i) For a function of the form *g*(*t*, *r*)/*r*, ∇²(*g*/*r*) = (∂²*g*/∂*r*²)/*r* for *r* > 0. With *g* = *f*(*t* − *r*/*c*)/4*π*, ∂²*g*/∂*r*² = *f*″/(4*πc*²) and ∂ₜ²*u* = *f*″/(4*πr*), so ∇²*u* − *c*⁻²∂ₜ²*u* = 0. (ii) ∂*u*/∂*r* = −*f*′(*t* − *r*/*c*)/(4*πcr*) − *f*(*t* − *r*/*c*)/(4*πr*²). Multiplying by the area 4*πR*² at *r* = *R* gives −*R* *f*′(*t* − *R*/*c*)/*c* − *f*(*t* − *R*/*c*), which tends to −*f*(*t*). (iii) *f*(*t* − *r*/*c*) = 0 when *t* − *r*/*c* ≤ 0. (iv) The computation of (i) is unchanged when *r*/*c* is replaced by −*r*/*c*; and *f*(*t* + *r*/*c*) ≠ 0 wherever *t* + *r*/*c* > 0 and *f* ≠ 0 there, which includes events with *t* < 0. □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of the radiative arrow.** Both functions of Theorem II.8.6.7 solve the wave equation. The first is supported on the McGucken Spheres of the source events, which by the orientation +i*c* exist after those events and expand. The second is supported on spheres that contract onto the source and exist before it acts; the Principle has no such spheres. The observed fact that radiation is retarded is the orientation +i*c* of *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*.

**Theorem II.8.6.8 (Reversible equations and the irreversible expansion; Theorem 12 of [80]).** (i) If *φ*(*t*, **x**) satisfies the Schrödinger equation of Theorem II.8.3.4 with real *V*(**x**), so does *χ*(*t*, **x**) = *φ*∗(−*t*, **x**). (ii) If *ψ*(*t*, **x**) satisfies the Klein–Gordon equation of Theorem II.8.3.3, so does *ψ*(−*t*, **x**). (iii) If *ρ*(*t*, **x**) satisfies the diffusion equation ∂ₜ*ρ* = *D* ∇²*ρ* of Theorem II.8.6.2, then *ρ*(−*t*, **x**) satisfies ∂ₜ*ρ* = −*D* ∇²*ρ*, a different equation, along whose solutions the entropy of Theorem II.8.6.5 decreases.

*Proof.* (i) Taking the complex conjugate of iℏ ∂ₜ*φ* = −(ℏ²/2*m*) ∇²*φ* + *Vφ* gives −iℏ ∂ₜ*φ*∗ = −(ℏ²/2*m*) ∇²*φ*∗ + *Vφ*∗. Since (∂ₜ*χ*)(*t*) = −(∂ₜ*φ*∗)(−*t*), iℏ ∂ₜ*χ* is the left side evaluated at −*t*, and the right side evaluated at −*t* is −(ℏ²/2*m*) ∇²*χ* + *Vχ*. (ii) The equation contains ∂ₜ only through ∂ₜ², which is unchanged when *t* is replaced by −*t*. (iii) With *σ*(*t*) = *ρ*(−*t*), ∂ₜ*σ* = −(∂ₜ*ρ*)(−*t*) = −*D* ∇²*σ*. For *σ* the computation of Theorem II.8.6.5(i) gives *dS*/*dt* = −*k*_B *D* ∫ |∇*σ*|²/*σ* < 0. □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of Loschmidt’s objection.** Loschmidt objected in 1876 that equations unchanged under reversal of *t* cannot yield an entropy that always increases. Theorem II.8.6.8 locates the two kinds of equation. The Schrödinger and Klein–Gordon equations are unchanged under reversal. The diffusion equation is changed by it, and it was derived in Theorem II.8.6.2 from the hypotheses (S1)–(S3), in which each step starts from an event and proceeds to later times, the order in which the McGucken Sphere of that event expands. The increase of entropy in Theorem II.8.6.5 is a consequence of the diffusion equation and so of that order; it is derived from (S1)–(S3) and makes no use of the reversible equations.

**Theorem II.8.6.9 (The earliest state has the least entropy; Theorem 13 of [80]).** Let *t* > 0 be the time elapsed since the origin event of the ensemble. For the matter ensemble of Theorem II.8.6.5(ii), *S*(*t*) = (3/2)*k*_B[1 + ln(4*πDt*)], and for the light of Theorem II.8.6.6, *S*(*t*) = *k*_B ln(4*πc*²*t*²). In both cases *S*(*t*₁) < *S*(*t*₂) whenever 0 < *t*₁ < *t*₂, and *S*(*t*) → −∞ as *t* → 0.

*Proof.* The logarithm is strictly increasing and tends to −∞ as its argument tends to 0, and 4*πDt* and 4*πc*²*t*² are strictly increasing in *t* > 0 and tend to 0. □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of the Past Hypothesis.** The Past Hypothesis is the separate assumption that the universe began in a state of very low entropy, with the improbability estimated by Penrose [123]. For the ensembles of Theorems II.8.6.5 and II.8.6.6 no such assumption is made: the McGucken Sphere of the origin event has radius zero at that event, and the entropy is lower at every earlier time by Theorem II.8.6.9.

**Hypothesis on the force (Theorem 14 of [80]).** (S4) Along each axis a body of mass *m* is subject to the damping force −*mγv* and to a fluctuating force *ξ*(*t*) of mean zero and strength *D*_p, in the sense that for continuous functions *g*, *h* the mean of the product (∫ *g* *ξ* *ds*)(∫ *h* *ξ* *ds*) is 2*D*_p ∫ *g* *h* *ds*. This holds for independent momentum kicks of size ±*q* arriving at the mean rate *ν*, with 2*D*_p = *νq*². Theorem 14 of [80] takes, as the Compton-coupling ansatz, kicks of size *q* = *εmc* at the rate *ν* = Ω, where *ε* is a dimensionless modulation amplitude and Ω a modulation frequency of the expansion of *x*₄; then *D*_p = *ε*²*m*²*c*²Ω/2.

**Theorem II.8.6.10 (Langevin theorem and the diffusion constant; Theorem 14 of [80]).** Under (S4), for the motion *m* *dv*/*dt* = −*mγv* + *ξ* with *v*(0) = 0 and *x*(0) = 0: (i) the mean of *x*(*t*)² is (2*D*_p/(*m*²*γ*²)) [*t* − (2/*γ*)(1 − exp(−*γt*)) + (1/2*γ*)(1 − exp(−2*γt*))]; (ii) the mean of *x*(*t*)²/(2*t*) tends to *D* = *D*_p/(*mγ*)² as *t* → ∞; (iii) the mean of *v*(*t*)² tends to *D*_p/(*m*²*γ*); (iv) for the kicks of [80], *D* = *ε*²*c*²Ω/(2*γ*²), in which the mass has cancelled; (v) for thermal kicks at temperature *T*, for which the mean of *mv*²/2 is *k*_B*T*/2, *D*_p = *mγk*_B*T* and *D* = *k*_B*T*/(*mγ*); (vi) for independent thermal and Compton-coupling kicks together, *D* = *k*_B*T*/(*mγ*) + *ε*²*c*²Ω/(2*γ*²).

*Proof.* The solution of the equation of motion is *v*(*t*) = (1/*m*) ∫ exp(−*γ*(*t* − *s*)) *ξ*(*s*) *ds* over 0 ≤ *s* ≤ *t*. Integrating, and exchanging the order of the two integrations, *x*(*t*) = (1/*m*) ∫ *ξ*(*s*) (1 − exp(−*γ*(*t* − *s*)))/*γ* *ds*. (i) By (S4) with *g* = *h* = (1 − exp(−*γ*(*t* − *s*)))/(*mγ*), the mean of *x*(*t*)² is (2*D*_p/(*m*²*γ*²)) ∫ (1 − exp(−*γu*))² *du* over 0 ≤ *u* ≤ *t*, and ∫ (1 − 2 exp(−*γu*) + exp(−2*γu*)) *du* is the bracket of the statement. (ii) The bracket divided by *t* tends to 1. (iii) By (S4) with *g* = *h* = exp(−*γ*(*t* − *s*))/*m*, the mean of *v*(*t*)² is (2*D*_p/*m*²)(1 − exp(−2*γt*))/(2*γ*), which tends to *D*_p/(*m*²*γ*). (iv) *D*_p/(*mγ*)² = *ε*²*m*²*c*²Ω/(2*m*²*γ*²). (v) Setting the limit of (iii) equal to *k*_B*T*/*m* gives *D*_p = *mγk*_B*T*, and then (ii) gives *D* = *k*_B*T*/(*mγ*). (vi) For independent forces of mean zero the means of the cross products vanish, so the strengths *D*_p add, and by (ii) so do the diffusion constants. □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of the diffusion constant.** The constant *D* of Theorem II.8.6.2 is *ℓ*²/(6Δ*t*); Theorem II.8.6.10 expresses it through the force. The thermal part *k*_B*T*/(*mγ*) vanishes as *T* → 0 and depends on the mass. The part *ε*²*c*²Ω/(2*γ*²) is independent of temperature and of mass: this is prediction (P2) of Appendix B of [115].

**Theorem II.8.6.11 (The thermal period in *τ*_E).** Let *H* be a self-adjoint operator on a finite-dimensional Hilbert space, *β* > 0, ⟨*X*⟩ = Tr(exp(−*βH*) *X*)/Tr(exp(−*βH*)), and for complex *z* let *A*(*z*) = exp(i*zH*/ℏ) *A* exp(−i*zH*/ℏ). Then ⟨*B* *A*(*t* + iℏ*β*)⟩ = ⟨*A*(*t*) *B*⟩ for all operators *A*, *B*. In the variable *τ*_E = i*t* of §II.9.5 the shift of *t* by iℏ*β* is the shift of *τ*_E by −ℏ*β*: the correlation functions of a state of temperature *T* = 1/(*k*_B*β*) repeat, with the order of the operators exchanged, when *τ*_E advances by the period ℏ/(*k*_B*T*).

*Proof.* *A*(*t* + iℏ*β*) = exp(−*βH*) *A*(*t*) exp(*βH*). Hence Tr(exp(−*βH*) *B* *A*(*t* + iℏ*β*)) = Tr(exp(−*βH*) *B* exp(−*βH*) *A*(*t*) exp(*βH*)), and moving exp(*βH*) to the front under the trace gives Tr(*B* exp(−*βH*) *A*(*t*)) = Tr(exp(−*βH*) *A*(*t*) *B*). □

**Theorem II.8.6.12 (Hawking temperature; Theorem 16 of [80]).** Take the Schwarzschild metric of a mass *M*, *ds*² = −*f* *c*²*dt*² + *dr*²/*f* + *r*²*d*Ω² with *f* = 1 − *r*_s/*r* and *r*_s = 2*GM*/*c*² (Theorem II.8.2.1), and let *τ*_E = i*t*. (i) With *ξ* the proper distance from the horizon *r* = *r*_s along a radius, the metric in the (*τ*_E, *r*) plane is, to leading order as *ξ* → 0, *dξ*² + *ξ*² *dθ*² with *θ* = *cτ*_E/(2*r*_s). (ii) This is the metric of the plane in polar coordinates, with no conical point at *ξ* = 0, exactly when *θ* has the period 2*π*, that is, when *τ*_E has the period 4*πr*_s/*c* = 2*πc*/*κ*, where *κ* = *c*²/(2*r*_s) = *c*⁴/(4*GM*) is the surface gravity. (iii) A state with this period has, by Theorem II.8.6.11, the temperature *T*_H = ℏ*κ*/(2*πck*_B) = ℏ*c*³/(8*πGMk*_B).

*Proof.* (i) Since *dt* = −i *dτ*_E, −*f* *c*²*dt*² = *f* *c*²*dτ*_E². Near the horizon *f* = (*r* − *r*_s)/*r*_s to leading order, and the proper distance is *ξ* = ∫ *dr*/*f*^(1/2) = 2 (*r*_s(*r* − *r*_s))^(1/2) to leading order, so *f* = *ξ*²/(4*r*_s²) to leading order, and *dr*²/*f* = *dξ*² by the definition of *ξ*. The metric of the plane is *f* *c*²*dτ*_E² + *dξ*² = *ξ*² (*c* *dτ*_E/(2*r*_s))² + *dξ*². (ii) In the metric *dξ*² + *ξ*²*dθ*² with *θ* of period *P*, the circle of radius *ξ* has circumference *Pξ*; the plane has 2*πξ*, and for *P* ≠ 2*π* the point *ξ* = 0 is the vertex of a cone. *θ* = *cτ*_E/(2*r*_s) has period 2*π* when *τ*_E has period 4*πr*_s/*c*, and 2*πc*/*κ* = 2*πc* · 2*r*_s/*c*² = 4*πr*_s/*c*. (iii) Setting ℏ/(*k*_B*T*) = 2*πc*/*κ* gives *T* = ℏ*κ*/(2*πck*_B), and *κ* = *c*⁴/(4*GM*). □

**Theorem II.8.6.13 (Bekenstein–Hawking entropy; Theorem 15 of [80]).** For the black hole of Theorem II.8.6.12, with energy *E* = *Mc*², temperature *T*_H and horizon area *A* = 4*πr*_s², the entropy defined by *dS* = *dE*/*T*_H with *S* = 0 at *M* = 0 is *S* = 4*πGk*_B*M*²/(ℏ*c*) = *k*_B *A*/(4*ℓ*_P²), where *ℓ*_P = (ℏ*G*/*c*³)^(1/2).

*Proof.* *dS* = *c*² *dM* · 8*πGMk*_B/(ℏ*c*³) = (8*πGk*_B/(ℏ*c*)) *M* *dM*, and integrating from 0 to *M* gives 4*πGk*_B*M*²/(ℏ*c*). The area is *A* = 4*π*(2*GM*/*c*²)² = 16*πG*²*M*²/*c*⁴, so *k*_B *A* *c*³/(4ℏ*G*) = 4*πGk*_B*M*²/(ℏ*c*), and *c*³/(ℏ*G*) = 1/*ℓ*_P². □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of black-hole thermodynamics.** The variable *τ*_E = *x*₄/*c* = i*t* is the fourth coordinate of the Principle divided by *c*. In Theorem II.8.6.12 the temperature is fixed by the requirement that the geometry in *x*₄ be regular at the horizon, and in Theorem II.8.6.13 the factor 1/4 follows from that temperature. The Schwarzschild metric is that of Theorem II.8.2.1. One input is taken from outside: the regularity of the state at the horizon.

**(c) The five arrows of time (Th 11–14).** The thermodynamic, cosmological, radiative, psychological/biological, and quantum-measurement arrows are projections onto the spatial slice of the single +i*c*-directed expansion *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* (Th 11). Loschmidt’s reversibility paradox is dissolved (Th 12) by the McGucken Duality: the Algebraic Channel (algebra) is time-symmetric, but the Geometric Channel (geometric propagation on the McGucken Sphere Σ_M(τ)) is monotone in *x*₄. The Past Hypothesis is dissolved (Th 13) because the *x*₄ origin at *R* = 0 is geometrically the lowest-entropy state; no 10^(−10¹²³) initial-condition fine-tuning (Penrose 1979) is required. Th 14 supplies the explicit Compton-coupling diffusion *Dₓ*^(McG) = *ε*²*c*²Ω/(2*γ*²) — the *x*₄-driven diffusion coefficient that appears in Appendix B of [115] as prediction (P2), where *ε* is the dimensionless modulation amplitude, Ω the modulation angular frequency, and *γ* here the environmental damping rate (not the Lorentz factor of §II.2).

**(d) Black-hole thermodynamics via the McGucken Wick rotation (Th 15–18).** Define the McGucken Wick rotation *τ*_E = *x*₄/*c*; this removes the i from the law (*dx*₄/*dτ*_E = *c*) and produces a Euclidean four-manifold on which the metric *c*²*dτ*_E² + *d***x**² is real and positive-definite and the McGucken Operator D_M = ∂ₜ + i∂_τE is the elliptic Cauchy–Riemann operator of *z* = *t* + i*τ*_E. At a Killing horizon, *x*₄-stationary modes are Planck-quantized at one mode per *ℓ*_*P*², yielding the Bekenstein–Hawking entropy *S*_BH = *k*_*B* *A*/(4*ℓ*_*P*²) (Th 15). The Euclidean cigar period fixes the Hawking temperature *T*_H = ℏ*κ*/(2*πck*_*B*) (Th 16). Th 17 gives the generalized second law *dS*ₜₒₜₐₗ/*dt* = *dS*_matter/*dt* + (*k*_*B*/(4*ℓ*_*P*²))*dA*/*dt* ≥ 0; Th 18 gives the FRW relations, including the reconstitution signature: with *ρ*(*t*) = *R*₄(*t*)*H*(*t*)/*c* the ratio of the McGucken horizon to the Hubble horizon, *ρ*(*t*_rec) ≈ 2.6, giving the entropy ratio the McGucken Sphere Σ_McG/*S*_Hub = *ρ*²(*t*_rec) ≈ 7 ([80], Theorem 18). The Wick rotation *τ*_E = *x*₄/*c* is exactly the inverse of the i that makes the McGucken Operator D_M tangent to the McGucken hypersurface; black-hole thermodynamics is therefore not an additional axiom (as in semiclassical gravity) but a direct consequence of the i in the source-law.

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* Reading of the Cosmological, Measurement and Psychological Arrows of Time and of the Thermodynamics of the Friedmann Universe, with Proofs.** **McGucken Principle anchor.** The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* states that the fourth dimension is expanding at the velocity of light in a spherically symmetric manner from every spacetime event, as a McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action *h*. The thermodynamic arrow is Theorems II.8.6.5 and II.8.6.6 and the radiative arrow is Theorem II.8.6.7. For the three remaining arrows of Theorem 11 of [80] the Principle predicts: the radius of every McGucken Sphere grows at the rate *c*, and the sign of the rate of the cosmic scale factor cannot change (Theorem II.8.6.14); a registration replaces a wavefunction by a distribution from which the wavefunction cannot be recovered, with non-negative entropy (Theorem II.8.6.15); and a memory can be reset only by delivering heat to its surroundings (Theorem II.8.6.16). Theorem 8.6.17 gives the thermodynamic relations of Theorem 18 of [80] that follow from the theorems of this paper. Each theorem names the hypothesis it uses beyond *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*.

**Theorem II.8.6.14 (Cosmological arrow; Theorem 11, Step 3, of [80]).** (i) The McGucken Sphere of an event at the time *t*₀ has at the time *t* > *t*₀ the radius *R*(*t*) = *c*(*t* − *t*₀), and *dR*/*dt* = *c* > 0. (ii) In the cosmology of Theorem II.8.2.7 with *ρ* > 0, Λ ≥ 0 and *k* ≤ 0, the scale factor satisfies *a*′ ≠ 0 at every time; if *a*′ > 0 at one time then *a*′ > 0 at all times. (iii) Under the hypothesis (C) of [80], that the growth of *a*(*t*) is the reading in *x*₁, *x*₂, *x*₃ of the growth of the McGucken Sphere of the initial event, whose radius is *R*₄(*t*) = *ct*, the sign of *a*′ is the sign of *dR*₄/*dt* = *c*: the universe expands.

*Proof.* (i) By the McGucken Principle *x*₄ advances by i*c*(*t* − *t*₀) between the two times, and the McGucken Sphere is the set of points of space at the distance |*x*₄(*t*) − *x*₄(*t*₀)| = *c*(*t* − *t*₀) from the event (§II.6). (ii) The quantity (8π*G*/3)*ρ* + Λ*c*²/3 − *kc*²/*a*² is positive under the stated conditions, and Theorem II.8.2.7(vi) applies on every interval. (iii) is the hypothesis combined with (i) and (ii). □

Hypothesis (C) is an input. Theorem II.8.2.7 determines *a*(*t*) from the field equations and does not by itself fix the sign of *a*′; (ii) shows that the sign, once given, is permanent, and (C) gives it.

**Theorem II.8.6.15 (Measurement arrow; Theorem 11, Step 5, of [80]).** Let *ψ* be a wavefunction with ∫ |*ψ*|² *d*³*x* = 1, and let space be divided into cells *V*₁, *V*₂, …, the cells of a detector. By the Born rule (Theorem 7.2 of [114], §II.9.3) the probability of registration in *V*_j is *p*_j = ∫ |*ψ*|² *d*³*x* over *V*_j. (i) The entropy of the registration, *S* = −*k*_B Σ_j *p*_j ln *p*_j, satisfies *S* ≥ 0, with *S* = 0 exactly when *ψ* vanishes outside one cell. (ii) The wavefunction that equals exp(i*θ*_j)*ψ* on *V*_j, for any real numbers *θ*_j, has the same probabilities *p*_j. The passage from *ψ* to the distribution (*p*_j) therefore has no inverse. (iii) The amplitude registered at an event is the amplitude carried there by the outgoing wavefront from the preparation (Theorems II.8.6.7 and II.9.3.5), so the registration is at a later time *t* than the preparation.

*Proof.* (i) Σ_j *p*_j = 1 and 0 ≤ *p*_j ≤ 1, so each term −*p*_j ln *p*_j is non-negative, and it is zero only for *p*_j = 0 or *p*_j = 1. All terms vanish exactly when one *p*_j is 1 and the others are 0. (ii) |exp(i*θ*_j)*ψ*| = |*ψ*|. Two wavefunctions with different relative phases *θ*_j − *θ*_l are different functions, and they evolve differently under Theorem II.8.3.4, yet they give one distribution. (iii) By Theorem II.8.6.7(iii) the outgoing wave vanishes at every event earlier than the arrival of the McGucken Sphere of its source. □

**Theorem II.8.6.16 (Psychological arrow; Theorem 11, Step 6, of [80]; Landauer’s bound [137]).** Let a memory be a system with states 1, …, *N* occupied with probabilities *q*₁, …, *q*_N, in contact with surroundings at the temperature *T*. Hypothesis (L): in any process the entropy of the memory plus the entropy of the surroundings does not decrease, and the entropy of the surroundings changes by *Q*/*T* when they receive the heat *Q*. Then a process that resets the memory to one fixed state delivers to the surroundings the heat *Q* ≥ −*k*_B*T* Σ_j *q*_j ln *q*_j. For one binary digit with *q*₁ = *q*₂ = ½, *Q* ≥ *k*_B*T* ln 2.

*Proof.* Before the reset the entropy of the memory is −*k*_B Σ_j *q*_j ln *q*_j; after it the memory is in one state with probability 1 and its entropy is 0. By hypothesis (L), *Q*/*T* − (−*k*_B Σ_j *q*_j ln *q*_j) ≥ 0. For *q*₁ = *q*₂ = ½ the sum is −ln 2. □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of Theorem II.8.6.16.** A memory that is used more than once must be reset, and each reset raises the entropy of the surroundings. Hypothesis (L) is the second law for the memory with its surroundings; Theorems II.8.6.5 and II.8.6.6 prove the increase of entropy with *t* for matter diffusing by the steps of Theorem II.8.6.1 and for light on the McGucken Sphere, and (L) extends it to the surroundings of a memory. Under (L) the making and remaking of records proceeds in the direction of *t* in which *x*₄ advances.

**Theorem II.8.6.17 (Thermodynamics of the Friedmann universe; Theorem 18, items 1–3, of [80]).** (i) The McGucken Sphere of the initial event has the radius *R*₄(*t*) = *ct* and the area 4π*c*²*t*². Under the hypothesis (H) of [80], that this McGucken Sphere carries the entropy of Theorem II.8.6.13 per unit area, its entropy is *S*₄(*t*) = *k*_B·4π*c*²*t*²/(4ℓ_P²) = π*k*_B*c*²*t*²/ℓ_P², with *dS*₄/*dt* = 2π*k*_B*c*²*t*/ℓ_P² > 0. With the rates 3*k*_B/(2*t*) of Theorem II.8.6.5 and 2*k*_B/*t* of Theorem II.8.6.6, every term of the total increases with *t*. (ii) The metric *ds*² = −*f* *c*²*dt*² + *dr*²/*f* + *r*²(*dθ*² + sin²*θ* *dφ*²) with *f* = 1 − *H*²*r*²/*c*² satisfies the vacuum field equations with Λ = 3*H*²/*c*². Its horizon is at *r* = *c*/*H*, with the area 4π*c*²/*H*². With *τ*_E = i*t*, the metric near the horizon has no conical point exactly when *τ*_E has the period 2π/*H*, and a state with this period has the temperature *T* = ℏ*H*/(2π*k*_B). Under (H) the entropy of the horizon is π*k*_B*c*²/(*H*²ℓ_P²). (iii) The ratio of *R*₄(*t*) to the Hubble radius *c*/(*a*′/*a*) is *ρ*₄(*t*) = *t* *a*′/*a*. In the two eras of Theorem II.8.2.7(v) it is 2/3 for *p* = 0 and 1/2 for *p* = *ρc*²/3, and the ratio of the entropies assigned by (H) to the two radii is its square.

*Proof.* (i) Theorem II.8.6.14(i) with *t*₀ = 0, and the area law *S* = *k*_B*A*/(4ℓ_P²) of Theorem II.8.6.13. (ii) For a metric of this form with a function *f*(*r*), the Ricci tensor is *R*_tt = ½*c*²*f*(*f*″ + 2*f*′/*r*), *R*_rr = −(*f*″ + 2*f*′/*r*)/(2*f*), *R*_θθ = 1 − *f* − *rf*′, *R*_φφ = sin²*θ* *R*_θθ, the prime here denoting *d*/*dr* (Theorem II.8.2.1(ii) with *A* = *f* and *B* = 1/*f*). For *f* = 1 − *H*²*r*²/*c*², *f*″ + 2*f*′/*r* = −6*H*²/*c*² and 1 − *f* − *rf*′ = 3*H*²*r*²/*c*², so *R*_μν = (3*H*²/*c*²)*g*_μν, *R* = 12*H*²/*c*² and *G*_μν + (3*H*²/*c*²)*g*_μν = 0. Let *r*_H = *c*/*H*. Near *r* = *r*_H, *f* = 2(*r*_H − *r*)/*r*_H to leading order, and the proper distance from the horizon is *ξ* = ∫ *dr*/*f*^(1/2) = (2*r*_H(*r*_H − *r*))^(1/2), so *f* = *ξ*²/*r*_H². With *τ*_E = i*t*, −*f* *c*²*dt*² + *dr*²/*f* = *dξ*² + *ξ*² *dϑ*² with *ϑ* = *cτ*_E/*r*_H = *Hτ*_E. As in Theorem II.8.6.12(ii) this is the plane without a conical point exactly when *ϑ* has the period 2π. By Theorem II.8.6.11 the period of *τ*_E is ℏ/(*k*_B*T*), so ℏ/(*k*_B*T*) = 2π/*H*. The entropy is the area law applied to 4π*r*_H². (iii) The Hubble radius is *ca*/*a*′, so the ratio is *ct* divided by *ca*/*a*′, which is *t* *a*′/*a*, and Theorem II.8.2.7(v) gives the two values; the entropies of (H) are proportional to the squares of the radii. □

Two inputs of Theorem II.8.6.17 are hypotheses: (H), and, as in Theorem II.8.6.12, the regularity of the state at the horizon. The value *ρ*₄ ≈ 2.6 at recombination and the further items 4–6 of Theorem 18 of [80], quoted in paragraph (d) above and in Appendix B of [115], are results of the cosmological model cited there, in which the Hubble rate is taken from the contraction of the spatial factor; they are not consequences of Theorem II.8.2.7, for which *ρ*₄ = 2/3 in the era of matter.

We summarize the closure of Einstein’s three gaps:

| Gap | McGucken closure                                            | Theorem          | Standard problem                                |
|-----|-------------------------------------------------------------|------------------|-------------------------------------------------|
| T1  | Haar measure on SO(3), acting on the McGucken Sphere Σ_M(τ) | Th 7             | Liouville measure postulated                    |
| T2  | Huygens wavefront = ensemble                                | Th 8             | Ergodicity false (KAM)                          |
| T3  | dS/dt = (3/2)k_B/t > 0 strict, geometric                   | Th 9, 10, 12, 13 | dS/dt ≥ 0 statistical; requires Past Hypothesis |

*Table 8.1. Closure of Einstein’s three statistical-mechanical gaps by the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework.*

Notation bridge. McGucken (2026e) writes the future light cone as *Σ*₊(*p*₀) with *R*(*t*) = *c*(*t* − *t*₀); this is the same surface we have called the McGucken Sphere Σ_M(τ) with radius *cτ*. Theorem 3 (Huygens propagation), Theorem 8 (wavefront ergodicity), and Theorem 10 (photon entropy via the 4*π*(*cτ*)² area) all live on the McGucken Sphere Σ_M(τ). The diffusion of Theorem 14 follows from the Compton coupling of Theorem 4, with rest-frame modulation *H*_mod(*τ*) = *εmc*² cos(Ω*τ*) in an environment of damping rate *γ* ([80], Theorem 14). Thermodynamics is thus not appended to the framework; it is the statistical content of the McGucken Operator D_M acting on the McGucken Sphere Σ_M(τ).

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* Reading of the Inputs of the Second Law: Proposition II.8.6.18.** **McGucken Principle anchor.** The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* states that the fourth dimension is expanding at the velocity of light in a spherically symmetric manner from every spacetime event, as a McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action *h*. For the steps of a body coupled to the expansion of *x*₄ the Principle predicts that the direction of each step has no preferred value, whatever path led to the event at which the step starts. The proposition shows that this one statement yields both (S1) and (S2), and it records which of the theorems of this section use (S3).

**Proposition II.8.6.18 (The inputs of Theorems II.8.6.1–II.8.6.9).** (i) Suppose that at each event at which a step starts, the direction of the step, conditionally on the directions of all earlier steps, is distributed by a rotation-invariant probability measure. Then (S1) and (S2) hold. (ii) Theorem II.8.6.6, and Theorem II.8.6.9 for light, use neither (S2) nor (S3). (iii) The rate *dS*/*dt* = 3*k*_B/(2*t*) of Theorem II.8.6.5(ii) has the same value for every step length ℓ and every step duration Δ*t*.

*Proof.* (i) By Theorem II.8.6.1 a rotation-invariant probability measure on the unit sphere is *d*Ω/4π, so the conditional distribution of the *N*-th direction, given the first *N* − 1 directions, is *d*Ω/4π for every value of those directions. Let *f*₁, …, *f*_N be bounded measurable functions on the sphere. Conditioning on the first *N* − 1 directions, the mean of the product of the *f*_a at the *a*-th directions, *a* = 1, …, *N*, equals the mean of the product for *a* = 1, …, *N* − 1 times the integral of *f*_N *d*Ω/4π. By induction on *N* the mean of the product is the product of the integrals of *f*_a *d*Ω/4π. This is (S1) for each step together with the independence (S2). (ii) The proof of Theorem II.8.6.6 uses Theorem II.8.1.9 and Theorem II.8.6.1, and Theorem II.8.6.9 for light uses the formula of Theorem II.8.6.6. (iii) *S*(*t*) = (3/2)*k*_B[1 + ln(4π*Dt*)] has the derivative 3*k*_B/(2*t*), in which *D* = ℓ²/(6Δ*t*) does not appear. □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of Proposition II.8.6.18.** The hypothesis of (i) is the clause “from every spacetime event” of the Principle: the McGucken Sphere of the event at which a step starts is spherically symmetric, whatever path led to that event. With it, the inputs of Theorems II.8.6.1–II.8.6.9 are the Principle and (S3), the existence of a step length ℓ and a step duration Δ*t* for a body of mass *m*. (S3) belongs to the Compton coupling of [80], Theorem 4, whose statement reads: “Massive matter couples to *x*₄’s expansion through the Compton angular frequency ω_C = *mc*²/ℏ.” By (iii) the strict second law for matter holds with the same rate for every value of ℓ and Δ*t*, and by (ii) the strict second law for light emitted with no preferred direction uses the Principle alone, through Theorems II.8.1.9 and II.8.6.1.

**II.8.7 Action: The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* Reading of the Principle of Least Action.** **McGucken Principle anchor.** The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* states that the fourth dimension is expanding at the velocity of light in a spherically symmetric manner from every spacetime event, as a McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action *h*. For the action of a free particle the Principle predicts the following: the action of a worldline is its accumulated *x*₄-advance, *S* = −*mc* ∫ |*dx*₄| = −*mc*² ∫ *dτ*, and in units of the action ℏ it is the *x*₄-phase accumulated at the Compton frequency *ω*_C = *mc*²/ℏ of §II.8.3, *S*/ℏ = −*ω*_C *τ*. Proposition IV.1 of [77] proves that this functional is the only one satisfying five stated conditions, and Theorem VI.1 of [77] extends the uniqueness to four sectors.

**Proposition II.8.7.1 (Uniqueness of the free-particle action; Proposition IV.1 of [77]).** Let *γ* be a timelike worldline in Minkowski spacetime and let *S*[*γ*] be a real scalar functional of *γ* satisfying: (a) Poincaré invariance — *S*[*γ*] is invariant under the full Poincaré group (Lorentz transformations plus translations) of spacetime, so that no spacetime point is preferred over any other; (b) reparametrization invariance — *S*[*γ*] depends on *γ* only through its image as a curve, not on the choice of parameter *λ* labeling points on *γ*; (c) locality — *S*[*γ*] is the integral along *γ* of a local functional of position and first derivatives, *S*[*γ*] = ∫ *F*(*x*, *ẋ*) *dλ*; (d) first-order derivatives only — *F* depends on *ẋ*^μ but not on second or higher derivatives; (e) dimensional consistency — *S* has units of action (energy × time). Under these five conditions, the unique (up to overall multiplicative constant and additive total-derivative terms) functional *S*[*γ*] is

*S*[*γ*] = −*mc* ∫ √(−*η*_μν *ẋ*^μ *ẋ*^ν) *dλ* = −*mc* ∫ |*dx*₄| = −*mc*² ∫ *dτ*,

with *m* a constant of dimension mass. No other functional of the worldline satisfies all five conditions.

*Proof (Proposition IV.1 of [77]).* The argument is a standard result in the calculus of variations, adapted here to the Lorentzian worldline setting. By condition (c), *S*[*γ*] = ∫ *F*(*x*, *ẋ*) *dλ* for some local *F*. By condition (b) (reparametrization invariance), *F* must be homogeneous of degree one in *ẋ*^μ: *F*(*x*, *αẋ*) = *αF*(*x*, *ẋ*) for *α* > 0. To see this, consider a reparametrization *λ* → *λ*′ with *dλ*′ = *α* *dλ* for constant *α* > 0 (local rescaling; the argument extends to general monotonic reparametrizations). Then the velocity in the new parameter is *dx*/*dλ*′ = *ẋ*/*α*, and the measure is *dλ* = *dλ*′/*α*. The integrand *F*(*x*, *ẋ*) *dλ* expressed in the new parameter becomes *F*(*x*, *α* *dx*/*dλ*′) *dλ*′/*α*. Reparametrization invariance requires this to equal *F*(*x*, *dx*/*dλ*′) *dλ*′, so *F* is homogeneous of degree one in its velocity argument. By condition (a) (Lorentz invariance), *F* must be a Lorentz scalar built from *ẋ*^μ and (possibly) *η*_μν. The most general such *F* that is homogeneous of degree one in *ẋ* is

*F*(*x*, *ẋ*) = *A*(*x*) √(−*η*_μν *ẋ*^μ *ẋ*^ν) + *B*_μ(*x*) *ẋ*^μ,

where *A*(*x*) is a Lorentz scalar and *B*_μ(*x*) is a Lorentz covector. The first term is homogeneous of degree one and Lorentz-invariant; the second term is also homogeneous of degree one and Lorentz-covariant when contracted with a covector. Exhaustiveness: any other degree-one scalar built from *ẋ* with *η*-contractions must be a power of *ẋ*·*ẋ*, and only the half-power is of degree one, giving the first term; degree-one scalars with external covector structure must contract *ẋ* against a rank-one object, giving the second term. The two-term form above is the most general allowed by (a)–(d).

By condition (d) (no higher-order derivatives) and condition (c) (locality), neither *A*(*x*) nor *B*_μ(*x*) can depend on *ẋ*^μ. For a free particle — by hypothesis, no external fields couple to the particle — the term *B*_μ(*x*) *ẋ*^μ must either vanish or reduce to a boundary term. The reason is that a nonzero field strength *F*_μν = ∂_μ *B*_ν − ∂_ν *B*_μ would produce, under variation of the action, an equation of motion containing the term *F*_μν *ẋ*^ν. This is the Lorentz force on a particle of unit charge in an external electromagnetic potential *B*_μ. The free-particle assumption therefore forces *F*_μν = 0, so the covector *B*_μ is closed. By the Poincaré lemma on the Minkowski manifold, a closed covector on a contractible domain is exact: *B*_μ = ∂_μ *φ* for some scalar function *φ*. The resulting contribution to *S* is ∫ ∂_μ *φ* *ẋ*^μ *dλ* = *φ*(end) − *φ*(start), a pure boundary term. Boundary terms do not affect the Euler–Lagrange equations and can be dropped from *S* without loss of generality. Therefore *F*(*x*, *ẋ*) = *A* √(−*η*_μν *ẋ*^μ *ẋ*^ν) with *A* a constant. (The *x*-independence of *A* follows from the translation-invariance part of condition (a): Poincaré invariance forbids *A* from depending on *x*^μ, since any *x*-dependent *A*(*x*) would distinguish some spacetime point as the origin of the function.) By condition (e) (dimensional consistency), *A* must have units of mass × velocity. Writing *A* = −*mc* (with the conventional sign chosen to make the Euler–Lagrange equations agree with the standard relativistic free-particle equations, and with *m* a mass parameter), we obtain *S*[*γ*] = −*mc* ∫ √(−*η*_μν *ẋ*^μ *ẋ*^ν) *dλ*, which is the claimed unique functional. The equivalent forms *S* = −*mc* ∫ |*dx*₄| and *S* = −*mc*² ∫ *dτ* follow from the interval *ds*² = *dx*₄² + *d***x**² of §II.9.1 and the definition of proper time *c*²*dτ*² = −*ds*². □

**The geodesic equation and the Newtonian limit [120, Part III].** Varying *S* = −*mc*² ∫ *dτ* with respect to the worldline *x*^μ(*τ*) with the endpoints held fixed, and using *c*²*dτ*² = −*η*_μν *dx*^μ *dx*^ν, gives *δS* = [−]*m* ∫ (*du*_μ/*dτ*) *δx*^μ *dτ*. Since *δx*^μ is arbitrary at interior points, *du*^μ/*dτ* = 0: the free worldline is a straight line of Minkowski spacetime. In coordinate time the Lagrangian is *L* = −*mc*² (1 − *v*²/*c*²)^(1/2) = −*mc*² + *mv*²/2 + ⋯, and with a potential *V*(**x**) the Euler–Lagrange equations give Newton’s second law.

**The Dirac matter sector: the McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* Reading of the Dirac Lagrangian.**

**McGucken Principle anchor.** The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* states that the fourth dimension is expanding at the velocity of light in a spherically symmetric manner from every spacetime event, as a McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action *h*. For a field *ψ* with the four components on which the matrices *γ*^μ of Theorem II.8.5.3 act, the Principle predicts the following. The Lagrangian whose field equation is linear and of first order in the four coordinates, which is real, invariant under Lorentz transformations and parity, unchanged by the choice of the origin of *x*₄ (Proposition II.9.3.2), and whose solutions carry the Compton frequency *ω*_C = *mc*²/ℏ of Theorem II.8.3.1, is *ψ*†*γ*⁰(iℏ*γ*^μ∂_μ − *mc*)*ψ*, and there is no other. This is Proposition V.1 of [77]. The lemma on the sixteen products is Theorem 4.2 of [132]. The proofs are given here in full. Throughout, ∂₀ = *c*⁻¹∂ₜ, *η* = diag(−1, 1, 1, 1), the matrices *γ*^μ are those of Theorem II.8.5.3(i), *ψ*† is the conjugate transpose of *ψ*, and a divergence is an expression ∂_μ*V*^μ.

**Lemma II.8.7.2 (The sixteen products of the matrices *γ*^μ; Theorem 4.2 of [132]).** For a subset *S* of the indices 0, 1, 2, 3 let *γ*_S be the product of the *γ*^μ with *μ* in *S*, taken in increasing order of *μ*, with the empty product equal to 1. (i) The sixteen matrices *γ*_S are a basis of the complex 4 × 4 matrices. (ii) For *μ* ≠ *ν*, *γ*^μ*γ*^ν *γ*_S = ± *γ*_S *γ*^μ*γ*^ν, with the sign + exactly when *S* contains both of *μ*, *ν* or neither. (iii) The matrix *γ*⁵ = i*γ*⁰*γ*¹*γ*²*γ*³ satisfies (*γ*⁵)² = 1 and (*γ*⁵)† = *γ*⁵; it anticommutes with each *γ*^μ and commutes with each product *γ*^μ*γ*^ν; and *γ*⁰(*γ*^μ)†*γ*⁰ = *γ*^μ for each *μ*.

*Proof.* In the matrices of Theorem II.8.5.3(i), *γ*⁰ is Hermitian with square 1 and each *γ*^j is anti-Hermitian with square −1, because the Pauli matrices are Hermitian. (iii) Moving one *γ*^μ through the product *γ*⁰*γ*¹*γ*²*γ*³ it passes three matrices with which it anticommutes and one, itself, with which it commutes, so it anticommutes with *γ*⁵; a product of two of them then commutes with *γ*⁵. Reversing the order of four mutually anticommuting factors takes six transpositions, so (*γ*⁰*γ*¹*γ*²*γ*³)² = (*γ*⁰)²(*γ*¹)²(*γ*²)²(*γ*³)² = −1 and (*γ*⁵)² = i²·(−1) = 1. The conjugate transpose of *γ*⁵ is −i(*γ*³)†(*γ*²)†(*γ*¹)†(*γ*⁰)† = i*γ*³*γ*²*γ*¹*γ*⁰ = i*γ*⁰*γ*¹*γ*²*γ*³. Finally *γ*⁰(*γ*⁰)†*γ*⁰ = *γ*⁰ and *γ*⁰(*γ*^j)†*γ*⁰ = −*γ*⁰*γ*^j*γ*⁰ = *γ*^j. (ii) A factor *γ*^ρ of *γ*_S with *ρ* different from *μ* and *ν* commutes with *γ*^μ*γ*^ν, since it anticommutes with each of the two. The factor *γ*^μ, if present, anticommutes with *γ*^μ*γ*^ν, since *γ*^μ*γ*^ν*γ*^μ = −*γ*^μ*γ*^μ*γ*^ν, and likewise *γ*^ν. The sign is therefore −1 raised to the number of the indices *μ*, *ν* that lie in *S*. (i) Each *γ*^μ is unitary, so each *γ*_S is unitary and the trace of (*γ*_S)†*γ*_S is 4. For *S* ≠ *S*′, the product (*γ*_S)†*γ*_S′ is, after the squares (*γ*^μ)² = ±1 are removed, ±*γ*_T with *T* the non-empty set of indices lying in exactly one of *S*, *S*′. For every non-empty *T* there is a *γ*^ν that anticommutes with *γ*_T: if *T* has one or three elements take *ν* outside *T*, and if *T* has two or four elements take *ν* in *T*; in both cases *γ*^ν passes an odd number of matrices with which it anticommutes. Then the trace of *γ*_T equals the trace of (*γ*^ν)⁻¹*γ*^ν*γ*_T = −(*γ*^ν)⁻¹*γ*_T*γ*^ν, which is minus the trace of *γ*_T; the trace is zero. So the trace of (*γ*_S)†*γ*_S′ is 4 for *S* = *S*′ and 0 otherwise. If a combination of the *γ*_S with coefficients *k*_S vanishes, multiplying by (*γ*_S′)† and taking the trace gives 4*k*_S′ = 0. The sixteen matrices are linearly independent in a space of dimension 16. □

**Lemma II.8.7.3 (The Lorentz scalars and the Lorentz vectors among the 4 × 4 matrices).** Put *J*^μν = ½*γ*^μ*γ*^ν for *μ* ≠ *ν*. (i) *J*^μν*γ*^ρ − *γ*^ρ*J*^μν = *η*^μρ*γ*^ν − *η*^νρ*γ*^μ, and *γ*⁰(*J*^μν)†*γ*⁰ = −*J*^μν. (ii) A matrix *M* commutes with every *J*^μν if and only if *M* = *a* + *d* *γ*⁵ with complex numbers *a*, *d*. (iii) Four matrices *A*⁰, *A*¹, *A*², *A*³ satisfy *J*^μν*A*^ρ − *A*^ρ*J*^μν = *η*^μρ*A*^ν − *η*^νρ*A*^μ for all *μ* ≠ *ν* and all *ρ* if and only if *A*^ρ = *γ*^ρ(*b* + *e* *γ*⁵) with complex numbers *b*, *e*.

*Proof.* (i) By the relation *γ*^μ*γ*^ν + *γ*^ν*γ*^μ = −2*η*^μν of Theorem II.8.5.3, *γ*^μ*γ*^ν*γ*^ρ − *γ*^ρ*γ*^μ*γ*^ν = *γ*^μ(*γ*^ν*γ*^ρ + *γ*^ρ*γ*^ν) − (*γ*^μ*γ*^ρ + *γ*^ρ*γ*^μ)*γ*^ν = −2*η*^νρ*γ*^μ + 2*η*^μρ*γ*^ν; halve it. By Lemma II.8.7.2(iii), *γ*⁰(*γ*^μ*γ*^ν)†*γ*⁰ = *γ*⁰(*γ*^ν)†*γ*⁰ *γ*⁰(*γ*^μ)†*γ*⁰ = *γ*^ν*γ*^μ = −*γ*^μ*γ*^ν for *μ* ≠ *ν*. (ii) Expand *M* in the basis of Lemma II.8.7.2(i) with coefficients *k*_S. By Lemma II.8.7.2(ii), *γ*^μ*γ*^ν *M* − *M* *γ*^μ*γ*^ν is twice the sum of the terms *k*_S *γ*^μ*γ*^ν*γ*_S over the sets *S* containing exactly one of *μ*, *ν*, and the matrices *γ*^μ*γ*^ν*γ*_S are, up to sign, distinct basis matrices. So *M* commutes with *γ*^μ*γ*^ν exactly when *k*_S = 0 for every *S* containing exactly one of *μ*, *ν*. A set that contains both or neither of every pair of indices is the empty set or the full set, and *γ*_S for the full set is −i*γ*⁵. Conversely 1 and *γ*⁵ commute with every *J*^μν by Lemma II.8.7.2(iii).

(iii) Let the *A*^ρ satisfy the relation. For *μ*, *ν* both different from *ρ* the right side is zero, so *A*^ρ commutes with *J*^μν. By the argument of (ii), the sets *S* occurring in *A*^ρ contain both or neither of every pair taken from the three indices other than *ρ*: they contain none of the three or all of the three. These are the empty set, the set {*ρ*}, the set of the three other indices, and the full set, and *γ*_S for the set of the three other indices is a multiple of *γ*^ρ*γ*⁵. Hence, with no sum over *ρ*, *A*^ρ = *a*_ρ + *b*_ρ *γ*^ρ + *e*_ρ *γ*^ρ*γ*⁵ + *d*_ρ *γ*⁵. Now take the relation with *ρ* = *μ*: its right side is *η*^μμ *A*^ν. On the left, *J*^μν commutes with 1 and *γ*⁵, and by (i) *J*^μν*γ*^μ − *γ*^μ*J*^μν = *η*^μμ*γ*^ν; multiplying on the right by *γ*⁵, which commutes with *J*^μν, gives *η*^μμ*γ*^ν*γ*⁵ for *γ*^μ*γ*⁵. The relation becomes *b*_μ *γ*^ν + *e*_μ *γ*^ν*γ*⁵ = *a*_ν + *b*_ν *γ*^ν + *e*_ν *γ*^ν*γ*⁵ + *d*_ν *γ*⁵, and the linear independence of Lemma II.8.7.2(i) gives *a*_ν = *d*_ν = 0, *b*_ν = *b*_μ and *e*_ν = *e*_μ, for every pair *μ* ≠ *ν*. Conversely *γ*^ρ(*b* + *e* *γ*⁵) satisfies the relation by (i), since *γ*⁵ commutes with *J*^μν. □

**Theorem II.8.7.4 (Uniqueness of the Dirac matter sector; Proposition V.1 of [77]).** Let *ψ* be a field with four complex components and let *L* be a Lagrangian density with the following properties. (a) Linearity, first order, and the origin of *x*₄: *L* is quadratic in the field, so that the field equation is linear; *L* is unchanged when *ψ* is replaced by exp(i*α*)*ψ* with *α* a real constant; *L* has no explicit dependence on the coordinates; and the field equation is of first order and contains the first derivatives. (b) Reality: the difference between *L* and its complex conjugate is a divergence. (c) Lorentz invariance: for every *μ* ≠ *ν* and real *s*, with *S* = exp(*s* *J*^μν) and Λ the matrix defined by *S*⁻¹*γ*^ρ*S* = Λ^ρ_σ *γ*^σ, the field *ψ*′(*x*) = *S* *ψ*(Λ⁻¹*x*) has *L*′(*x*) = *L*(Λ⁻¹*x*). (d) Parity: the field *ψ*′(*t*, **x**) = *γ*⁰*ψ*(*t*, −**x**) has *L*′(*t*, **x**) = *L*(*t*, −**x**). (e) Mass shell: every solution of the field equation satisfies the Klein–Gordon equation (□ − *μ*²)*ψ* = 0 of Theorem II.8.3.3, with *μ* = *mc*/ℏ = *ω*_C/*c*. Then, up to a divergence,

*L* = *b* *ψ*†*γ*⁰(iℏ*γ*^μ∂_μ − *σ* *mc*)*ψ*,

with *b* a non-zero real constant and *σ* = 1 or *σ* = −1. The substitution of *γ*⁵*ψ* for *ψ* carries the Lagrangian with *σ* = −1 to the Lagrangian with *σ* = 1. For *σ* = 1 the field equation is the Dirac equation (iℏ*γ*^μ∂_μ − *mc*)*ψ* = 0 of Theorem II.8.5.3(iii).

*Proof.* Step 1, the general form. A quadratic expression in the components of *ψ*, of *ψ*† and of their first derivatives that is unchanged by *ψ* → exp(i*α*)*ψ* contains one factor from *ψ*† and one from *ψ* in each term, because a term with two factors from *ψ* is multiplied by exp(2i*α*). It is therefore a sum of terms *ψ*†*Nψ*, *ψ*†*B*^μ∂_μ*ψ*, (∂_μ*ψ*†)*C*^μ*ψ* and (∂_μ*ψ*†)*D*^μν∂_ν*ψ* with matrices that are constant by the absence of explicit coordinates. A term with *D*^μν ≠ 0 gives second derivatives in the field equation, so *D*^μν = 0. Since (∂_μ*ψ*†)*C*^μ*ψ* = ∂_μ(*ψ*†*C*^μ*ψ*) − *ψ*†*C*^μ∂_μ*ψ*, the third kind reduces to the second up to a divergence. Writing the matrices as *γ*⁰ times others, which is possible because *γ*⁰ is invertible, *L* = *ψ*†*γ*⁰(iℏ*A*^μ∂_μ − *M*)*ψ* up to a divergence, with constant complex matrices *A*^μ and *M*.

Step 2, Lorentz invariance. By Lemma II.8.7.3(i), *γ*⁰*S*†*γ*⁰ = exp(*s* *γ*⁰(*J*^μν)†*γ*⁰) = *S*⁻¹, so *S*†*γ*⁰ = *γ*⁰*S*⁻¹. The derivative of *S*⁻¹*γ*^ρ*S* with respect to *s* is *S*⁻¹(*γ*^ρ*J*^μν − *J*^μν*γ*^ρ)*S* = −*S*⁻¹(*η*^μρ*γ*^ν − *η*^νρ*γ*^μ)*S*, a linear differential equation with constant coefficients within the span of the *γ*^σ, so *S*⁻¹*γ*^ρ*S* = Λ^ρ_σ *γ*^σ with Λ = exp(−*sK*) and *K*^ρ_σ = *η*^μρ δ^ν_σ − *η*^νρ δ^μ_σ. With the first index lowered, *K* is antisymmetric, so Λ preserves *η* and is a Lorentz transformation of §II.8.1. With *y* = Λ⁻¹*x*, the derivative of *ψ*(Λ⁻¹*x*) with respect to *x*^ρ is (Λ⁻¹)^σ_ρ (∂_σ*ψ*)(*y*), so *L*′(*x*) = *ψ*†(*y*)*γ*⁰(iℏ *S*⁻¹*A*^ρ*S* (Λ⁻¹)^σ_ρ ∂_σ − *S*⁻¹*MS*)*ψ*(*y*). The values of *ψ* and of ∂_σ*ψ* at a point are arbitrary, so *L*′(*x*) = *L*(*y*) for all fields holds exactly when *S*⁻¹*MS* = *M* and *S*⁻¹*A*^ρ*S* = Λ^ρ_σ *A*^σ. Differentiating at *s* = 0 gives *MJ*^μν = *J*^μν*M* and *J*^μν*A*^ρ − *A*^ρ*J*^μν = *K*^ρ_σ *A*^σ = *η*^μρ*A*^ν − *η*^νρ*A*^μ. By Lemma II.8.7.3, *M* = *a* + *d* *γ*⁵ and *A*^ρ = *γ*^ρ(*b* + *e* *γ*⁵).

Step 3, parity. For *ψ*′(*t*, **x**) = *γ*⁰*ψ*(*t*, −**x**), (*ψ*′)†*γ*⁰*Xψ*′ = *ψ*†*γ*⁰(*γ*⁰*Xγ*⁰)*ψ* for any matrix *X*, and a derivative ∂_j of *ψ*′ is −*γ*⁰(∂_j*ψ*) at the reflected point. Since *γ*⁵ anticommutes with *γ*⁰, *γ*⁰(*a* + *d* *γ*⁵)*γ*⁰ = *a* − *d* *γ*⁵, *γ*⁰*γ*⁰(*b* + *e* *γ*⁵)*γ*⁰ = *γ*⁰(*b* − *e* *γ*⁵), and *γ*⁰*γ*^j(*b* + *e* *γ*⁵)*γ*⁰ = −*γ*^j(*b* − *e* *γ*⁵), the last sign being cancelled by the sign of ∂_j. So *L*′(*t*, **x**) is the expression *L* with *e*, *d* replaced by −*e*, −*d*, at the point (*t*, −**x**). Property (d) holds for all fields exactly when *e* = 0 and *d* = 0. Hence *L* = *ψ*†*γ*⁰(iℏ *b* *γ*^μ∂_μ − *a*)*ψ*.

Step 4, reality. By Lemma II.8.7.2(iii), (*γ*^μ)†*γ*⁰ = *γ*⁰*γ*^μ. The complex conjugate of *ψ*†*γ*⁰ iℏ*b* *γ*^μ∂_μ*ψ* is −iℏ*b*∗ (∂_μ*ψ*†)*γ*⁰*γ*^μ*ψ* = iℏ*b*∗ *ψ*†*γ*⁰*γ*^μ∂_μ*ψ* − iℏ*b*∗ ∂_μ(*ψ*†*γ*⁰*γ*^μ*ψ*), and the complex conjugate of *a* *ψ*†*γ*⁰*ψ* is *a*∗ *ψ*†*γ*⁰*ψ*. So the conjugate of *L* minus *L* is iℏ(*b*∗ − *b*) *ψ*†*γ*⁰*γ*^μ∂_μ*ψ* − (*a*∗ − *a*) *ψ*†*γ*⁰*ψ* plus a divergence. The integral of a divergence over spacetime is zero for a field that vanishes outside a bounded region. Take *ψ* = *f* *u* with *f* a real function vanishing outside a bounded region and *u* a constant column with *u*†*γ*⁰*u* ≠ 0: the first term integrates to a multiple of the integral of *f* ∂_μ*f* = ∂_μ(*f*²/2), which is zero, and the second to (*a*∗ − *a*) *u*†*γ*⁰*u* times the integral of *f*², so *a*∗ = *a*. Take next *ψ* = *f* exp(−i*ωt*) *u*: the first term integrates to iℏ(*b*∗ − *b*) (−i*ω*/*c*) *u*†*u* times the integral of *f*², so *b*∗ = *b*. Both constants are real.

Step 5, the field equation and the mass shell. The variation of the action with respect to *ψ*† gives (iℏ*b* *γ*^μ∂_μ − *a*)*ψ* = 0, and property (a) requires *b* ≠ 0. Applying iℏ*b* *γ*^μ∂_μ + *a* and repeating the computation of Theorem II.8.5.3(ii), every solution satisfies (ℏ²*b*²□ − *a*²)*ψ* = 0. A non-zero solution exists: *ψ* = exp(−i*ωt*)*u* with *γ*⁰*u* = *u* and ℏ*bω*/*c* = *a*. By property (e) it also satisfies ℏ²*b*²(□ − *μ*²)*ψ* = 0; subtracting, (*a*² − ℏ²*b*²*μ*²)*ψ* = 0, so *a*² = *b*²*m*²*c*² and *a* = *σ* *b* *mc* with *σ* = ±1. This is the stated form. Step 6, the two signs. For *χ* = *γ*⁵*ψ*, *χ*†*γ*⁰ = *ψ*†*γ*⁵*γ*⁰ = −*ψ*†*γ*⁰*γ*⁵, so *χ*†*γ*⁰(iℏ*γ*^μ∂_μ + *mc*)*χ* = −*ψ*†*γ*⁰*γ*⁵(iℏ*γ*^μ∂_μ + *mc*)*γ*⁵*ψ* = −*ψ*†*γ*⁰(−iℏ*γ*^μ∂_μ + *mc*)*ψ* = *ψ*†*γ*⁰(iℏ*γ*^μ∂_μ − *mc*)*ψ*, because *γ*⁵*γ*^μ*γ*⁵ = −*γ*^μ and (*γ*⁵)² = 1. □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of Theorem II.8.7.4.** Each hypothesis is a property the Principle supplies. The first order of the field equation is the first order of *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* and of its McGucken Operator *D*_M (Theorem II.8.5.1). The invariance under exp(i*α*) is the absence of an origin of *x*₄ (Proposition II.9.3.2). The Lorentz transformations are those of §II.8.1, and *S* = exp(*s* *J*¹²) is the rotation *R*(*θ*) of Theorem II.8.3.7 with *θ* = *s*. The mass term is the Compton frequency: *mc*/ℏ = *ω*_C/*c*, the rate of the *x*₄-phase of Theorem II.8.3.1. The matrices *γ*^μ are those of Theorem II.8.5.3, and the sign of their relation is the one fixed in the remark that follows that theorem. The Lagrangian of Dirac’s equation of 1928 is therefore the Lagrangian of the first-order field carried by the expansion of *x*₄.

**The Yang–Mills gauge sector: the McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* Reading of the Yang–Mills Lagrangian.**

**McGucken Principle anchor.** The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* states that the fourth dimension is expanding at the velocity of light in a spherically symmetric manner from every spacetime event, as a McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action *h*. The Principle fixes no origin of *x*₄ (Proposition II.9.3.2). Theorem 123 of [133] states the requirement that follows when the origin may be chosen separately at each event: the physics is unchanged by *ψ* → exp(i*α*(*x*))*ψ* with *α* a function on spacetime. Theorem II.8.5.4 and §II.9.4 show that this requirement introduces the connection *A*_μ in the covariant derivative. For the field *A*_μ itself the Principle then predicts the Lagrangian −¼ *F*^a_μν *F*_a^μν, and Theorem II.8.7.8 proves that it is the only one under the stated conditions. This is Proposition VI.2 of [77]. The local invariance is a hypothesis of the theorem, named as hypothesis (a).

**Definition II.8.7.5 (Gauge group, connection, gauge transformation, field strength).** Let *G* be U(1) or SU(*N*) with *N* ≥ 2, a group of unitary *n* × *n* matrices. Its Lie algebra is the real vector space of the matrices *X* with exp(*sX*) in *G* for every real *s*; these matrices are anti-Hermitian, and the Lie algebra contains *XY* − *YX* and *gXg*⁻¹ whenever it contains *X*, *Y* and *g* is in *G*. On it ⟨*X*, *Y*⟩ = −tr(*XY*) is a real inner product, positive because −tr(*X*²) = tr(*X*†*X*), and ⟨*gXg*⁻¹, *gYg*⁻¹⟩ = ⟨*X*, *Y*⟩. Let *T*_a be a basis with ⟨*T*_a, *T*_b⟩ = δ_ab and *T*_b*T*_c − *T*_c*T*_b = *f*_bc^a *T*_a. A connection is a field *A*_μ(*x*) = *A*^a_μ(*x*) *T*_a with values in the Lie algebra, and the covariant derivative of a field *ψ* with *n* components is *D*_μ*ψ* = ∂_μ*ψ* + *A*_μ*ψ*; for U(1) this is the derivative of Theorem II.8.5.4 and §II.9.4. A gauge transformation by a smooth function *g*(*x*) with values in *G* replaces *ψ* by *gψ* and *A*_μ by *A*′_μ = *gA*_μ*g*⁻¹ − (∂_μ*g*)*g*⁻¹, which has values in the Lie algebra. The field strength is *F*_μν = ∂_μ*A*_ν − ∂_ν*A*_μ + *A*_μ*A*_ν − *A*_ν*A*_μ, with components *F*^a_μν = ∂_μ*A*^a_ν − ∂_ν*A*^a_μ + *f*_bc^a *A*^b_μ *A*^c_ν. The coupling constant of [77] is included in *A*_μ.

**Lemma II.8.7.6 (Covariance of the field strength, and reduction of an invariant Lagrangian to it).** (i) *D*′_μ(*gψ*) = *g* *D*_μ*ψ*, where *D*′_μ = ∂_μ + *A*′_μ. (ii) *F*_μν*ψ* = *D*_μ*D*_ν*ψ* − *D*_ν*D*_μ*ψ*. (iii) *F*′_μν = *gF*_μν*g*⁻¹. (iv) Let *L* be a function of the values at a point of *A*_μ and of the first derivatives ∂_μ*A*_ν, and let *L* have the same value on *A*′ as on *A* for every gauge transformation. Then *L* = Φ(*F*), where Φ(*X*) is the value of *L* at *A*_μ = 0 and ∂_μ*A*_ν = ½*X*_μν, and Φ(*gFg*⁻¹) = Φ(*F*) for every *g* in *G*.

*Proof.* (i) ∂_μ(*gψ*) + *A*′_μ*gψ* = (∂_μ*g*)*ψ* + *g*∂_μ*ψ* + *gA*_μ*ψ* − (∂_μ*g*)*ψ* = *g*(∂_μ*ψ* + *A*_μ*ψ*). (ii) *D*_μ*D*_ν*ψ* = ∂_μ∂_ν*ψ* + (∂_μ*A*_ν)*ψ* + *A*_ν∂_μ*ψ* + *A*_μ∂_ν*ψ* + *A*_μ*A*_ν*ψ*. The first term and the sum of the third and fourth are symmetric in *μ*, *ν*; subtracting the same expression with *μ*, *ν* exchanged leaves *F*_μν*ψ*. (iii) By (ii) and (i) applied twice, *F*′_μν*gψ* = *D*′_μ*D*′_ν(*gψ*) − *D*′_ν*D*′_μ(*gψ*) = *g*(*D*_μ*D*_ν*ψ* − *D*_ν*D*_μ*ψ*) = *gF*_μν*ψ*. The value of *ψ* at a point is arbitrary, so *F*′_μν*g* = *gF*_μν.

(iv) Fix a point *x*₀ and write *y* = *x* − *x*₀, *a*_μ = *A*_μ(*x*₀) and *b*_μν = ½(∂_μ*A*_ν + ∂_ν*A*_μ)(*x*₀), which lie in the Lie algebra. Put *α*(*x*) = *a*_μ *y*^μ + ½ *b*_μν *y*^μ*y*^ν and *g*(*x*) = exp(*α*(*x*)), a smooth function with values in *G*. Since *g* = 1 + *α* + ½*α*² plus terms of the third order in *y*, at *x*₀ we have *g* = 1, ∂_μ*g* = *a*_μ, ∂_μ∂_ν*g* = *b*_μν + ½(*a*_μ*a*_ν + *a*_ν*a*_μ), and ∂_ν(*g*⁻¹) = −*a*_ν. Then *A*′_μ(*x*₀) = *A*_μ(*x*₀) − *a*_μ = 0. Differentiating *A*′_μ = *gA*_μ*g*⁻¹ − (∂_μ*g*)*g*⁻¹ and evaluating at *x*₀, ∂_ν*A*′_μ = *a*_ν*A*_μ + ∂_ν*A*_μ − *A*_μ*a*_ν − ∂_ν∂_μ*g* + *a*_μ*a*_ν. With *a*_μ = *A*_μ this is ∂_ν*A*_μ − *b*_μν − ½(*A*_μ*A*_ν − *A*_ν*A*_μ) = ½(∂_ν*A*_μ − ∂_μ*A*_ν) − ½(*A*_μ*A*_ν − *A*_ν*A*_μ) = ½*F*_νμ(*x*₀). So at *x*₀ the transformed connection has *A*′_μ = 0 and ∂_μ*A*′_ν = ½*F*_μν, and by the invariance of *L* its value on *A* at *x*₀ is Φ(*F*(*x*₀)). Every antisymmetric array *X*_μν of elements of the Lie algebra is the field strength at *x*₀ of the connection *A*_ν = ½*X*_μν *y*^μ. For a constant *g*, *A*′_μ = *gA*_μ*g*⁻¹ and *F*′ = *gFg*⁻¹ by (iii), so Φ(*gFg*⁻¹) = Φ(*F*). □

**Lemma II.8.7.7 (The Lorentz-invariant forms of the field strength).** Put *E*^a_j = *F*^a_{0j} and *B*^a_j = ½ ε_jkl *F*^a_kl, and write *E*^a, *B*^a for the three-component vectors, with · the scalar product and × the vector product of such vectors. The position of the index *a* has no significance, since ⟨*T*_a, *T*_b⟩ = δ_ab. (i) *F*^a_μν *F*_b^μν = 2(*B*^a·*B*^b − *E*^a·*E*^b), and with ε^{0123} = 1, ε^{μνρσ} *F*^a_μν *F*^b_ρσ = 4(*E*^a·*B*^b + *E*^b·*B*^a). (ii) A rotation of the coordinates rotates every *E*^a and every *B*^a. The Lorentz transformation 1 + *sK*, with *K* the generator of the boost along the unit vector *n*, changes them to first order in *s* by *s* *n* × *B*^a and −*s* *n* × *E*^a. Parity changes *E*^a to −*E*^a and leaves *B*^a unchanged. (iii) Let *Q* be a real quadratic form in the components *E*^a_j, *B*^a_j that is invariant under the rotations and the boosts. Then *Q* = *P*_ab(*E*^a·*E*^b − *B*^a·*B*^b) + *R*_ab *E*^a·*B*^b with *P*_ab and *R*_ab real and symmetric. If *Q* is also invariant under parity, *R*_ab = 0. (iv) A linear form in the components that is invariant under the rotations is zero.

*Proof.* (i) With *η* = diag(−1, 1, 1, 1), *F*_μν*F*^μν = −2 *F*_{0j}*F*_{0j} + *F*_jk*F*_jk, and *F*_jk = ε_jkl *B*_l gives *F*^a_jk*F*^b_jk = 2 *B*^a·*B*^b. In ε^{μνρσ}*F*^a_μν*F*^b_ρσ the index 0 is carried by the first or the second factor; each case contributes 2·*F*_{0j} ε_jkl *F*_kl = 4 *E*·*B* of the corresponding pair. (ii) *F*_μν transforms as a covariant tensor. For a rotation the components *F*_{0j} form a vector and *F*_jk an antisymmetric tensor, whose dual *B* is a vector under rotations of determinant 1. For the boost along the first axis, *K* has the entries *K*^0_1 = *K*^1_0 = 1 and the change of *F* is −*s*(*K*ᵀ*F* + *FK*), which gives the changes *s*(0, −*B*₃, *B*₂) of *E* and *s*(0, *E*₃, −*E*₂) of *B*; these are *s* *n* × *B* and −*s* *n* × *E* for *n* = (1, 0, 0), and the other directions follow by a rotation. Under **x** → −**x**, *F*_{0j} changes sign and *F*_jk does not.

(iii) *Q* is a sum of terms *X*ᵀ*CY* with *X*, *Y* running over the vectors *E*^a, *B*^a and *C* a real 3 × 3 matrix for each pair, symmetric when *X* = *Y*. Invariance under every rotation ρ requires ρᵀ*C*ρ = *C*, that is, *C* commutes with ρ. Commuting with the rotation by π about each coordinate axis makes the off-diagonal entries of *C* zero, and commuting with the rotations by π/2 that exchange two axes makes the diagonal entries equal. So each *C* is a multiple of the identity and *Q* = *P*_ab *E*^a·*E*^b + *R*_ab *E*^a·*B*^b + *P*′_ab *B*^a·*B*^b with *P*, *P*′ symmetric. Under the boost, using *B*^a·(*n* × *E*^b) = −*E*^b·(*n* × *B*^a) and (*n* × *B*^a)·*B*^b = *n*·(*B*^a × *B*^b), the change of *Q* divided by *s* is 2(*P*_ab + *P*′_ab) *E*^a·(*n* × *B*^b) + *R*_ab *n*·(*B*^a × *B*^b + *E*^a × *E*^b). The cross products are antisymmetric in *a*, *b*. Setting every *B* to zero, the antisymmetric part of *R*_ab must vanish; then, choosing *E*^a and *B*^b along the first and second axes and *n* along the third, *P*_ab + *P*′_ab = 0. Under parity *E*^a·*B*^b changes sign and the other two products do not. (iv) A linear form is a sum of scalar products of fixed vectors with the *E*^a and *B*^a, and a vector fixed by every rotation is zero. □

**Theorem II.8.7.8 (Uniqueness of the Yang–Mills gauge sector; Proposition VI.2 of [77]).** Let *G* be U(1) or SU(*N*), and let *L* be a real function of the values at a point of the connection *A*_μ and of its first derivatives, with the following properties. (a) Local gauge invariance: *L* has the same value on *A*′ as on *A* for every gauge transformation. (b) Lorentz invariance. (c) *L* contains no derivative of *A*_μ above the first. (d) *L* is a polynomial of degree at most 2 in the field strength; in units with ℏ = *c* = 1 the field strength has the dimension of the square of a mass, and this is the condition that *L* have dimension at most the fourth power of a mass. (e) Parity invariance. Then *L* = *L*₀ + *α* *F*^a_μν *F*_a^μν with real constants *L*₀ and *α*. For *α* = −¼ this is the Yang–Mills Lagrangian −¼ *F*^a_μν *F*_a^μν, which for U(1) is Maxwell’s Lagrangian ½(*E*·*E* − *B*·*B*). Its field equation is ∂_μ*F*^μν + *A*_μ*F*^μν − *F*^μν*A*_μ = 0.

*Proof.* By (a), (c) and Lemma II.8.7.6(iv), *L* = Φ(*F*) with Φ(*gFg*⁻¹) = Φ(*F*). By (d), Φ = Φ₀ + Φ₁ + Φ₂ with Φ_k homogeneous of degree *k* in the components *F*^a_μν, and since rotations, boosts, parity and *F* → *gFg*⁻¹ act linearly on the components, each Φ_k has the invariances of Φ. Φ₀ is a constant *L*₀. Φ₁ = 0 by Lemma II.8.7.7(iv). By Lemma II.8.7.7(iii) and (e), Φ₂ = *P*_ab(*E*^a·*E*^b − *B*^a·*B*^b), which by Lemma II.8.7.7(i) is −½ *P*_ab *F*^a_μν *F*_b^μν. It remains to determine the symmetric form *P*(*X*, *Y*) = *P*_ab *x*^a *y*^b on the Lie algebra, where *X* = *x*^a*T*_a and *Y* = *y*^a*T*_a. For a field strength whose only non-zero component is *F*_{01} = *X*, Φ₂ = *P*(*X*, *X*), so *P*(*gXg*⁻¹, *gXg*⁻¹) = *P*(*X*, *X*) for every *g* in *G*, and by the identity 4*P*(*X*, *Y*) = *P*(*X* + *Y*, *X* + *Y*) − *P*(*X* − *Y*, *X* − *Y*) the same holds for *P*(*X*, *Y*).
